Intrinsic mirror symmetry and categorical crepant resolutions Thanks: D. P. was supported by EPSRC, University of Cambridge, and UMass Boston during the development of this project.
Daniel Pomerleano

Abstract
. The main result of the present paper concerns finiteness properties of Floer theoretic invariants on affine log Calabi-Yau varieties X X . Namely, we show that: (1) the degree zero symplectic cohomology S ​ H 0 ​ ( X ) SH^{0}(X) is finitely generated and is a filtered deformation of a certain algebra defined combinatorially in terms of a compactifying divisor 𝐃 \mathbf{D} . (2) For any Lagrangian branes L 0 , L 1 L_{0},L_{1} , the wrapped Floer groups W ​ F ∗ ​ ( L 0 , L 1 ) WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1}) are finitely generated modules over S ​ H 0 ​ ( X ) . SH^{0}(X). We then describe applications of this result to mirror symmetry, the first of which is an “automatic generation” criterion for the wrapped Fukaya category 𝒲 ⁡ ( X ) \mathcal{W}(X) . We also show that, in the case where X X is maximally degenerate and admits a “homological section”, 𝒲 ⁡ ( X ) \mathcal{W}(X) gives a categorical crepant resolution of the potentially singular variety Spec ⁡ ( S ​ H 0 ​ ( X ) ) \operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X)) . This provides a link between the intrinsic mirror symmetry program of Gross and Siebert and the categorical birational geometry program initiated by Bondal-Orlov and Kuznetsov.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Finiteness

A positive pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
consists of a smooth, complex projective variety
M
and a strict normal crossings anti-canonical divisor
\mathbf{D}:=D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{i}\cup\cdots\cup D_{k}
supporting an ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}
. For positive pairs, the complement
X
is an affine variety and can be equipped with a symplectic structure by taking a Kähler form
\omega
associated to (a positive Hermitian metric
||\cdot||
on)
\mathcal{L}
and restricting this form to
X
. This symplectic structure is exact and convex at infinity and, furthermore, independent of the choice of compactification up to a suitable notion of deformation. In view of this, one can attach a number of Floer theoretic invariants to
X
. The most classical of these is symplectic cohomology,
SH^{*}(X)
, which is a Hamiltonian Floer cohomology for exact, convex symplectic manifolds
X
developed by Viterbo
[
89
]
(building on pioneering work of Cieliebak-Floer-Hofer
[
13
]
). As with ordinary Hamiltonian Floer cohomology, it carries a pair-of-pants product which makes it into a unital ring. In
[
4
]
, Abouzaid and Seidel introduced wrapped Floer cohomology, which is a Lagrangian intersection version of Viterbo’s construction for pairs of (suitably decorated) exact Lagrangian submanifolds which are cylindrical at infinity. For any two such Lagrangians
L_{0},L_{1}\subset X
, the wrapped Floer groups
WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
are naturally modules over
SH^{*}(X)
.

This paper concerns the study of these invariants in the case that
\mathbf{D}
is anticanonical; we refer to such pairs
(M,\mathbf{D})
as positive Calabi-Yau pairs and to the complements
X:=M\setminus\mathbf{D}
as affine log Calabi-Yau varieties. We note that in the log Calabi-Yau case,
SH^{*}(X)
is canonically
\mathbb{Z}
-graded (because there is a preferred trivialization of
\mathcal{K}_{X}
). Wrapped Floer invariants on affine log Calabi-Yau varieties have recently attracted a great deal of attention because of their importance in mirror symmetry (see e.g.
[
6
,
34
,
66
,
39
]
and references there-in). This is the prediction that (at least in nice cases) there exists a mirror algebraic variety
X^{\vee}
so that symplectic invariants on
X
can be described in terms of algebro-geometric invariants on
X^{\vee}
. Under the mirror dictionary, the symplectic cohomology is expected to correspond to the polyvector field cohomology
HT^{*}(X^{\vee}):=H^{*}(X^{\vee},\Lambda^{*}TX^{\vee})
, whose degree zero piece is nothing but
\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{X^{\vee}})
, the ring of global functions on
X^{\vee}
. Furthermore, for each Lagrangian
L
, there should exist a corresponding complex of coherent sheaves
E_{L}\in D^{b}Coh(X^{\vee})
so that for any pair of Lagrangians
L_{0},L_{1}
,
WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
is canonically isomorphic to
\operatorname{RHom}_{X^{\vee}}^{*}(E_{L_{0}},E_{L_{1}})
.

The mirror partner to a given
X
is in general not another affine variety, but one expects that it is at least
semi-affine
, meaning it admits a proper map to an affine scheme. Semi-affineness imposes the following finiteness conditions on
HT^{*}(X^{\vee})
and
\operatorname{RHom}_{X^{\vee}}^{*}
:

(a)

the ring of global functions
\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{X^{\vee}})
is finitely generated over the base field
[
33
]
.

(b)

polyvector field cohomology
HT^{*}(X^{\vee})
is a finitely generated module over
\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{X^{\vee}}).

(c)

For any
E_{0},E_{1}\in D^{b}Coh(X^{\vee})
,
\operatorname{RHom}_{X^{\vee}}^{*}(E_{0},E_{1})
is a finitely generated module over
\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{X^{\vee}}).

Our main result is a direct mirror “translation” of the above finiteness statements to Floer cohomology on
X
:

Theorem 1.1
.

For any affine log Calabi-Yau variety and any field
{\mathbf{k}}
:

(a)

SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
is a finitely generated
{\mathbf{k}}
-algebra.

(b)

SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
is a finitely generated module over
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
.

(c)

For any
L_{0},L_{1},

WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
is a finitely generated module over
SH^{0}(X).

The actual proof of Theorem
1.1
, whose main ideas we now outline, is largely independent of these mirror symmetry heuristics. Part
(a)
of Theorem
1.1
follows immediately from a more precise result, Theorem
1.2
, which requires a bit of additional notation to state.

For a vector
\mathbf{v}=(v_{i})
in
(\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0})^{k}
, we define the support of
\mathbf{v}
,
|\mathbf{v}|
, to be the set of
i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
such that
v_{i}\neq 0.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}
be the free
{\mathbf{k}}
-module given by:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{{\mathbf{k}}}:=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}}H^{0}(D_{|\mathbf{v}|},{\mathbf{k}})

We can equip this vector space with a ring structure which, in intuitive terms, records how the different strata of
\mathbf{D}
intersect. To do this, represent homogeneous elements of
\mathcal{A}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
by
\alpha_{\mathbf{v}}
with
\alpha\in H^{0}(D_{|\mathbf{v}|},{\mathbf{k}})
. For any pair
\alpha\in H^{0}(D_{|\mathbf{v}_{1}|},{\mathbf{k}})
,
\beta\in H^{0}(D_{|\mathbf{v}_{2}|},{\mathbf{k}})
define

(1.2)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\ast_{\operatorname{SR}}\beta_{\mathbf{v}_{2}}=(i_{\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{1}}^{*}(\alpha)\cup i_{\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2},v_{2}}^{*}(\beta))_{\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2}}

where
i_{\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{1}}:D_{|\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2}|}\hookrightarrow D_{|\mathbf{v}_{1}|}
,
i_{\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{2}}:D_{|\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2}|}\hookrightarrow D_{|\mathbf{v}_{2}|}
denote the natural inclusion maps. Extending (
1.2
) linearly defines a commutative algebra structure on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}
which depends only on the dual intersection complex of
\mathbf{D}
,
\Delta(\mathbf{D}).
We will denote the ring
(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}},\ast_{\operatorname{SR}})
by
\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D})).
1
1

1

the notation comes from the fact that if
\Delta(\mathbf{D})
is a simplicial complex(as opposed to a
\Delta
-complex),
\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D}))
agrees with the Stanley-Reisner ring of
\Delta(\mathbf{D})
as studied in combinatorial commutative algebra.
Lastly, we let
B(M,\mathbf{D})\subseteq(\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0})^{k}
to be the set of vectors
\mathbf{v}
such that
D_{|\mathbf{v}|}\neq\emptyset
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(M,\mathbf{D})
be a positive Calabi-Yau pair (equipped with a polarizing line bundle
\mathcal{L}
). There is a canonical isomorphism of rings

(1.3)

\displaystyle gr_{F_{w}}SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})\cong\mathcal{SR}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D}))

Moreover, when
\operatorname{char}({\mathbf{k}})=0
, the module
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
has a canonically defined basis of elements
\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}
with
\mathbf{v}\in B(M,\mathbf{D})
and
c
is a connected component of
D_{|\mathbf{v}|}
(and is thus isomorphic to
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}
as an
{\mathbf{k}}
-module with fixed basis).

We also prove a version of this result (Theorem
4.25
) for an enhanced version of symplectic cohomology,
SH^{*}(X,\underline{\Lambda})
, which is linear over a certain Novikov ring
\Lambda
. Theorem
1.2
builds on
[
26
,
27
]
and specifically improves on those papers in two different respects.

•

First, the theorem holds for arbitrary Calabi-Yau pairs (in fact a version of it also holds in the somewhat more general context of “log nef pairs”; see Definition
3.1
) unlike
[
27
, Theorems 5.31, 5.37]
which establish an isomorphism of rings of the form (
1.3
) when
M
is Fano or when
\operatorname{dim}(M)=2
.
2
2

2

Theorem
[
27
, Theorem 5.37]
was previously proven in a completely different way by Pascaleff
[
66
, Theorem 1.2]

•

Second, in characteristic zero, it constructs a specific basis of elements,
\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}
, for symplectic cohomology which is conjecturally related to similar bases which appear in Gross-Siebert’s intrinsic mirror symmetry programme (
[
37
]
). We discuss the connections between our work and their programme a bit more in §
1.3
.

To explain the new ingredient in the proof of Theorem
1.2
, recall from
[
27
]
that for any positive
(M,\mathbf{D})
, the filtration
F_{w}
gives rise to a multiplicative spectral sequence converging to the symplectic cohomology ring:

(1.4)

\displaystyle E_{r}^{p,q}=>SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})

For Calabi-Yau pairs, (a special case) of
[
27
, Theorem 1.1]
provides a canonical identification of rings:

(1.5)

\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}:\mathcal{SR}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}\bigoplus_{p}E_{1}^{p,-p}.

The map (
1.5
) is given by counting certain low-energy moduli spaces of solutions (“log PSS solutions”)
u:\mathbb{C}P^{1}\setminus\{0\}\to M
which solve a suitable version of Floer’s equation and which intersect the divisor
\mathbf{D}
with a prescribed multiplicity at
\{\infty\}
.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
is given by constructing, when
\operatorname{char}({\mathbf{k}})=0
, a degree zero (additive) splitting of the spectral sequence

(1.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{PSS}_{log}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}),

i.e. a filtered isomorphism (with respect to a natural filtration
F_{w}
on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}
) whose associated graded map in a suitable sense is Equation (
1.5
). This map will be constructed by counting log PSS solutions of aribitrary energy, as opposed to just low energy solutions.

The main challenge in doing this is that whereas sphere bubbling is
a priori
excluded in the low energy moduli spaces, it can occur in the moduli spaces of arbitrary energy, which potentially interferes with having well-behaved compactifications. To overcome this, we use
refined
versions of Gromov compactness in the relative setting. There are by now several different approaches to this in the literature
[
19
,
44
,
65
]
. We rely on the approach of
[
19
]
because it is phrased in elementary geometric terms and produces a smaller compactification than
[
44
]
, however any of these approaches would be suitable for proving Theorem
1.2
. To regularize these strata, we rely on the technique of stabilizing divisors
[
14
]
which has become widely used in the symplectic topology literature (see e.g.
[
12
]
). Having constructed the splitting (
1.6
) in characteristic zero, a simple algebraic argument shows that, in arbitrary characteristic, the spectral sequence (
1.4
) degenerates in degree zero thereby completing the proof of Theorem
1.2
. Part
(a)
of Theorem
1.1
follows from Theorem
1.2
because a filtered ring (with positive ascending filtration) is finitely generated iff its associated graded is finitely generated.

The proof of parts
(b)
and
(c)
are conceptually quite similar. In part
(b)
, we recall that in
[
27
, Theorem 1.1.]
we identified the full
E_{1}
page of (
1.4
) with a certain logarithmic cohomology group
H_{log}^{*}(M,\mathbf{D})
. This logarithmic cohomology has a certain “periodic” structure, containing many copies of the cohomology of torus bundles over various divisor strata indexed by multiplicities
\mathbf{v}
. Algebraically, this periodicity is captured by a finitely generated module structure over
\mathcal{SR}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}\bigoplus_{p}E_{1}^{p,-p}
. Because the spectral sequence collapses in degree zero, the remaining pages are modules over
\mathcal{SR}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D}))
as well. Because the
E_{1}
page is finitely generated as a module, the subsequent pages are finitely generated as well. For part
(c)
, using a result from
[
64
]
, we show that one can deform the two Lagrangians so that the Hamiltonian chords (for suitable choices of Hamiltonians) between them exhibit a similar periodic structure. There is again a spectral sequence for
WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
each of whose pages are modules over
\mathcal{SR}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D}))
. We again show that the
E_{1}
page is a finitely generated module over
\mathcal{SR}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D}))
(generated by “short chords”).

1.2.
Applications

We now turn to applications of the above results to wrapped Fukaya categories
\mathcal{W}(X)

[
4
]
of affine log Calabi-Yau varieties (all
A_{\infty}
categories in this section will be linear over the ground field
{\mathbf{k}}
). For any
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
, we will let
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{C})\subset\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{C})
denote the dg-category of perfect
A_{\infty}
modules.

A key structural fact about the dg-categories
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
is that they are smooth, Calabi-Yau dg-categories of dimension
n=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}X

[
30
]
. (This in turn relies on the difficult fact that
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
is split-generated by the Lagrangian “co-cores” of any Weinstein handlebody presentation
[
25
,
11
]
.) Combining Theorem
1.1
with these algebraic properties, we obtain the following “automatic generation” criterion for wrapped categories of log Calabi-Yau varieties:

Proposition 1.3
.

(Proposition
5.18
) Let
(M,\mathbf{D})
be a Calabi-Yau pair and let
L
be an object of
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
such that

\operatorname{Perf}(\operatorname{Hom}^{\bullet}(L,L))\cong\operatorname{Perf}(Y)

where
Y
is a smooth quasi-projective scheme over
{\mathbf{k}}
. Then
<L>=H^{0}(\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))).

The result is reminiscent of recent results
[
28
, Theorem 1]
and especially
[
67
, Theorem A]
in the case of compact Fukaya categories. However, it should be emphasized that the method of proof of Proposition
1.3
is quite different and consists of two algebraic observations (in addition to Theorem
1.1
). The first is that a smooth, Calabi-Yau dg-category with connected
HH^{0}
does not admit non-trivial semi-orthogonal decompositions—this is a variant of a standard argument for proper Calabi-Yau categories (
c.f.

[
28
, Theorem 36]
). The second observation (see Lemmas
5.16
and
A.2
) is that the module finiteness of wrapped Floer groups implies that any pre-triangulated subcategory of the form
\operatorname{Perf}(Y)
is automatically admissible, thereby generating a semi-orthogonal decomposition(which is necessarily trivial in view of the first observation).

As mentioned above, it is already known that
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
has a collection of generators. Nevertheless, we expect that this result will find applications in situations where there is a collection of potential generators which are not obviously related to these co-cores. As an illustration of this, suppose
L_{0}
is an object of
\mathcal{W}(X)
such that the “zeroeth order term” of the closed-open map

(1.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{(0)}:SH^{0}(X)\cong WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{0})

is an isomorphism. (In particular,
WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{0})=0
for
*\neq 0.
) We will refer to such an object as a “homological section.” The terminology comes from the fact that general expectations suggest that a section of a putative SYZ fibration on
X
will be a homological section. However, in examples, it is usually much easier to construct homological sections than Lagrangian fibrations— in dimension two
[
66
, Proposition 7.2]
gives a geometric criterion for a Lagrangian brane to be a homological section and we give a similar criterion in Proposition
5.23
which applies in all dimensions.

If
X
is equipped with a homological section, it follows that there is a fully faithful functor

(1.8)

\displaystyle\pi^{*}:\operatorname{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X)))\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))

which sends the structure sheaf to
L_{0}
. In particular, when a homological section exists and
SH^{0}(X)
is smooth, Proposition
1.3
immediately yields:

Corollary 1.4
.

Suppose
SH^{0}(X)
is smooth and
X
admits a homological section, then (
1.8
) is an equivalence of categories.

We illustrate this Corollary in the relatively straightforward case of one of the simple local models of Lagrangian fibrations with singularites. Consider specifically the conic bundle:

(1.9)

\displaystyle X=\{(z,u,v)\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n-1}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}|\;uv=1+z_{1}+\cdots+z_{n-1}\}

Proposition 1.5
.

(Proposition
5.30
) Let
{\mathbf{k}}
denote a field of characteristic zero and let
\mathcal{A}
be the ring

(1.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:=({\mathbf{k}}[u_{1},\cdots,u_{n},w_{1},w_{2}]/(\prod_{j}u_{j}=1+w_{1},w_{1}w_{2}=1)

We have an equivalence of categories

\operatorname{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{A}))\cong\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))

Proposition
1.5
also implies a mirror symmetry statement for finite (abelian) covers of
X
; Corollary
5.31
.

When
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
is singular, a result of the form of Corollary
1.4
cannot hold because, as discussed above,
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
is always a smooth dg-category. This leads to the idea that
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
could be a categorical resolution of
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
in the sense of Kuznetsov
[
55
, Definition 3.2 and Definition 3.4]
). For this to make sense, we need to assume that
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
has the same dimension as
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
(
=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}X
). To achieve this, we will assume that
(M,\mathbf{D})
is maximally degenerate, i.e. the divisor
\mathbf{D}
has a zero-dimensional stratum. In all of the subsequent results of this section, we will further assume that
\operatorname{char}({\mathbf{k}})=0
and that the strata
D_{I}
of
\mathbf{D}
are all connected. Theorem
1.2
implies that
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
has very mild singularities:

Proposition 1.6
.

Suppose
\operatorname{char}({\mathbf{k}})=0.
Let
(M,\mathbf{D})
be a maximally degenerate positive Calabi-Yau pair of dimension
n
such that all strata of
\mathbf{D}
are connected. Then
Y:=\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
is a reduced Gorenstein
n
-dimensional scheme of finite type. Moreover,
Y
is Calabi-Yau.

One can also show (see Proposition
5.32
) that
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
has other nice properties; for example it is Du Bois (a weakening of rationality introduced by Streenbrink in
[
83
]
which is important in the theory of moduli of varieties
[
52
, Chapter 6]
). In any event, using the fact that
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
is Calabi-Yau, the same methods from Corollary
1.4
show that
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
is a categorical resolution of
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X))
which is furthermore crepant in the sense of Definition
5.41
.
3
3

3

The definition of crepancy that we use in this paper is slightly different from the more standard definition
[
55
, Definition 3.5]
, see Remark
5.42
.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
(M,\mathbf{D})
be as in Corollary
1.6
and suppose that
X
admits a homological section
L_{0}
. Then the pair
(\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))^{\otimes_{SH}},\pi^{*})
define a categorical crepant resolution of
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
in the sense of Definition
5.41
.

In the above proposition,
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))^{\otimes_{SH}}
denotes a “strictified” model of
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
which is linear over
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
in the naive sense. In the affine log Calabi-Yau setting, such models always exist (see Corollary
5.46
) and are unique up to
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
-linear equivalence. One interesting corollary of Proposition
1.7
is the following refinement of Corollary
1.4
:

Corollary 1.8
.

Let
(M,\mathbf{D})
be as in Proposition
1.7
. For any affine subset
\operatorname{Spec}(B)
in the regular locus of
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X))
, there is an equivalence of dg-categories:

(1.11)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Perf}(B)\cong\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))^{\otimes_{SH}}\otimes_{SH^{0}(X)}B

A far reaching conjecture of Kuznetsov (Conjecture
5.43
) postulates that categorical crepant resolutions are unique up to equivalence. This conjecture together with Corollary
1.7
would imply that whenever a crepant resolution
Y
of
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X))
does exist, we have an equivalence

\operatorname{Perf}(Y)\cong\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))

While this conjecture seems currently out of reach, there has been partial progress in some cases. For example, using a well-known result of Van Den Bergh
[
88
]
, we can show that:

Corollary 1.9
.

Let
(M,\mathbf{D})
be as in Proposition
1.7
with
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M\leq 3
. Suppose that
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
is integral with terminal singularities and that
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
admits a tilting generator (Definition
5.50
). Then
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
admits a crepant resolution
Y
and there is a derived equivalence:

\operatorname{Perf}(Y)\cong\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))

We expect that Corollary
1.9
will provide an efficient method for establishing new cases of mirror symmetry. Nevertheless, from a conceptual point of view, it seems desirable to replace the assumption that
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
admits a tilting generator with the assumption that it admits a Bridgeland stability condition. This and other questions are discussed in §
5.4.1
.

1.3.
Intrinsic mirror symmetry

In the above discussion, mirror symmetry served as heuristic motivation for Theorem
1.1
. We now examine deeper connections between our work and the existing mirror symmetry literature. The approach to mirror symmetry that is closest to our work here is the algebro-geometric framework of intrinsic mirror symmetry
[
37
]
(building on
[
34
,
35
,
47
]
). Gross and Siebert have defined for any Calabi-Yau pair (not necessarily positive) a commutative ring which encodes the counts of certain rational curves in
M
with incidence conditions along
\mathbf{D}
. When
(M,\mathbf{D})
is maximally degenerate, Gross and Siebert propose that the spectrum of their ring should be viewed as a mirror partner to the pair
(M,\mathbf{D}).
This construction is
intrinsic
to the pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
without relying on any further degenerations (or choice of Lagrangian fibration).

For positive pairs, the Gross-Siebert ring is defined directly on the vector space
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}
(the obvious variant of (
1.1
) over the Novikov ring
\Lambda
), respects the filtration
F_{w}
and the associated graded gring is again
\mathcal{SR}_{\Lambda}(\Delta(\mathbf{D}))
. Theorem
1.2
therefore suggests that
SH^{0}(X)
and
(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda},\ast_{\operatorname{GS}})
are isomorphic as rings. Establishing such an isomorphism would be very interesting because
(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda},\ast_{\operatorname{GS}})
is relatively computable (and in many cases can be computed entirely combinatorially
[
59
]
). We will take up this question in
[
69
]
.

The focus of the present paper is rather different and we temporarily set aside this question by (re-)defining the intrinsic mirror as
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})).
Indeed, Theorem
1.2
is sufficient to guarantee that most of the general properties of
(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda},\ast_{\operatorname{GS}})
also hold for
SH^{0}(X).
On the other hand, the advantage of defining the intrinsic mirror in terms of symplectic cohomology is that there is a direct point of contact between
SH^{0}(X)
and
\mathcal{W}(X)
. For example, Corollary
1.4
, should allow one to establish HMS in many cases once the relationship between symplectic cohomology and the intrinsic mirror construction has been ironed out. In the general case when
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
has singularities, Conjecture
5.43
and Proposition
1.7
provide a potential path towards establishing Kontsevich’s HMS conjecture (though one that seems out of reach for the moment). As hinted at above, it seems likely that one should also incorporate Bridgeland stability conditions into this picture. On the other hand, it is also worth noting that starting in complex dimension 4, we expect that there are examples where no crepant resolution (even “stacky”) of
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
exists. If such examples do exist, this would be evidence that the framework of categorical resolutions may be relevant to understanding Kontsevich’s conjecture in higher dimensions.

1.4.
Outline of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. In §
2
, we review the necessary background on normal crossings compactifications, Hamiltonian Floer cohomology, and wrapped Floer cohomology. This material is mostly standard or contained in
[
27
]
. The heart of the paper is §
3
and §
4
. In §
3
, we adapt Tehrani’s compactness analysis to described the compactified stable log PSS moduli spaces. The main goal of §
4
is to prove Theorem
1.1
(and along the way Theorem
1.2
). Section
4.1
describes the passage from Hamiltonian Floer cohomology at a fixed slope to symplectic cohomology and reviews from the spectral sequence from
[
27
]
. In §
4.2
, we explain how to use stabilization to regularize the compactified PSS moduli spaces. In §
4.3
, we begin by proving Theorem
4.21
which suffices for proving parts
(a)
and
(b)
of Theorem
1.1
. We also explain how to upgrade everything to “
\Lambda
-twisted coefficients” in Theorem
4.25
. The proof of Part
(c)
of Theorem
1.1
follows in §
4.4
.

We turn to the applications of these Floer theoretic results in §
5
. Section
5.2
develops the necessary homological algebra language and proves our “automatic generation” result. In this section, we also prove Lemma
5.22
, which gives a geometric criterion for a Lagrangian to be a homological section, and discuss the extended example of a particular local model of singularities of Lagrangian fibrations. §
5.4
is where we turn to discussing categorical crepant resolutions as well as some interesting open questions which naturally arise from this work. Throughout the applications section, a number of algebraic results are deferred to Appendices
A
and
B
. These are a mix of standard results and more novel results whose proof would disrupt the flow of the paper. The paper concludes with Appendix
C
, which describes a particular intrinsic mirror family with no smooth fiber.

2.
Symplectic Preliminaries

2.1.
Regularizations and normal crossings geometry

As a preliminary step in our analysis, we will deform the convex symplectic structure on an affine variety
X
to one which admits nice “models” near a compactifying divisor
\mathbf{D}
and hence is suitable for studying symplectic cohomology (this technique for studying symplectic cohomology originates from
[
62
]
and is a key ingredient in
[
26
,
27
]
).

Describing the models precisely requires some notation and terminology. To begin, recall that a Hermitian line bundle
(L,\rho,\nabla)
over a smooth manifold
Z
consists of a complex line bundle
\pi:L\to Z
over
Z
, a Hermitian metric
\rho
, and
\nabla
a
\rho
-compatible connection. A Hermitian structure on a real-oriented rank-two line bundle
L
over
Z
is a pair
(\rho,\nabla)
so that
(L,\rho,\nabla)
is a Hermitian line bundle, where
L
is given the complex structure
\mathfrak{i}_{\rho}
determined by the Riemannian metric
Re(\rho)
. In a slight abuse of notation, given a Hermitian structure on some
L
we will also use
\rho(v):=\rho(v,v)
to refer to the norm-squared function on
L
. Given a Hermitian structure on
L
, we can associate a connection 1-form
\theta_{e}\in\Omega^{1}(L\setminus Z)
which vanishes on the horizontal tangent spaces and which restricts to the “angular one-form,”
d\varphi=d\operatorname{log}(\rho)\circ\mathfrak{i}_{\rho}
on the fibers of
\pi
.

Suppose now that
Z
is symplectic — equipped with a symplectic form
\omega_{Z}
. Let
L
be a real-oriented rank-two bundle over
Z
with a Hermitian structure
(\rho,\nabla).
We can associate a 2-form on
L\setminus Z
by the formula:

(2.1)

\displaystyle\omega_{(\rho,\nabla)}=\pi^{*}\omega_{Z}+\frac{1}{2}d(\rho\theta_{e})

This form extends over the zero section
Z
and is in fact symplectic in a neighborhood of
Z
. Similarly, given
a collection of Hermitian line bundles
\{L_{i}=(L_{i},\rho_{i},\nabla_{i})\}_{i\in I}
, we have connection 1-forms
\{\theta_{e,i}\}_{i\in I}
. Setting

\pi_{I,j}:\oplus_{i\in I}L_{i}\to L_{j}
to be the projection we can form

(2.2)

\displaystyle\omega_{(\rho_{i},\nabla_{i})}=\pi^{*}\omega_{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\pi_{I,i}^{*}d(\rho_{i}\theta_{e,i})

Let
(M,\omega)
denote a compact symplectic manifold and let
Z\subset M
be a symplectic submanifold. We will let
NZ
denote the normal bundle of
Z
inside of
M
. In the case where
Z
is a codimension two symplectic submanifold
Z\subset M
, we can equip
NZ
with a Hermitian structure
(\rho,\nabla)
. It follows from Weinstein’s tubular neighborhood theorem that there is an embedding of a relatively compact open set

\psi:U\subset NZ\to M
, such that for every
z\in Z
, the (normal
component of the) derivative
D\psi_{z}:NZ_{z}\to NZ_{z}
is the identity map and

(2.3)

\displaystyle\psi^{*}(\omega)=\omega_{(\rho,\nabla)}

where
\omega_{(\rho,\nabla)}
is defined as in Equation (
2.1
). We will refer to such an embedding
\psi:U\subset NZ\to M
as a (symplectic) regularization of the submanifold
Z
.

We say that a collection
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\in S}
of codimension two symplectic submanifolds is transverse if any subcollection
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of the submanifolds meet transversely. We will need to extend the notion of regularization to such transverse collections. The correct generalization has been given in
[
21
]
. As before, we may equip each normal bundle
NZ_{i}
with a Hermitian structure
(\rho_{i},\nabla_{i})
and consider Weinstein tubular neighborhoods
\psi_{i}:U_{i}\to M.
For every non-empty stratum
Z_{I}
, we also require that the overlaps
\cap_{i\in I}\psi_{i}(U_{i})
be covered by tubular neighborhoods of
Z_{I}
,
\psi_{I}:U_{I}\subset NZ_{I}\to M
such that

(2.4)

\displaystyle\psi^{*}(\omega)=\omega_{(\rho_{i},\nabla_{i})}

The maps
\psi_{I}:U_{I}\to M
are required to satisfy a number of natural compatibility conditions; as these compatibility conditions will play a limited role in the work we carry out in this paper, we will not spell them out in detail here but refer the reader to
[
21
, Definition 2.11, Definition 2.12]
. One thing that is worth noting is that if a transverse collection of divisors
\{Z_{i}\}
admits a regularization, then they must be symplectically orthogonal. Thus, in contrast to the case of a single divisor, regularizations do not exist for arbitary transverse collections. However,
[
21
, Theorem 2.13]
(see also
[
62
]
*Lemma 5.4, 5.15) shows that given a transverse collection
\{Z_{i}\}
in a compact symplectic manifold which intersect “positively” in the sense of
[
62
, Definition 5.1]
, we can deform our symplectic structure so that a regularization exists:

Theorem 2.1
.

[
21
, Theorem 2.13]

Given a positively intersecting transverse collection of symplectic divisors
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
in a compact symplectic manifold
(M,\omega_{0})
, there is a deformation of symplectic structures
\omega_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
such that:

•

[\omega_{t}]=[\omega_{0}]\in H^{2}(M)
,

•

the divisors
\{Z_{i}\}
are symplectic submanifolds for all
\omega_{t}
,

•

the deformation is supported in an arbitrarily small
neighborhood of the singular strata of the divisors
\{Z_{i}\}
,

and such that the transverse collection of symplectic divisors

\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
admits an
\omega_{1}
-regularization.

Throughout this paper, our transverse collections of submanifolds will all come from algebraic geometry:

Definition 2.2
.

A positive pair is a pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
with
M
a smooth, projective
n
-dimensional variety and
\mathbf{D}\subset M
a divisor satisfying

(2.5)

The divisor
\mathbf{D}
is normal crossings in the strict sense, e.g.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}:=D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{i}\cup\cdots\cup D_{k}\ \textrm{where $D_{i}$ are smooth components of $\mathbf{D}$; and}

(2.6)

There is an ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
M
together with a section
s\in H^{0}(\mathcal{L})
whose

\displaystyle\textrm{divisor of zeroes is $\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}D_{i}$ with $\kappa_{i}>0$}.

For the remainder of this section, we fix a positive pair
(M,\mathbf{D}=D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{k})
. We let
X
denote the affine complement
X:=M\setminus\mathbf{D}
. For any
I\subset\{1,\ldots,k\}
, we set

(2.7)

D_{I}:=\cap_{i\in I}D_{i}.

Turning to symplectic structures, equip
M
with a symplectic form
\omega_{\mathcal{L}}
which is Kähler for some positive Hermitian metric
||\cdot||
on
\mathcal{L}
. Consider the potential
h:X\to\mathbb{R}
defined by the formula

h=-\operatorname{log}||s||,

where
s
is the section given in (
2.6
). Over
X
, we have that
\omega_{\mathcal{L}}:=-dd^{c}h
and hence
\theta_{\mathcal{L}}=-d^{c}h
is a primitive for
\omega_{\mathcal{L}}
(i.e.
d\theta_{\mathcal{L}}=\omega_{\mathcal{L}}
).
The tuple
(X,\omega_{\mathcal{L}},\theta_{\mathcal{L}})
equips
X
with the structure of a finite-type convex symplectic manifold (see e.g.
[
62
, §A]
for the definition) which, up to deformation, is independent of the compactification or the choice of ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}
(
[
77
, §4]
).

We are now in a position to explain how we want to deform the finite type convex symplectic structure
(X,\omega_{\mathcal{L}},\theta_{\mathcal{L}})
. First, using Theorem
2.1
, we deform the symplectic form
\omega_{\mathcal{L}}
to a form
\omega
for which our divisors
\mathbf{D}
admit a regularization. We next choose a primitive
\theta
for
\omega
that has nice local models with respect to the regularization. In what follows, except when necessary we will drop the parameterizations
\psi_{i}
from our notation and identify the source
U_{i}
with its image in
M
. To state the next result, we let

(2.8)

\pi_{I}:U_{I}\rightarrow D_{I}

denote the natural projection from the tubular neigborhood to the divisor stratum.

Theorem 2.3
.

[
62
]
*Lemma 5.14
There exists a primitive
\theta
for the restriction of the regularized symplectic form
\omega
to
X
so that:

•

(X,\omega,\theta)
is a finite type convex symplectic manifold which is deformation equivalent to
(X,\omega_{\mathcal{L}},\theta_{\mathcal{L}})
.

•

After possibly shrinking the neighborhoods
U_{i}
, we have that on each
U_{I}
,
\theta
restricted to a fiber of
\pi_{I}
agrees with

\sum_{i\in I}(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{i}-\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi})d\varphi_{i},

There is some
\epsilon_{0}
, such that
\rho_{i}^{-1}[0,\epsilon_{0}^{2}]\subset U_{i}
for
all
i
. For any
\epsilon_{1}
such that
\epsilon_{1}^{2}\leq\operatorname{inf}_{i}\frac{2\pi\epsilon_{0}^{2}}{\kappa_{i}},
we set
U_{i,\epsilon_{1}}
to be the region where
\frac{\rho_{i}}{\kappa_{i}/2\pi}\leq\epsilon_{1}^{2}.
We then set
UD_{\epsilon_{1}}:=\cup_{i}U_{i,\epsilon_{1}}
and

(2.9)

\displaystyle\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}:=M\setminus UD_{\epsilon_{1}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}:=\partial(\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}).

Let
\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}
be two additional small parameters and let
\vec{\epsilon}
denote the tuple
\vec{\epsilon}=(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3}).
The space
\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}
is a manifold with corners and in
[
27
, §2]
, we considered particular roundings of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
,
\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}}
, depending on a choice of tuple,
\vec{\epsilon}
. When
\epsilon_{2}
is sufficiently close to
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{3}
is sufficiently close to 0, the compact submanifold of
X
bounded by
\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}}
is a Liouville domain
\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
whose boundary is
C^{0}
close to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}.
For any
x\in X\setminus\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
, let
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}(x)
be the
Liouville coordinate
defined as

(2.10)

R^{\vec{\epsilon}}(x)=e^{t},

where
t

is the time it takes to flow along the Liouville vector field
Z
from the hypersurface
\partial\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
to
x
. Flowing for some small negative time
t_{0}^{\vec{\epsilon}}
defines a collar neighborhood of
\partial\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}=\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}}
,

C(\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}})\subset\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}.

Thus, letting
X_{\vec{\epsilon}}^{o}
denote the complement of this collar in the domain
\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}

X_{\vec{\epsilon}}^{o}:=\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}\setminus C(\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}}),

R^{\vec{\epsilon}}
may be viewed as a function

R^{\vec{\epsilon}}:X\setminus X_{\vec{\epsilon}}^{o}\to\mathbb{R}.

In fact, it was shown in
[
26
, Lemma 3.15]
that
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}
extends smoothly across the divisors
\mathbf{D}
and hence can be viewed as a function on
M\setminus X_{\vec{\epsilon}}^{o}
:

(2.11)

R^{\vec{\epsilon}}:M\setminus X_{\vec{\epsilon}}^{o}\to{\mathbb{R}}.

This is in turn a consequence of the fact (
[
26
, Lemma 3.14]
) that in
U_{I}\setminus\cup_{j\notin I}U_{j}
, the function
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}
only depends on the variables
\rho_{i}
for
i\in I
i.e. over this region we have that

(2.12)

\displaystyle R^{\vec{\epsilon}}=R^{\vec{\epsilon}}(\rho_{i}).

(This latter fact depends crucially on
\theta
having the form described in Theorem
2.3
and the specific choice of Liouville domain.)

2.2.
Floer cohomology

Here we explain our setup for Floer cohomology. We begin by laying down some notation. Let
H:M\times S^{1}\to\mathbb{R}
be a time-dependent Hamiltonian whose Hamiltonian flow preserves
\mathbf{D}.
Time-one orbits of
X_{H}
of such a Hamiltonian either lie entirely in
X
or entirely in
\mathbf{D}
. We will refer to the orbits
contained in
\mathbf{D}
as divisorial orbits and denote them by
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{D};H)
and denote all other orbits by
\mathcal{X}(X;H)
. For every orbit
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H)
, we can define the action of
x_{0}
,
A_{H}(x_{0}),
by the formula:

(2.13)

\displaystyle A_{H}(x_{0})=-\int_{x_{0}}x_{0}^{*}(\theta)+\int_{0}^{1}H(t,x_{0}(t))dt

In order to obtain a well-behaved Floer theory on a noncompact space such as
X
, we need to restrict to a nice class of Hamiltonians.

Definition 2.4
.

Fix a real number
\lambda>0.
We say that a function
h^{\lambda}(R):{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is admissible of slope
\lambda
if the following conditions hold:

(1)

h^{\lambda}=-\hbar_{\operatorname{min}}
for
R\leq e^{-t^{\vec{\epsilon}}_{0}}
where
\hbar_{\operatorname{min}}
is a small non-negative constant (and
t^{\vec{\epsilon}}_{0}
is as before).

(2)

(h^{\lambda})^{\prime}\geq 0
;

(3)

(h^{\lambda})^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
; as well as

(4)

For some
K_{\vec{\epsilon}}
satisfying
\operatorname{min}_{\mathbf{D}}R^{\vec{\epsilon}}>K_{\vec{\epsilon}}>1,

(2.14)

\displaystyle h^{\lambda}(R)=\lambda(R-1)\quad\forall R\geq K_{\vec{\epsilon}}.

Note that because of condition (1), the composition
h^{\lambda}\circ R^{\vec{\epsilon}}
can be extended smoothly to a Hamiltonian on all of
M
, which we also call
h^{\lambda}
:

(2.15)

h^{\lambda}:M\to\mathbb{R}.

Fix the
K_{\vec{\epsilon}}
for which (
2.14
) as well as a second constant

\mu_{\vec{\epsilon}}\in(K_{\vec{\epsilon}},\operatorname{min}_{\mathbf{D}}R^{\vec{\epsilon}})
. This latter constant enables us to define open neighborhoods of
\mathbf{D}

V_{0,\vec{\epsilon}}=(R^{\vec{\epsilon}})^{-1}(\mu_{\vec{\epsilon}},\infty)
,

V_{\vec{\epsilon}}=(R^{\vec{\epsilon}})^{-1}(K_{\vec{\epsilon}},\infty).
For later use, we note that Equation (
2.14
) implies that along the slice
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}=K_{\vec{\epsilon}},

(2.16)

\displaystyle H^{\lambda}=\theta(X_{H})-\lambda

Equation (
2.15
) implies that the Hamiltonian flow of
h^{\lambda}
preserves
\mathbf{D}
. Hence, the time-one orbits of this flow are either completely contained in
\mathbf{D}
or completely
contained in
X
(in fact
X\setminus V_{\vec{\epsilon}}
). For our purposes, it will be sufficient to give the following brief description of
\mathcal{X}(X;h^{\lambda})
(see
[
27
, §2.2]
for a more detailed discussion). The orbits
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;h^{\lambda})
come in two types of families, the first being the set of constant orbits. This set of orbits (a submanifold with
boundary) is given by the complement
\mathcal{F}_{\emptyset}:=M\setminus\{R_{m}^{\ell}\geq R_{0}\}
, where
R_{0}
be the largest value of
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}

for which
h^{\lambda}(R^{\vec{\epsilon}})=-\hbar_{\operatorname{min}}
.
The second type of Hamiltonian orbit corresponds to (possibly multiply-covered) circles in the fiber where

(2.17)

\displaystyle X_{h^{\lambda}}=\sum_{i}-2\pi v_{i}\partial_{\phi_{i}}

for an integer vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
which is strictly
supported on
I
. We will assume that along these orbits, the second derivative of
h^{\lambda}
is strictly positive. These orbits come in connected families, denoted by

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
, which are manifolds with corners. We define the weighted winding number of (a connected family
of) orbits
x_{0}
to be
w(x_{0})=\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}v_{i}(x_{0})
.

§
4
describes a careful choice of
C^{2}
small time-dependent perturbation
H^{\lambda}:M\times S^{1}\to\mathbb{R}
which enables us to make all of the orbits (divisorial and otherwise) nondegenerate and has several other desirable properties. For the moment, the two most important properties of this perturbation is the following:

•

The perturbation is disjoint from
V_{\vec{\epsilon}}\setminus V_{0,\vec{\epsilon}}
and inside of
M\setminus V_{\vec{\epsilon}}
, it is supported in the disjoint union of small isolating neighborhoods
U_{\mathbf{v}}
of the orbit sets
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
.

•

The Hamiltonian flow of
H^{\lambda}
preserves each divisor
D_{i}
.

At this stage, we assume for simplicity that
M
has an anti-canonical divisor supported on
\mathbf{D}
,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\Omega_{M}\cong\mathcal{O}(\sum_{i}-a_{i}D_{i})

(This enables us to ensure that the Floer cohomology groups we define below may be
\mathbb{Z}
-graded.) For each Hamiltonian orbit
x\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
, the induced trivialization
\gamma
of
x^{*}(TX)
determines a 1-dimensional real vector space
\mathfrak{o}_{x}
, the
determinant line
associated to a local Cauchy-Riemann operator
D_{\gamma}
. The
{\mathbf{k}}
-normalization
of any vector space
W
, denoted
|W|_{\mathbf{k}}
is the free
{\mathbf{k}}
module generated by the set of orientations of
W
, modulo the relation that the sum of the orientations vanishes. When the coefficient field is understood, we will sometimes just drop the
{\mathbf{k}}
subscripts and just write
|W|
. In the case when
W=\mathfrak{o}_{x}
, the
{\mathbf{k}}
-normalization
|\mathfrak{o}_{x}|_{\mathbf{k}}
is known as the orientation line.

Define the Floer complex to be the vector space generated by these orientation lines:

(2.19)

\displaystyle CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda}):=\bigoplus_{x\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})}|\mathfrak{o}_{x}|_{\mathbf{k}}.

We grade the subspaces
|\mathfrak{o}_{x}|
by
\deg(x)
, the index of the local operator
D_{x}
associated to
x
(all of this defined with respect to the trivialization of the holomorphic volume form on
X
). This index is in turn equal to
n-CZ(x)
, where
CZ(x)
is the Conley-Zehnder index of

x

[
24
]
. The next step is to equip the Floer complex with a differential. To do this, we must specify the class of almost complex structures that we wish to work with.

Definition 2.5
.

Define
\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D})
to be the space of
\omega
-tamed almost complex structures
J
which preserve
\mathbf{D}.

Definition 2.6
.

For any choice of Liouville domain and shells
\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}},V_{\vec{\epsilon}},V_{0,\vec{\epsilon}}
,
define
\mathcal{J}(\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}},V)\subset\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D})
to be the space of

\omega
-compatible almost complex structures which are of contact type on
the closure of
V_{\vec{\epsilon}}\backslash V_{0,\vec{\epsilon}}
, meaning on this region

(2.20)

\theta\circ J=-dR^{\vec{\epsilon}}.

Finally, let
\mathcal{J}_{F}(\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}},V)
denote the space of
S^{1}
dependent complex structures,
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(S^{1};\mathcal{J}(\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}},V))
.

Fix a pair of orbits
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
and some
J_{F}\in\mathcal{J}_{F}(\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}},V).
A Floer trajectory is a solution to the PDE:

(2.21)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &u\colon\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\to X,\\
&\lim_{s\to-\infty}u(s,-)=x_{0}\\
&\lim_{s\to+\infty}u(s,-)=x_{1}\\
&\partial_{s}u+J_{F}(\partial_{t}u-X_{H^{\lambda}})=0.\end{aligned}\right.

We denote the space of solutions to (
2.21
) by
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0},x_{1})
. There is an induced
\mathbb{R}
-action on the moduli space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1})
given by translation in the
s
-direction. Whenever
\deg(x_{0})-\deg(x_{1})\geq 1
and
J_{F}
is generic, the quotient space

(2.22)

\mathcal{M}(x_{0},x_{1}):=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0},x_{1})/\mathbb{R}

is a manifold of dimension

\deg(x_{0})-\deg(x_{1})-1.

Whenever
\deg(x_{0})-\deg(x_{1})=1
(and
J_{F}
generic), Gromov-Floer compactness implies that the moduli space (
2.22
) is compact of dimension 0. Moreover,
the theory of “coherent orientations” associates, to every rigid element

u\in\mathcal{M}(x_{0},x_{1})
an isomorphism

\mu_{u}:|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{1}}|\cong|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{0}}|.

For any
z\in|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{1}}|
, we can define the differential applied to
z
as follows:

(2.23)

\partial_{CF}(z)=\sum_{x_{0}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}(x_{0},x_{1})}\mu_{u}(z)

where
x_{0}
ranges over all orbits with
\deg(x_{0})=\deg(x_{1})+1
. We define
HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})
to be the cohomology of the complex

(CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda}),\partial_{CF})
. To define symplectic cohomology, it remains to note that for
\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{1}
, there are continuation maps (see e.g.
[
77
, §3]
)

HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda_{1}})\to HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda_{2}}).

The symplectic cohomology is then defined to be the direct limit:

(2.24)

\displaystyle SH^{*}(X):=\varinjlim_{\lambda}HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})

For the moment, we return to the Hamiltonians of a fixed slope
\lambda
and explain the connection between the action filtration on
HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})
and the winding numbers
w(x)
. The key computation is the following:

Lemma 2.7
.

[
27
, Lemma 2.10]

Fix a slope
\lambda>0
. Suppose that
\epsilon_{1}
is sufficiently small and that
\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}}
is sufficiently
C^{0}
close to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
. Then by taking:

•

K_{\vec{\epsilon}}
is sufficiently close to 1,

•

and
t^{\vec{\epsilon}}_{0}
,
\hbar_{\operatorname{min}}
,
||H^{\lambda}-h^{\lambda}||_{C^{2}}
all sufficiently small

we can make the action (see (
2.13
)) of each orbit
x_{0}\in U_{\mathbf{v}}
arbitarily close to:

(2.25)

\displaystyle A_{H^{\lambda}}(x_{0})\approx-w(x_{0})(1-\epsilon_{1}^{2}/2)

Meanwhile, for any Floer trajectory
u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\to X
, we can define the topological energy of
u
,
E_{top}(u)
as

(2.26)

\displaystyle E_{top}(u)=\int u^{*}\omega-d(u^{*}H^{\lambda}dt)

It is elementary to see that
E_{top}\geq 0
and it follows from Stokes’ theorem that

(2.27)

\displaystyle E_{top}(u)=A_{H^{\lambda}}(x_{0})-A_{H^{\lambda}}(x_{1})

Thus, the action of Hamiltonian orbits induces a filtration on the Floer complex. Lemma
2.7
shows that in our situation the action filtration can be described in terms of the winding number
w(x)
. For any
w
, let
F_{w}CF_{\Lambda}^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})
denote the complex generated by (orientation lines associated to) orbits
x\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})_{\leq w}
with
w(x)\leq w
:

(2.28)

\displaystyle F_{w}CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda}):=\bigoplus_{x\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})_{\leq w}}|\mathfrak{o}_{x}|

Corollary 2.8
.

Fix a slope
\lambda>0
. Suppose that
\epsilon_{1}
is sufficiently small and that
\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}}
is sufficiently
C^{0}
close to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
. Then by taking
K_{\vec{\epsilon}}
is sufficiently close to 1 and
t^{\vec{\epsilon}}_{0}
,
||H^{\lambda}-h^{\lambda}||_{C^{2}}
sufficiently small, we can ensure that the Floer differential preserves the filtration by
w(x_{0})
.

Proof.

Under the above assumptions, Equation (
2.25
) implies that the weight filtration agrees with the action filtration on the Floer complex, up to reversing the sign. ∎

For the rest of this paper, we will always choose our
\epsilon_{1}
,
\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}}
, and
H^{\lambda}
so that this filtration exists. More care is needed to construct the filtration on the direct limit (
2.24
) because as
\lambda
, increases, this entails taking roundings which are “sharper” (meaning
C^{0}
closer to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
). We postpone discussion of this to §
4.1
.

We let
F_{w}HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})
denote the filtration on
HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})
induced from the cochain level filtration
F_{w}CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})
. We also define the low energy Floer cohomology of weight
w
,
HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})_{w}
, by the formula

(2.29)

\displaystyle HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})_{w}:=H^{*}(\frac{F_{w}CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})}{F_{w-1}CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})})

We consider the corresponding descending filtrations:
4
4

4

This is done so that our conventions for cohomological spectral sequences match
[
60
]
.

F^{p}CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda}):=F_{-p}CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda}).

The filtration
F^{p}
on the cochain complex gives rise to a spectral
sequence

(2.30)

\displaystyle\{E_{H^{\lambda},r}^{p,q},d_{r}\}\implies HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})

where the first page is by definition
identified with

(2.31)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{q}E_{H^{\lambda},1}^{p,q}:=HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})_{w=-p}

As is customary, we set
E_{H^{\lambda},1}=\bigoplus_{p,q}E_{H^{\lambda},1}^{p,q}.
We end this subsection with a lemma concerning Floer trajectories that will be crucial to ruling out undesirable Hamiltonian breaking along orbits in the divisor
\mathbf{D}
in our compactness arguments for PSS moduli spaces:

Lemma 2.9
.

Suppose
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
and
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{D};H^{\lambda})
. Then there is no broken Floer trajectory (in
M
) with input
x_{1}
and output
x_{0}
such that

(2.32)

\displaystyle E_{top}(u)-A_{H^{\lambda}}(x_{0})<\lambda

Proof.

This argument is contained in
[
26
, Lemma 4.14]
. We summarize it here for completeness. Given such a Floer trajectory
u:C:=\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\to M,
We let
\bar{C}
denote the piece of this curve which lies above
R=K_{\vec{\epsilon}}
and
\underline{C}
to be the piece of this curve which lies below this level set. Then we have that

(2.33)

\displaystyle E_{top}(\bar{C})=E_{top}(u)-E_{top}(\underline{C})

(2.34)

\displaystyle=E_{top}(u)-A_{H^{\lambda}}(x_{0})+\int_{\partial\bar{C}}u^{*}\theta-H^{\lambda}dt

where in the first line we have used the additivity of the topological energy and in the second line we have used the fact that by Stokes’ theorem, we have that
E_{top}(\underline{C})=A_{H^{\lambda}}(x_{0})-(\int_{\partial\bar{C}}u^{*}\theta-H^{\lambda}dt).
In view of our assumption (
2.32
), we have that

(2.35)

\displaystyle E_{top}(u)-A_{H^{\lambda}}(x_{0})+\int_{\partial\bar{C}}u^{*}\theta-H^{\lambda}dt<\lambda+\int_{\partial\bar{C}}u^{*}\theta-H^{\lambda}dt

(2.36)

\displaystyle=\lambda+\int_{\partial\bar{C}}u^{*}\theta-u^{*}\theta(X_{H^{\lambda}})dt+\int_{\partial\bar{C}}\lambda dt

(2.37)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\bar{C}}u^{*}\theta-u^{*}\theta(X_{H^{\lambda}})dt

where in the second line we have used Equation (
2.16
) and in the third line we have used that
\int_{\partial\bar{C}}\lambda dt=-\lambda
by Stokes’ theorem. The rest proceeds as in the proof of the integrated maximum principle
[
4
, Lemma 7.2]
to conclude that
E_{top}(\bar{C})\leq 0
, which is a contradiction (
c.f.
Equations (4.43)-(4.45) of
[
26
]
).
∎

2.3.
Review of wrapped Floer cohomology

We next review the pieces of wrapped Floer cohomology and open closed maps that we will need to prove Theorem
4.42
and Proposition
5.23
below. In particular, while some of our results are phrased in terms of the wrapped Fukaya category of
X
, all of the geometric arguments in our paper take place at the cohomological level and only make use of constructions described below. Let
L
be a properly embedded Lagrangian in
X
. We say that
L
is:

•

exact
if
\theta_{|L}=df_{L}
for some function
f_{L}\to\mathbb{R}.

•

cylindrical
(at infinity) if outside of a compact set in
X
,
L
is invariant under the Liouville flow, i.e.
Z
is tangent to
L
.

Let
L_{0},L_{1}
be two exact Lagrangians and let
H:[0,1]\times X\to\mathbb{R}
be a time dependent Hamiltonian. We let
\mathcal{X}(L_{0},L_{1};H)
denote the (time-one) chords of the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}
from
L_{0}
to
L_{1}.
Given a chord
x_{0}
, we define the action functional to be given by

(2.38)

\displaystyle A_{H}^{L}(x_{0}):=f_{L_{1}}(x_{0}(1))-f_{L_{0}}(x_{0}(0))-\int_{x_{0}}x_{0}^{*}(\theta)+\int_{0}^{1}H(t,x_{0}(t))dt

Remark 2.10
.

It is worth mentioning explicitly that if
L
is an exact, cylindrical Lagrangian, the function
f_{L}
above is locally constant at infinity. So, assuming
L
is connected at infinity, one can normalize it to be zero on this region.

In order to define the Lagrangian version of Floer cohomology over the integers (instead of a field
{\mathbf{k}}
of characteristic two), we will assume all of our Lagrangians are Spin and equip them with a choice of Spin structure. Gradings in wrapped Floer theory similarly require a choice of grading on each Lagrangian submanifold
L
(recall that because we have assumed a natural class of volume form (
2.18
)).

Definition 2.11
.

A Lagrangian brane is an exact, cylindrical Lagrangian equipped with a grading and Spin structure.

Let
\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
be one of the Liouville domains from §
2
. Given two Lagrangian branes
L_{0},L_{1}
, let
H^{\lambda}:[0,1]\times X\to\mathbb{R}
be a compactly supported (time-dependent) small perturbation
of an admissible Hamiltonian of slope
\lambda
such that all time-one Hamiltonian chords,
\mathcal{X}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{\lambda})
, from (the Lagrangian underlying)
L_{0}
to (the Lagrangian underlying)
L_{1}
are nondegenerate. The Floer differential is defined on the
{\mathbf{k}}
-module:

(2.39)

\displaystyle CF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{\lambda}):=\bigoplus_{x\in\mathcal{X}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{\lambda})}|\mathfrak{o}_{L_{0},L_{1},x}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}

where
\mathfrak{o}_{L_{0},L_{1},x}
is a certain one-dimensional real vector space associated to the chord
x
using index theory
[
78
]
. Parallel to (
2.21
), we let
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}(x_{0},x_{1})
denote the space of solutions:

(2.40)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &u\colon\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\to X,\\
&u(s,0)\in L_{0},\quad u(s,1)\in L_{1}\\
&\lim_{s\to-\infty}u(s,-)=x_{0}\\
&\lim_{s\to+\infty}u(s,-)=x_{1}\\
&\partial_{s}u+J_{F}(\partial_{t}u-X_{H^{\lambda}})=0.\end{aligned}\right.

and we let
\mathcal{R}(x_{0},x_{1})
denote the quotient by the
\mathbb{R}
-translation action. Because our Lagrangians are equipped with a grading, each chord
x
can be assigned a degree
\deg(x)
. If
x_{0},x_{1}
are two chords with
\deg(x_{0})>\deg(x_{1})
, we have that for generic
J_{t}
the moduli space
\mathcal{R}(x_{0},x_{1})
is a manifold of dimension
\deg(x_{0})-\deg(x_{1})-1
which has a Gromov compactification
\overline{\mathcal{R}}(x_{0},x_{1})
. In particular, when
\deg(x_{0})-\deg(x_{1})=1
and
J_{t}
is generic
\mathcal{R}(x_{0},x_{1})
consists of a finite number of rigid solutions. Moreover, any rigid solution gives rise to an isomorphism of orientation lines,
\mu_{u}:\mathfrak{o}_{L_{0},L_{1},x_{1}}\cong\mathfrak{o}_{L_{0},L_{1},x_{0}}.
(In an abuse of notation we use the same notation to denote the induced map on
{\mathbf{k}}
-normalizations.) For any
z\in|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{1}}|_{\mathbf{k}}
, we can therefore set:

(2.41)

\displaystyle\partial_{LF}(z)=\sum_{x_{0}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}(x_{0},x_{1})}\mu_{u}(z)

which gives a differential on the complex (
2.39
). We let
HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{\lambda})
be the cohomology of this complex. (In the case when
L_{0}=L_{1}
, we abbreviate this as
HF^{*}(L_{0};H^{\lambda}).
) As with Hamiltonian Floer cohomology, choices of interpolating Hamiltonians
H_{s,t}
and (generic) almost complex structures
J_{s,t}
give rise to continuation maps:

\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}:HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{\lambda_{1}})\to HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{\lambda_{2}})

whenever
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}.
We can define

(2.42)

\displaystyle WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1}):=\varinjlim_{\lambda}HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{\lambda}).

One can formulate versions of (
2.40
) over more general Riemann surfaces with boundary. As we will only use very special cases of this construction, we will only recall the features of this that will be important for us, referring the reader to
[
78
, Ch. 8]
for more details. Let
(\bar{\Sigma},\{\zeta_{i}\})
be a pair consisting of a (temporarily compact) Riemann surface with boundary
\bar{\Sigma}
and boundary marked points
\{\zeta_{i}\}
which have been split into two groups (“positive” and “negative”)

\{\zeta_{i}\}:=\{\zeta^{+}\}\cup\{\zeta^{-}\}.

Let
\Sigma:=\bar{\Sigma}\setminus\cup_{i}\zeta_{i}
and equip
\Sigma
with Lagrangian labels, that is to say a collection of Lagrangian branes
\{L_{C}\}
indexed by boundary components
\partial\Sigma_{C}
of
\Sigma.
The choice of Lagrangian labels means that each boundary puncture
\zeta_{i}
has a pair of Lagrangian branes
(L_{0,\zeta_{i}},L_{1,\zeta_{i}})
associated to it by the two edges surrounding the puncture. (The convention is that if
\zeta_{i}
is a negative puncture,
L_{1,\zeta_{i}}
is assigned to the edge which comes before
\zeta_{i}
according to the boundary orientation, while the opposite holds at a negative puncture). We also assume that
\Sigma
is equipped with the following additional choices:

•

strip-like ends (
[
78
, §8.d]
) along the boundary punctures (positive or negative according to the partition of the boundary points).

•

Floer data
(H_{t,i},J_{t,i})
at each boundary marked point (
[
78
, §8.e]
).

•

Perturbation data
(K,J_{\Sigma})
compatible with the previous choice of Floer data (
[
78
, §8.e]
).

A Floer solution is then a map
u:\Sigma\to X
such that

(2.43)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &u(\partial\Sigma_{C})\subset L_{C},\\
&(du-X_{K})^{0,1}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

and so that
u
is asymptotic to some
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}(L_{0,\zeta_{i}},L_{1,\zeta_{i}};H_{t,i})
at each boundary puncture
\zeta_{i}
.

A very special case of this construction allows one to construct a
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear graded category,
H^{*}(\mathcal{W}(X))
, whose objects are Lagrangian branes. (In the literature this is sometimes referred to as the Donaldson category.) The morphisms between two Lagrangian branes
L_{0},L_{1}
in this category are the above wrapped Floer groups i.e.

\operatorname{Hom}_{H^{*}(\mathcal{W}(X))}(L_{0},L_{1}):=WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1}).

The composition in this category is given by the “triangle product” which we summarize as follows: Consider a disc with three boundary punctures
(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
ordered counter-clockwise. Equip
\zeta_{0}
with a negative strip like end and
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}
with positive strip like ends. Consider a closed one form
\beta
which restricts to
dt
along the positive strip-like ends,
2dt
along the negative strip-like end, and such that
\beta_{|\partial S}=0.
Taking
K
to be a suitable perturbation of the perturbation one form
h^{\lambda}\otimes\beta
, we can define a map

HF^{*}(L_{1},L_{2};H^{\lambda})\otimes HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{\lambda})\to HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{2};H^{2\lambda})

by counting rigid solutions (with respect to a generic surface dependent almost complex structure) of (
2.43
) which are

•

asymptotic along the strip-like ends to the appropriate Hamiltonian chords.

•

map the component of the boundary between
\zeta_{1}
and
\zeta_{2}
to
L_{1}
, the component between
\zeta_{2}
and
\zeta_{0}
to
L_{2}
and the component between
\zeta_{0}
and
\zeta_{1}
to
L_{0}
.

This operation is easily seen to pass to direct limits and defines the composition in the category.

The last piece of structure we will need is a map connecting the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian flavours of Floer cohomology:

(2.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{(0)}:HF^{*}(X\subset M;H_{F}^{\lambda})\to HF^{*}(L;H_{L}^{\lambda})

(
H_{F}^{\lambda}
and
H_{L}^{\lambda}
are used to distinguish the
a priori
different perturbations used to define the two Floer theories.) To define these, consider the “chimney domain,” a disc with one boundary puncture (equipped with a negative strip like end) and one interior puncture (equipped with a positive cylindrical end). Over the chimney domain, we consider a closed one-form
\beta
which vanishes along the boundary and which restricts to
dt
along the (cylindrical and strip-like) ends and let
K
be a suitable perturbation of
h^{\lambda}\otimes\beta
. As before, (
2.44
) is defined by counting solutions to (
2.43
) which mapping the boundary to
L
and are asymptotic to prescribed chords (see
[
70
, §6.14]
for more details). It is perhaps also worth noting that (
2.44
) fits into a more general TQFT framework where studies (
2.43
) over Riemann surfaces with both interior punctures and boundary.

The maps (
2.44
) are easily seen to be compatible with continuation maps, giving rise to a map:

(2.45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{(0)}:SH^{*}(X)\to WF^{*}(L)

It is not hard to check that this map is a ring homomorphism
[
70
, Theorem 6.17]
. Thus, composing (
2.45
) with the triangle product gives the wrapped Floer groups the structure of a (graded-) module over
SH^{*}(X)
.

3.
PSS moduli spaces

Let us first specify the type of pairs that we wish to work with:

Definition 3.1
.

We say that a pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
is
log nef
if there is an isomorphism:

(3.1)

\displaystyle K_{M}\cong\mathcal{O}(\sum_{i}-a_{i}D_{i}),

with all of the
a_{i}\leq 1
.

Definition 3.2
.

We let
B(M,\mathbf{D})\subseteq(\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0})^{k}
to be the set of vectors
\mathbf{v}
such that

•

D_{|\mathbf{v}|}\neq\emptyset
.

•

\sum(1-a_{i})v_{i}=0.

Note:
Throughout this section and §
4
, any vector
\mathbf{v}
will always be assumed to be in
B(M,\mathbf{D})
.

3.1.
Properties of relative maps

Before turning to our specific geometric context, we begin by recalling a few facts and constructions concerning
J
-holomorphic maps
u:(\Sigma,\mathfrak{j})\to M
which apply very generally. These concepts will be crucial to defining (stable) log PSS moduli spaces in §
3.2
and §
3.3
. Throughout this subsection, we let
J
be an almost complex structure in
\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D})
. Moreover, we will assume that
J
is “integrable in the normal directions to the divisors
D_{i}
” or more precisely that:

(1)

for any
i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
,
p\in D_{i}
, and tangent vectors
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in T_{p}M
, the Nijenhuis tensor
N_{J}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\in T_{p}D_{i}
.

We say that a
J
-holomorphic map
u:\Sigma\to M
has
depth

I_{u}\subset\{1,\cdots,k\}
if
u(\Sigma)\subset D_{I_{u}}
and
u(\Sigma)\not\subset D_{j}
for
j\notin I_{u}
. If
i\notin I_{u}
(i.e.
u(\Sigma)\not\subset D_{i}
), it is well-known that for each point
z_{0}\in\Sigma
with
u(z_{0})\in D_{i}
, there is a well-defined intersection multiplicity
\operatorname{ord}_{i}(z_{0})
with
D_{i}
(see e.g.
[
14
, Lemma 6.4]
or
[
43
, §3]
). We can extend
\operatorname{ord}_{i}
to other points of
\Sigma
by setting it equal to zero at points with
u(z_{0})\notin D_{i}
; observe that with this definition
\operatorname{ord}_{i}(z)
is non-vanishing at only finitely many points. For defining log moduli spaces, we will need a slight refinement of this idea which allows for taking into account holomorphic jets at these non-vanishing points. Namely, as before, we use the regularization to identify
U_{i}
with its image in
M
. Let
z_{0}
be a point with
\operatorname{ord}_{i}(z_{0})\neq 0
(in particular
u(z_{0})\in D_{i}
) and fix a trivialization of
ND_{i}
over a coordinate neighorhood
W\subset D_{i}
centered about the point
u(z_{0}):

(3.2)

\displaystyle ND_{i}|_{W}\cong W\times ND_{i,u(z_{0})}

We then have an expansion (
[
43
, Lemma 3.4]
,
[
22
, Eq. 6.1]
) over a sufficiently small coordinate chart
\Delta\subset\mathbb{C}
(centered about
z_{0}
and with coordinate
z
)

(3.3)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{C}}\circ u|_{\Delta}=\eta_{i,z_{0}}z^{\operatorname{ord}_{i}}+O(|z|^{\operatorname{ord}_{i}+1})

where
\eta_{i,z_{0}}\in ND_{i,u(z_{0})}
is non-vanishing. We say that
\eta_{i,z_{0}}
is the
\operatorname{ord}_{i}
-th
jet evaluation
at the point
z_{0}
. This depends on the coordinate chart
\Delta
(more precisely the choice of coordinate), however we suppress this from the notation.

One of the main ideas of relative Gromov-Witten theory is to enhance the moduli space of stable maps in such a way that the notion of intersection multiplicity (and jet evaluations) extends to the case where
u(\Sigma)\subset D_{i}.
The approach to this that we take here follows
[
19
]
, which has the advantage of being both efficient and phrased in terms of standard differential geometric objects (holomorphic line bundles, meromorphic sections, etc.) If
i\in I_{u}
, the operator
D_{u}\bar{\partial}
descends to a first-order differential operator

(3.4)

\displaystyle D_{u}^{ND_{i}}\bar{\partial}:\Gamma(\Sigma,u^{*}ND_{i})\to\Gamma(\Sigma,\Omega_{\Sigma,\mathfrak{j}}^{0,1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}u^{*}ND_{i})

It follows from the fact that
J
is integrable in the normal direction that this is a complex linear operator and hence gives
u^{*}ND_{i}
the structure of a holomorphic line bundle (
[
19
, Lemma 2.1]
).

As
u^{*}ND_{i}
is a holomorphic line bundle over
\Sigma
, we can consider the space of meromorphic sections
\Gamma_{\operatorname{mero}}(\Sigma,u^{*}(ND_{i}))
. Notice that the complex Lie group
\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts on
\Gamma_{\operatorname{mero}}(\Sigma,u^{*}(ND_{i}))
by rescaling. We will use the notation
[\zeta]\in\Gamma_{\operatorname{mero}}(\Sigma,u^{*}(ND_{i}))/\mathbb{C}^{*}
to denote the equivalence class of a meromorphic section
\zeta.
The following is the key definition:

Definition 3.3
.

A log curve
(\Sigma,u,[\zeta])
consists of Riemann surface
\Sigma
, a
J
-holomorphic map
u:\Sigma\to M
, together with a collection of non-constant equivalence classes of meromorphic sections

(3.5)

\displaystyle[\zeta]=([\zeta_{i}])_{i\in I_{u}}\in\prod_{i\in I_{u}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{mero}}(\Sigma,u^{*}(ND_{i}))/\mathbb{C}^{*}

Remark 3.4
.

We use the terminology “log” in order to be consistent with
[
19
]
. However, in algebraic geometry, log schemes form a category and log stable maps as considered in
[
36
]
are morphisms internal to this category. In this sense, the theory of exploded manifolds as developed by
[
65
]
is perhaps a closer analogue of log geometry in the differential geometric setting.

Assume that
(\Sigma,u,[\zeta])
is a log curve. Then we can construct a well-defined
contact order function
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{ord}_{u}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{Z}^{k}

\displaystyle z\to\{\operatorname{ord}_{u,i}(z)\}

•

For
i\in I_{u}
, we set
\operatorname{ord}_{u,i}(z)

to be the order of any zero or pole of any lift
\zeta_{i}\in\Gamma_{\operatorname{mero}}(\Sigma,u^{*}(ND_{i}))
at
z
. This function is
non-vanishing at at most finitely many points
z\in\Sigma
. Note that the function
\operatorname{ord}_{u,i}
is independent of the given lift
\zeta_{i}\in\Gamma_{\operatorname{mero}}(\Sigma,u^{*}(ND_{i}))
.

•

For
i\notin I_{u}
, we let
\operatorname{ord}_{u,i}=\operatorname{ord}_{i}(z)

to be the order of contact with
D_{i}
as defined previously(which is again
non-vanishing at at most finitely many points).

The notion of jet evaluation extends as well using the analogue of Equation (
3.3
) applied to lifts
\zeta_{i}
for each equivalence class
[\zeta_{i}]
involved in the definition of our log curve. In more detail, assume we are at a marked point with
\operatorname{ord}_{i}(z_{0})\neq 0
for some
i\in I_{u}.
Choose a lift
\zeta_{i}
for each equivalence class
[\zeta_{i}]
, and a small chart
\Delta
near
z_{0}
. Over
\Delta
, one may choose a holomorphic trivialization of
u^{*}ND_{i}:

(3.6)

\displaystyle u^{*}ND_{i}\cong\Delta\times ND_{i,u(z_{0})}

over
\Delta
. Then after possibly shrinking
\Delta
we have

(3.7)

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}|_{\Delta}=\eta_{i,z_{0}}z^{\operatorname{ord}_{i}}+O(|z|^{\operatorname{ord}_{i}+1})

for some
\eta_{i,z_{0}}\neq 0\in ND_{i,u(z_{0})}.
As before, the element
\eta_{i,z_{0}}
manifestly depends on both the choice of lifts
\zeta_{i}
as well as the given coordinate chart
\Delta
, however we again suppress this from the notation.

3.2.
(Log) PSS moduli spaces

In this section, we review the log PSS moduli spaces from
[
26
,
27
]
(see also
[
79
,
87
]
for related constructions in the case of a single smooth divisor) and we begin by describing their classical analogues from
[
68
]
. These
\operatorname{PSS}
-moduli spaces are certain Floer theoretic moduli spaces defined on the domain
S=\mathbb{C}P^{1}\setminus\{0\}
. We view this domain as having a distinguished marked point at
z_{0}=\infty
and equip it with a negative cylindrical end about
z=0
defined by

\displaystyle\varepsilon:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\to S

\displaystyle\varepsilon:(s,t)\to e^{2\pi(s+it)}.

We let
\rho(s)
be a monotone (non-increasing) cutoff function such that
\rho(s)=0
for
s>>0
and
\rho(s)=1
for
s<<0
and set
\beta=\rho(s)dt.

In the next definition, we let
J_{S}
be a surface dependent almost complex structure which is surface independent in a neighborhood of the point
z_{0}
and which agrees with some time-dependent almost complex structure
J_{t}
along the cylindrical ends.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
be a Hamiltonian orbit in
X
. Recall that a
\mathbf{\operatorname{PSS}}
solution asymptotic to
x_{0}
is a map
u:S\rightarrow M

satisfying the following variant of Floer’s equation:

(3.8)

(du-X_{H^{\lambda}}\otimes\beta)^{0,1}=0

(where
({0,1})
is taken with respect to
J_{S}
) such that

(3.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}u(\varepsilon(s,t))

\displaystyle=x_{0}

Let
\mathcal{M}(x_{0})
denote the moduli space of PSS solutions asymptotic to
x_{0}
.

Note that all of the orbits
x\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
are contractible when viewed as orbits in
M
and hence admit capping discs (in
M
). As is standard, we declare that two capping discs
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
are equivalent if

[u_{1}\#(-u_{2})]=0\in\hat{H}_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})

We let
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}(X;H^{\lambda})
denote the set of pairs
\tilde{x}=(x,[u_{x}])
, with
x\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
and
[u_{x}]
is an equivalence class of capping disc for
x.
We separate the curves according to their relative homology classes as follows:

Definition 3.6
.

For any capped orbit
\tilde{x}_{0}=(x_{0},[u_{x_{0}}]),
let
\mathcal{M}(\tilde{x}_{0})\subset\mathcal{M}(x_{0})
denote those log
\operatorname{PSS}
solutions
u
such that
-u
is equivalent to the capping disc
u_{x_{0}},

-[u]\simeq[u_{x_{0}}].

We naturally have

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(x_{0}):=\bigsqcup_{[u_{x_{0}}]}\mathcal{M}((x_{0},[u_{x_{0}}])).

For later use, it is convenient to record an alternative view on solutions to (
3.8
)
(this alternative point of view is often called the “Gromov trick”). Let
M_{S}:=S\times M
and let
\pi_{S}:M_{S}\to S
denote the projection to
S
. Equip
M_{S}
with the almost complex structure complex structure
J_{M_{S}}
defined by :

(3.11)

\begin{bmatrix}j_{S}&0\\
(X_{H^{\lambda}}\otimes\beta)\circ j_{S}-J_{M}\circ(X_{H^{\lambda}}\otimes\beta)&J_{M}\end{bmatrix}

Specifying a solution to (
3.8
) is then the same as specifying a
pseudo-holomorphic
map (considered up to domain reparameterization)

(3.12)

\displaystyle\tilde{u}:S\to S\times M

such that the projection
\pi_{S}\circ\tilde{u}
is a bi-holomorphism and
\pi_{S}\circ u(z_{0})=\{\infty\}
. For any
u\in\mathcal{M}(x_{0})
, we define

(3.13)

\displaystyle E_{top}(u)=\int_{S}u^{*}\omega-d(u^{*}H^{\lambda}\beta)

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose
H^{\lambda}\geq-\hbar
for some constant
\hbar>0.
Then for any PSS solution
u
,
E_{top}(u)\geq-\hbar.

Proof.

The geometric energy

E_{geo}(u)=\int_{S}u^{*}\omega-u^{*}(dH^{\lambda})\wedge\beta

is always non-negative. We have that

E_{top}(u)=E_{geo}(u)-\int_{S}H^{\lambda}d\beta.

By assumption,
\int_{S}H^{\lambda}d\beta\leq\hbar.
Therefore,

E_{top}(u)\geq E_{geo}(u)-\hbar\geq-\hbar.

∎

For the next construction, we choose a surface dependent
J_{S}
which preserves
\mathbf{D}
i.e.
J_{z}\in\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D})
for all
z\in S
.

Definition 3.8
.

A
log

\mathbf{\operatorname{PSS}}
(with multiplicity
\mathbf{v}
) solution is a
\operatorname{PSS}
solution such that

•

u
does not intersect
\mathbf{D}
anywhere except for at
z_{0}
.

•

The intersection multiplicity of u with
D_{i}
at
z_{0}
is
v_{i}
:

(3.14)

\displaystyle(u\cdot D_{i})=\mathbf{v}

We denote the moduli space of log PSS solutions with multiplicity
\mathbf{v}
and asymptote
x_{0}
by
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0}).
It follows from standard arguments (see e.g.
[
26
, Section 4]
) that

(3.15)

\displaystyle\operatorname{vdim}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0}))=\operatorname{deg}(x_{0})

Furthermore, for generic
J_{S}
with
J_{z}\in\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D})
for all
z\in S
, the moduli space is cut out transversely. Another application of Stokes’ theorem shows that for any
u\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
, the topological energy from (
3.13
) is given by

(3.16)

\displaystyle E_{top}(u)=w(\mathbf{v})+A_{H^{\lambda}}(x_{0})

We record the following lemma for later use:

Lemma 3.9
.

Fix a slope
\lambda>0
. Suppose that
\epsilon_{1}
is sufficiently small and that
\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}}
is sufficiently
C^{0}
close to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
. Then by taking
K_{\vec{\epsilon}}
is sufficiently close to 1 and
t^{\vec{\epsilon}}_{0}
,
||H^{\lambda}-h^{\lambda}||_{C^{2}}
sufficiently small, we have that for any
\mathbf{v}
and
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
, and
u\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
,
E_{top}(u)
can be made arbitrarily close to

(3.17)

\displaystyle E_{top}(u)\approx w(\mathbf{v})-w(x_{0})(1-\epsilon_{1}^{2}/2)

Proof.

This follows immediately by combining (
3.16
) and (
2.25
). ∎

It is sometimes useful to keep track of the relative homology class of the log PSS solution:

Definition 3.10
.

For any capped orbit
\tilde{x}_{0}=(x_{0},[u_{x_{0}}]),
let
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0})\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
denote those log
\operatorname{PSS}
solutions
u
such that
-u
is equivalent to the capping disc
u_{x_{0}},

-u\simeq u_{x_{0}}.

We have

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0}):=\bigsqcup_{[u_{x_{0}}]}\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},(x_{0},[u_{x_{0}}]))

where
\tilde{x}_{0}
ranges over all the (homology classes of) capping discs for
x_{0}
.

3.3.
Stable log PSS moduli spaces

The purpose of this subsection is to show that given a triple
\mathbf{v}
,
H^{\lambda}
,
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
, with
\lambda>w(\mathbf{v})
and
||H^{\lambda}-h^{\lambda}||_{C^{2}}
sufficiently small (and our complex structures suitably chosen), there is a well-behaved compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
(see Theorem
3.27
below). We first recall that the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(\tilde{x}_{0})
have appropriate Gromov compactifications
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{x}_{0})
which incorporate:

(1)

sphere bubbling

(2)

breaking along cylindrical ends.

The spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{x}_{0})
can be given a topology (we recall what we need about this topology in Definition
3.24
) which makes them into compact Hausdorff spaces. As in (
3.10
), we have:

(3.19)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0}):=\bigsqcup_{[u_{x_{0}}]}\overline{\mathcal{M}}((x_{0},[u_{x_{0}}])).

As is usual in pseudoholomorphic curve theory, the combinatorics of curves in our compactification are governed by graphs (in our case trees as we are working in genus zero) equipped with additional structure. We begin by specifying our notation/conventions about these trees:

Notation for trees:

(1)

The collection of vertices of a tree
\underline{\Gamma}
will be denoted by
\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma})
. Individual vertices will usually be denoted by
\nu
and if
\underline{\Gamma}
is a rooted tree, the root vertex will be denoted by
\nu_{root}
.

(2)

Internal or bounded edges of
\underline{\Gamma}
will be denoted by
\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma})
, and
\vec{\mathbb{E}}(\underline{\Gamma})
will denote the set of oriented internal edges. For a vertex
\nu
,
\mathbb{E}(\nu)
shall be the set of internal edges which neighbor
\nu
and
\vec{\mathbb{E}}(\nu)
shall denote the oriented edges. The oriented edge from
\nu
to
\nu^{\prime}
will be denoted by
e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
.

(3)

Trees may also come with unbounded edges which we call legs. A
PSS tree
will be a rooted tree with one leg
l_{0}
.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
y_{0}
be a Hamiltonian orbit of
H^{\lambda}
and fix a surface dependent almost complex structure
J_{S}
. A (parameterized) bubbled
\operatorname{PSS}
solution modelled on
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}
and asymptotic to
y_{0}
is an assignment of a marked curve to each vertex
\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})
as follows. At the root vertex
\nu_{root}
, assign a PSS solution
u_{root}
asymptotic to
y_{0}
with marked points
z_{e}
,
e\in\mathbb{E}(\nu_{root})
. We impose that the marked point
z_{e}
corresponding to the edge closest to
l_{0}
is equal to
z_{0}
. For any other vertex in
\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})\setminus\nu_{root},
we assign a map
u_{\nu}:\mathbb{C}P^{1}\to M.
We require the following conditions to hold:

•

For any vertex
\nu
, let
C_{\nu}
be the domain of the map corresponding to that vertex. For any pair of vertices, let
z_{e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}}\in C_{\nu}
,
z_{e_{\nu^{\prime},\nu}}\in C_{\nu^{\prime}}
be the marked points determined by
e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
and
e_{\nu^{\prime},\nu}
respectively. We require

u_{\nu}(z_{e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}})=u_{\nu^{\prime}}(z_{e_{\nu^{\prime},\nu}}).

•

Moreover, for any
\nu\neq\nu_{root}
, let
e\in\mathbb{E}(\nu_{root})
be the edge closest to this vertex. We require
u_{\nu}
is
J_{S,z(e)}
-holomorphic.

We wish to consider bubbled
\operatorname{PSS}
solutions up to isomorphism of maps
(C,u)\cong(C^{\prime},u^{\prime})
—that is to say

•

an isomorphism of rooted trees
\tau:\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}\to\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{\prime}

•

suitable isomorphisms
\phi_{\nu}:C_{\nu}\cong C^{\prime}_{\tau(\nu)}
of punctured sphere (thimble) or marked
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
for each vertex such that
u_{\nu}=u^{\prime}_{\tau(\nu)}\circ\phi_{\nu}
.

A bubbled
\operatorname{PSS}
solution is stable if its automorphism group is finite. We let
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS},y_{0})
