denote the moduli space of stable PSS solutions modelled on
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}
up to isomorphism.

Remark 3.12
.

As mentioned above, the full Gromov compactification also incorporates cylindrical breaking into Floer trajectories (with further bubbles attached to these). The combinatorics of this is also naturally described by trees. However, cylindrical breaking in our setting will be highly simplified and we therefore don’t describe the somewhat involved notation needed for the general case.

To have compact stable log PSS moduli spaces, it will be necessary to impose further restrictions on the complex structures that we use. In each tubular neighborhood
\pi_{I}:U_{I}\to D_{I}
, the connections
\nabla_{i}
chosen for the regularization induce a canonical direct sum decomposition of the tangent space to any point
p\in U_{I}

(3.20)

\displaystyle T_{p}U_{I}\cong\pi_{I}^{*}TD_{I}\oplus\pi_{I}^{*}ND_{I}

Definition 3.13
.

We say that
J_{\operatorname{std}}\in\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D})
is standard if over each
\pi_{I}:U_{I}\to D_{I}

•

For every point p, the complex structure respects the decomposition (
3.20
).

•

On the first factor
\pi_{I}^{*}TD_{I}
, the almost complex structure is pulled back from one on
D_{I}
.

•

On the second factor
\pi_{I}^{*}ND_{I}\cong\pi_{I}^{*}(\bigoplus_{i}ND_{i})
, the complex structure agrees with the sum

\pi_{I}^{*}(\bigoplus_{i}\mathfrak{i}_{i,I})

where
\mathfrak{i}_{i,I}
is the complex structure on
ND_{i,|D_{I}}
induced from the regularization.

We denote the space of complex structures which are standard in some neighborhood of
\mathbf{D}
by
AK(M,\mathbf{D}).

Definition 3.14
.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{S}(M,\mathbf{D})
denote the space of surface dependent almost complex structures
J_{S}
such that

•

J_{S,s}\in\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D})
for all
s\in S

•

In a neighborhood of
z_{0}
,
J_{S,s}=J_{0}
for some surface independent
J_{0}
which lies in
AK(M,\mathbf{D}).

•

Along the cylindrical end,
J_{S}\in\mathcal{J}_{F}(\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}},V).
for
s<<0
(recall Definition
2.6
).

Note:
For the remainder of the paper, all surface dependent complex structures
J_{S}
are taken to lie in
\mathcal{J}_{S}(M,\mathbf{D})
.

The moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
will be a compact Hausdorff space equipped with a canonical topological embedding
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0})
and the compactifying strata
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})\setminus\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
will have virtual codimension two (or higher). Just as in the classical case, the boundary of the compactification will consist of moduli spaces of “stable log PSS solutions.” In order to define these stable log PSS moduli spaces, we must introduce a bit more combinatorial notation. Let
\mathcal{P}
denote the set of subsets of
\{1,\cdots,k\}
. Given a PSS tree
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}
:

•

a depth function is an assignment

I^{\nu}:\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})\to\mathcal{P},\\
I^{e}:\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})\to\mathcal{P}

such that
I^{\nu_{root}}=\emptyset
and for any
e
which is bounded by vertices
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
,
I^{\nu}\cup I^{\nu^{\prime}}=I^{e}.

•

a contact function is a map

(3.21)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(-):\vec{\mathbb{E}}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})\to\mathbb{Z}^{k}

such that for any pair of neighboring vertices
\nu,\nu^{\prime}

(3.22)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}})=-\mathbf{v}(e_{\nu^{\prime},\nu})

and the support
|\mathbf{v}(e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}})|\subset I^{e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}}
(here we mean the depth function of the edge underlying the oriented edge).

Definition 3.15
.

A log
\operatorname{PSS}
tree
\Gamma=(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS},I^{\bullet},\mathbf{v}(-))
is a triple consisting of

•

a
\operatorname{PSS}
tree
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}
so that
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}\setminus\nu_{root}
is connected.

•

a depth function
I^{\nu},I^{e}.

•

and a contact function
\mathbf{v}(-)
.

The condition that
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}\setminus\nu_{root}
is connected will correspond geometrically to the fact that for stable
\operatorname{PSS}
solutions all sphere bubbles will attach at the marked point
z_{0}\in S
. We set

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\Gamma):=\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}\oplus\bigoplus_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}\mathbb{Z}^{I^{\nu}}.

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}(\Gamma):=\bigoplus_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}\mathbb{Z}^{I^{e}}

For the next construction, we require a choice of an arbitrary orientation on the set of edges
\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})
(we let
\tilde{e}
denote the oriented edge assigned to
e
). After making such a choice, we define a natural homomorphism

(3.23)

\displaystyle\rho:\mathbb{D}(\Gamma){\rightarrow}\mathbb{T}(\Gamma)

by the following rule.
For vectors
1_{e}
in the first component, we set

(3.24)

\displaystyle\rho(1_{e})=\mathbf{v}(\tilde{e})\in\mathbb{Z}^{I^{e}}.

Given a vertex
\nu
,
i\in I^{\nu}
, and
e
an edge, we set

(3.25)

\displaystyle\tau_{\nu,i,e}=:\begin{cases}\vec{1}_{e,i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I^{e}}&\mbox{if }\tilde{e}=e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}\\
-\vec{1}_{e,i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I^{e}}&\mbox{if }\tilde{e}=e_{\nu^{\prime},\nu}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

Finally, we set

\rho(1_{\nu,i})=\sum_{e}\tau_{\nu,i,e}\in\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}^{I^{e}}

This defines the map (
3.23
) on generators and we extend linearly to define the map on all elements. Let
\Lambda
be the image of
\rho
and let
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
denote its complexification.

Definition 3.16
.

We set
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
to be the quotient group

(3.26)

\displaystyle\prod_{e}(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{I^{e}}/\operatorname{exp}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}).

With the preceding understood, we turn to defining moduli spaces of holomorphic curves with incidence conditions modelled on log
\operatorname{PSS}
trees. Observe that for any
u:=(C_{\nu},u_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}\in\mathcal{M}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS},x_{0})
with
x_{0}
a Hamiltonian orbit in
X
, we can define a function
I_{(C_{\nu},u_{\nu})}^{\bullet}
to
\Gamma
as follows.

•

For any vertex
\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})
, let

I_{(C_{\nu},u_{\nu})}^{\nu}:=I_{u_{\nu}}.
(
I_{u_{\nu_{root}}}=\emptyset
corresponding to the fact that the broken PSS solutions pass through points in
X
.)

•

For any edge
e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})
, let
I_{(C_{\nu},u_{\nu})}^{e}
to be the depth of the point
u_{\nu}(z_{\tilde{e}})
; i.e. the subset corresponding to the deepest stratum containing
u_{\nu}(z_{\tilde{e}})
.

This is not quite a depth function for general
u:=(C_{\nu},u_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}\in\mathcal{M}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS},x_{0})
because it only satisfies
I^{\nu}\cup I^{\nu^{\prime}}\subset I^{e}
(as opposed to equality), however, in the following definition we restrict our attention to those solutions for which this assignment does define a depth function.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
\Gamma
be a log
\operatorname{PSS}
tree (equipped with a depth function
I^{\bullet}
and contact function
\mathbf{v}(-)
).
A pre-log (stable) map of multiplicity
\mathbf{v}
, modelled on
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}
, and asymptotic to
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
consists of a collection

(C_{\nu},u_{\nu},[\zeta]_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}
where

•

(C_{\nu},u_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}
define
a stable
\operatorname{PSS}
solution
u_{PSS}:C\to M
asymptotic to
x_{0}
and modelled on
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}
. We require that the function
I_{(C_{\nu},u_{\nu})}^{\bullet}
associated to
(C_{\nu},u_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}
be equal to
I^{\bullet}.

•

For each
\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})\setminus\nu_{root}

(3.27)

\displaystyle[\zeta]_{\nu}=([\zeta_{i}])_{\nu}\in\{\Gamma_{\operatorname{mero}}(\mathbb{C}P^{1},u_{\nu}^{*}(ND_{i}))/\mathbb{C}^{*}\}_{i\in I^{\nu}}

is a collection of meromorphic sections (defined up to scale) such that the associated functions

\{\operatorname{ord}_{\nu}\}_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}
satisfy:

(i)

\operatorname{ord}_{\nu}
is non-vanishing only at the marked points corresponding to
l_{0}
and to edges
e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}\in\vec{\mathbb{E}}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}).

(ii)

We have that
\operatorname{ord}_{\vec{\nu}}(z_{l_{0}})=\mathbf{v}
and for any vertex
\nu
,

(3.28)

\displaystyle\operatorname{ord}_{\nu}(z_{e})=\mathbf{v}(e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}),

where
z_{e}
is the marked point on
C_{\nu}
corresponding to
e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}.

An isomorphism of pre-log maps
(C,u,\{[\zeta]\})\cong(C^{\prime},u^{\prime},\{[\zeta^{\prime}]\})
is an isomorphism of PSS solutions such that

\phi_{\nu}^{*}([\zeta^{\prime}_{i}]_{\tau(\nu)})=[\zeta_{i}]_{\nu}.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0})
denote the moduli space of stable pre-log maps (with the natural
C^{\infty}_{loc}
topology) and
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0})
denote the moduli space of stable pre-log maps up to isomorphism.

Proposition 3.18
.

The expected dimension of a stratum
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0})
is given by:

(3.29)

\displaystyle\operatorname{vdim}(\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0}))=\operatorname{deg}(x_{0})+2(\sum_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}(|I^{e}|-1)-\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}|I^{\nu}|)

Proof.

Let
e_{root}\in\mathbb{E}(\nu_{root})
denote the edge which corresponds to
\{\infty\}.
Then the expected dimension of the spherical components before imposing the incidence condition is

\sum_{\nu\neq\nu_{root}}(2n-2|I^{\nu}|-6)+\sum_{e\neq e_{root}}4+4

where the second term comes from the fact that each edge corresponds to two marked points and the last term corresponds to the two additional marked points on the spherical components. Combining this with (
3.15
) shows that the expected dimension of all components before imposing the incidence condition is:

(3.30)

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}(x_{0})+\sum_{\nu\neq\nu_{root}}(2n-2|I^{\nu}|-6)+\sum_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}4

The matching condition at the marked points is a codimension
\sum_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}2n-2|I^{e}|
condition. Thus the expected dimension of
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0})
is given by

\operatorname{deg}(x_{0})+\sum_{\nu\neq\nu_{root}}(2n-2|I^{\nu}|-6)+\sum_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}4-\sum_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}2n-2|I^{e}|

which is easily seen to be equal to (
3.29
) (recalling that
I^{\nu_{root}}=\emptyset)
. ∎

When the divisor
\mathbf{D}
is not smooth, the quantity

\sum_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}(|I^{e}|-1)-\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}|I^{\nu}|

can potentially be non-negative (and in fact positive) for certain nodal pre-log curves and so we must impose further conditions on our curves to obtain a well-behaved compactification. The key to doing this is the following construction. Suppose we are given a pre-log map
(C,u,\{[\zeta]\})
with underlying graph
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}
and let
e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})
be an edge connecting two vertices
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
(i.e.
e\in\mathbb{E}(\nu)\cap\mathbb{E}(\nu^{\prime})
). As in (
3.25
), we arbitrarily choose an orientation
\tilde{e}=e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
on
e
. Suppose that
C_{\nu}
and
C_{\nu}^{\prime}
are equipped with meromorphic lifts
\zeta_{i}\in\Gamma_{\operatorname{mero}}(u^{*}(ND_{i}))
of each equivalence class
[\zeta_{i}]
. Choose coordinate charts
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
centered about
z_{e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}}
and
z_{e_{\nu^{\prime},\nu}}
respectively so that the expansions (
3.3
) and (
3.7
) hold. For each
i\in I^{e}
, we set:

(3.31)

\displaystyle\eta_{e,i}=(\eta_{i,z_{e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}}}/\eta_{i,z_{e_{\nu^{\prime},\nu}}})\in\mathbb{C}^{*}

Letting
e
range over all edges
e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})
then determines an element

(3.32)

\displaystyle\prod_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}(\eta_{e,i})_{i\in I^{e}}\in\prod_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{I^{e}}

Lemma 3.19
.

The construction (
3.32
) descends to a well-defined map

(3.33)

\displaystyle ob_{\Gamma}:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0})\to\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)

Moreover, (
3.33
) is continuous in the Gromov topology.

Proof.

The action of the subgroup
\operatorname{exp}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
correspond to changing the coordinate chart as well as lifts
\zeta_{i}
of the equivalence classes
[\zeta_{i}]
which are involved in the construction of (
3.32
). For example, changing the coordinate chart at a marked point rescales (
3.31
) by an element of the one-dimensional subgroup generated by a vector
\rho(1_{e})
from (
3.24
). Thus, the element is well-defined modulo the action of the subgroup
\operatorname{exp}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
. The continuity of (
3.33
) follows from the fact that, in the absence of bubbling (which occurs in deeper strata), the jet maps vary continuously in the
C_{loc}^{\infty}
topology. ∎

Definition 3.20
.

A log map (modelled on
\Gamma
) consists of a pre-log map

(C_{\nu},u_{\nu},[\zeta]_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0})

satisfying the following additional conditions:

(i)

There exists functions
\mathbf{v}_{\nu}:\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})\to\mathbb{R}^{k},\lambda_{e}:\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that

(a)

\mathbf{v}_{\nu}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{I^{\nu}}\times\{0\}^{k-|I^{\nu}|}

(b)

for every pair of vertices
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
connected by an (oriented) edge
e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
,
5
5

5

Below
\lambda_{e}(e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}})
denotes the value of
\lambda_{e}
on the edge underlying the oriented edge.

\mathbf{v}_{\nu}-\mathbf{v}_{\nu^{\prime}}=\lambda_{e}(e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}})\mathbf{v}(e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}).

(ii)

We have that

(3.34)

\displaystyle ob_{\Gamma}(u)=[1]\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)

We let
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0})
denote the moduli space of log maps (modelled on
\Gamma
) up to isomorphism.

The next proposition shows that once we have imposed these additional conditions, the strata
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0})
all have expected dimension at most
\operatorname{deg}(x_{0})-2:

Proposition 3.21
.

For any log PSS tree
\Gamma
, let
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma))
denote the complexification of the kernel of (
3.23
). The expected dimension of a stratum
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0})
is:

(3.35)

\displaystyle\operatorname{vdim}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma,x_{0}))=\operatorname{deg}(x_{0})-2(\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma))

Moreover, the only non-empty stratum with
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma))=0
is the stratum where
\Gamma_{PSS}
is a point (“the main stratum”).

Proof.

We have that the dimension of
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)
is given by

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{G}(\Gamma))=2(\sum_{e}(|I^{e}|-1)-\sum_{v}|I_{v}|+\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)).

(This is just an exercise in elementary linear algebra.) Combining this with (
3.29
) gives the statement on expected dimensions. The element
\mathbf{v}_{\nu}
defines an element of
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)
which is non-trivial whenever
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}
is, demonstrating the non-triviality of the kernel. ∎

Lemma 3.22
.

Given a stable bubbled PSS solution (as in Definition
3.11
) there is at most one (“log”) lift to a stable log PSS solution (as in Definition
3.20
).

Proof.

This follows exactly as in the proof of Lemma 3.14 of
[
19
]
. ∎

As noted in Remark
3.12
, to obtain a compact moduli space, we also need to account for breaking along the cylindrical end:

Definition 3.23
.

Fix a multiplicity
\mathbf{v}
and orbit
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda}).
A stable, broken log
\operatorname{PSS}
solution
u
consists of a collection of orbits
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
for
i\in\{1,\cdots,\ell_{u}\}
together with:

•

a sequence of Floer trajectories
u_{Floer,i}\in\mathcal{M}(x_{i-1},x_{i})
(in particular these curves lie in
X
and have no sphere bubbles attached.)

•

a stable log PSS solution
u_{PSS}
with output
x_{\ell_{u}}
.

We let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
denote the moduli space of stable, broken log
\operatorname{PSS}
solutions. In view of Lemma
3.22
, we can topologize
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
as a subspace of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0})
. Note that it is therefore a priori Hausdorff. The remainder of this section will be devoted to demonstrating compactness of these moduli spaces (see Corollary
3.27
). We first recall quickly what it means for a sequence of stable PSS solutions to Gromov converge to stable, broken PSS solutions (in the classical sense):
6
6

6

As in Remark
3.12
, the definition below does not account for the case where sphere bubbles attach on the Floer trajectories. That is for brevity and because these will not arise in our setup.

Definition 3.24
.

Recall that if
u_{n}
is a sequence of
stable\operatorname{PSS}
solutions, modelled on PSS trees
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(n)}
, we say that
u_{n}
Gromov converges to a stable, broken solution
u_{\infty}
modelled on
(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS},\underline{\Gamma}_{i})
(where all
\underline{\Gamma}_{i}
are trivial trees with a single vertex) if there exists contractions of rooted trees
\tau_{n}:\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}\to\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(n)}
, such that :

•

for each vertex
\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})\setminus\nu_{root}
, there exists Moebius transforms
\phi_{\nu,n}
such that
u_{\nu,n}=u_{\tau_{n}(\nu)}\circ\phi_{\nu,n}
converges to
u_{\nu,\infty}
on compact sets
K\subset C_{\nu}\setminus\cup_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\nu)}z_{e}
(here
z_{e}
are the nodal points on
C_{\nu}
).

•

u_{\nu_{root},\infty}=\operatorname{lim}_{n\to\infty}u_{\nu_{root},n}
in the
C^{\infty}_{loc}
topology.

•

for every
i
, there exist a sequence of constants
s_{n}^{i}\to-\infty
such that
u_{\nu_{root},n}(s+s_{n}^{i},t)
converges to the corresponding Floer trajectory
u_{Floer,i}
.

As has already been discussed in §
3.1
, a central feature of the relative setting is that components may “fall into
\mathbf{D}
.” For example, we may have a sequence of stable log
\operatorname{PSS}
maps
(C_{n},u_{n},\{[\zeta]\}_{n})
, where none of the components lie in
D_{i}
and yet the limit contains a component which does lie in
D_{i}
. (Of course, one must consider intermediate cases where some of the components already lie in
D_{i}
and a new component falls into the divisor.) Thus this component
u_{\nu}
should inherit a equivalence classes of meromorphic sections
[\zeta_{i}]\in\Gamma(u_{\nu}^{*}(ND_{i}))/\mathbb{C}^{*}.
The key construction is the following rescaling construction:

Rescaling construction(compare §3.2 of
[
19
]
):

Fix
J\in AK(M,\mathbf{D}).
Suppose we have a sequence of
J
-holomorphic maps
(C_{n},u_{n})
such that
u_{n}\not\subset D_{i}
converging to
u_{\infty}
which
does
contain a component
u_{\nu}
with
u_{\nu}(C_{\nu})\subset D_{i}
.(Recall the notations
\tau_{n}
and
\phi_{\nu,n}
from Definition
3.24
.) For any
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*},
set

(3.36)

\displaystyle R_{t}:ND_{i}\to ND_{i},\quad R_{t}(v)=tv

Let
\psi_{i}:U_{i}\to M
be the regularizing map and set

(3.37)

\displaystyle\psi_{t}=\psi_{i}\circ R_{t}:R_{t}^{-1}(U_{i})\to M

First consider a fixed compact subset
K\subset C_{\nu}
disjoint from the nodal points. We can assume that for
n
large, that

(3.38)

\displaystyle u_{n,K}:=\psi^{-1}\circ u_{\tau_{n}(\nu)}\circ\phi_{\nu,n}(K)\subset U_{i}

and is therefore described by an (a priori just smooth) section
\zeta_{n,K}
of the normal bundle, the norm of which converges to 0 as
n\to\infty
. For a sequence of non-zero complex numbers
(t_{n,K})
, set

(3.39)

\displaystyle u_{t_{n,K}}:=\psi_{t_{n,K}}^{-1}\circ u_{\tau_{n}(\nu)}\circ\phi_{\nu,n}.

Fix a positive real number
c_{K}
and choose a sequence of
(t_{n,K})
so that
\operatorname{sup}||t_{n,K}^{-1}\psi_{n,K}||=c_{K}.
Let

ND_{i,c_{k}}\subset ND_{i}:=\{v\in ND_{i},||v||\leq c_{k}\}.

For
n
sufficiently large, the sequence of maps
u_{t_{n,K}}
defines a sequence of holomorphic maps
u_{t_{n,K}}:K\to ND_{i,c_{K}}
for a standard complex structure
J_{\operatorname{std}}
pulled back to
ND_{i,c_{K}}
(this uses the fact that our complex structure is in
AK(M,\mathbf{D})
). Then by Gromov compactness, this converges to a holomorphic section
\zeta_{\nu,K}\in\Gamma(K,u_{\nu}^{*}(ND_{i})).

Of course, we must construct this section consistently over an exhaustive sequence of compact sets

K_{1}\subset K_{2}\subset\cdots.

To do this, choose a reference point
p
and assume
K_{j}
all contain this point. We may, after possibly rescaling
c_{K_{i}}
and the
(t_{n,K})
and the vectors
\zeta_{\nu,K_{i}}(p)
agree with a fixed vector
v_{p}\in ND_{i,u_{\nu}(p)}
. As a result, we get a holomorphic section over the open part of
C_{\nu}
which extends meromorphically over all of
C_{\nu}

[
19
, Proposition 3.10]
.

This construction leads naturally to a definition for convergence of log maps:

Definition 3.25
.

We say that a sequence of log
\operatorname{PSS}
maps
(C_{n},u_{n},\{[\zeta]\}_{n})
modelled on log
\operatorname{PSS}
trees
\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}
converges to
(C,u_{\infty},\{[\zeta]\}
modelled on
\Gamma
if
u_{n}
converges to
u_{\infty}
as ordinary
\operatorname{PSS}
solutions (recall the notations
\tau_{n}
and
\phi_{\nu,n}
from Definition
3.24
) and moreover there exists a subsequence
u_{g(n)}
(
g:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a strictly increasing function) such that:

•

the log trees
\Gamma^{\prime}_{g(n)}
have fixed underlying graph
\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
and depth function
(I^{\prime})^{\bullet}
.

•

Let
\tilde{\zeta}_{n}=\zeta_{g(n),i,\tau_{g(n)}(\nu)}.
For a given vertex
\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma})
and
i\in(I^{\prime})^{\tau_{g(n)}(\nu)}
there exists a sequence
(t_{\nu,n,i})
of non-zero complex numbers such that for every compact set
K\subset C_{\nu}\setminus\cup_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\nu)}z_{e}
,

t_{\nu,n,i}^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\zeta}_{n}\circ\phi_{\nu,n}|K

converges uniformly to
\zeta_{i,\nu}.

•

For a given vertex
\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma})
and
i\in I^{\nu}\setminus(I^{\prime})^{\tau_{g(n)}(\nu)}
, there exists a sequence
(t_{\nu,n,i})
of non-zero complex numbers so that for any
K\subset C_{\nu}\setminus\cup_{e\in\mathbb{E}(\nu)}z_{e}
,
7
7

7

the quantity in (
3.40
) is only defined for sufficiently large
n

(3.40)

\displaystyle\psi_{t_{\nu,n,i}}^{-1}\circ u_{\tau_{n}(\nu)}\circ\phi_{\nu,n}|K

converges uniformly to
\zeta_{i,\nu}.

The key compactness statement is then the following:

Theorem 3.26
.

Suppose that
\lambda>w(\mathbf{v})
and that
||H^{\lambda}-h^{\lambda}||_{C^{2}}
is sufficiently small. Then given a sequence of stable log
\operatorname{PSS}
curves
(C_{n},u_{n},[\zeta]_{n})
there a subsequence which converges in the sense of Definition
3.25
to a stable log
\operatorname{PSS}
curves
(C,u_{\infty},\{[\zeta]\}).

Proof.

Given the sequence of curves
(C_{n},u_{n},[\zeta]_{n})
, after passing to a subsequence, ordinary Gromov compactness produces a limit
(C,u_{\infty})
. In view of Lemma
3.7
and (
3.16
) we have that for any Floer trajectory component
u_{Floer}
of
u_{\infty}
,

(3.41)

\displaystyle E_{top}(u_{Floer})\leq w(\mathbf{v})+A_{H^{\lambda}}(x_{0})+\hbar

where
\hbar
can be made arbitrarily small by taking
||H^{\lambda}-h^{\lambda}||_{C^{2}}
sufficiently small. Lemma
2.9
therefore shows that
u_{Floer}
cannot have output in
X
and input in
\mathbf{D}
from which it follows that the sequence
u_{n}
does not break along
\mathbf{D}
.

Let us first discuss the sphere bubbles which attach onto the PSS component
u_{\infty,\nu_{root}}
at
z_{0}\in S
(
=\{\infty\}\in\mathbb{C}P^{1}\setminus\{0\}
). Then by the “rescaling construction” reviewed just before Definition
3.25

we conclude that each of these sphere bubbles may be equipped with equivalence classes of meromorphic sections
[\zeta_{i}]_{i\in I^{\nu}}
; Notice that if
\tau_{n}(\nu)
is the root vertex and
u_{\tau_{n}(\nu)}
is a
\operatorname{PSS}
solution, we can assume that
\phi_{\nu,n}(K)
lies in the region where
\beta=0
(
u_{\tau_{n}(\nu)}
is genuinely holomorphic) and so the construction and the arguments below apply to these sphere bubbles as well without modification. Let
\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)
denote the set of vertices on the tree connecting
l_{0}
to
\nu_{root}
(including
\nu_{root}
). Equation
3.22
holds for any pair of vertices
\nu,\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)
by the proof of Theorem 4.9 of
[
19
]
. By construction, we also have that

(3.42)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)\setminus\nu_{root}}([u_{\infty,\nu}]\cdot D_{i})+\operatorname{ord}(z_{0})

where
[u_{\infty,\nu}]\in H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
denotes the homology class of the sphere bubble component
u_{\infty,\nu}.

Now we rule out the possibility of sphere bubbling occuring at some other point
in
S
or along a Floer cylinder. Let
\mathcal{B}
denote the set of sphere bubble components which attach at a point other than
z_{0}
. By positivity of symplectic area, there must exist a divisor
D_{i}
for which

(3.43)

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}}[u_{\infty,b}]\cdot D_{i}>0.

By positivity of
intersection with
\mathbf{D}
, any Floer cylinder or
\operatorname{PSS}
solution must intersect
D_{i}
with non-negative multiplicity. This however contradicts (
3.42
) which implies that the sum of all intersections with
D_{i}
away from
z_{0}
must be zero.

This implies that the collection
(C,u_{\infty},\{[\zeta]\})
defines a pre-log PSS solution. The fact that is a log PSS solution is therefore equivalent to satisfying Conditions (i) and (ii) of Definition
3.20
. But this follows from the proof of Theorem 4.10 of
[
19
]
(which also applies without modification).

∎

Corollary 3.27
.

Suppose that
\lambda>w(\mathbf{v})
and that
||H^{\lambda}-h^{\lambda}||_{C^{2}}
is sufficiently small. Then
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
is a (sequentially) closed subspace of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0})
and is therefore (sequentially) compact.

Definition 3.28
.

Let
\mathbf{v}
be as in Corollary
3.27
and let
c
be a connected component of
D_{|\mathbf{v}|}
. For any orbit
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
, define
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0},c)\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
to be the moduli space of log PSS solutions with

u(z_{0})\in D_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},x_{0},c)
denote the closure of this moduli space in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},x_{0})
.

4.
Proof of Main Theorem

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.1
. From here until §
4.4
, we continue working in the context of log nef pairs (recall the beginning of §
3
). For any log nef pair, we will set
\Delta(\mathbf{D})_{(0)}
to denote the sub
\Delta
-complex of the dual intersection complex generated by those divisors
D_{i}
along which the chosen holomorphic volume form in (
3.1
) has a pole of order one. We also set

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}:=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}\in B(M,\mathbf{D})}H^{0}(D_{|\mathbf{v}|},{\mathbf{k}}).

This
{\mathbf{k}}
-module has a basis
\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}
, where
\mathbf{v}\in B(M,\mathbf{D})
and
c
is a connected component of
D_{|\mathbf{v}|}
. Equip
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}
with the product defined by extending (
1.2
)
{\mathbf{k}}
-linearly. The ring
(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}},\ast_{\operatorname{SR}})
will be denoted by
\mathcal{SR}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D})_{(0)}).
It is immediate that these definitions all generalize the corresponding definitions for Calabi-Yau pairs given just before Theorem
1.2
.

From §
4.4
onwards, we restrict our attention to Calabi-Yau pairs.

4.1.
Filtered symplectic cohomology

All of the constructions from §
3
are for Hamiltonians of a fixed slope
\lambda
. To transfer them to symplectic cohomology, we need to take the direct limit over Hamiltonians of higher slopes. To carry out our argument, we will choose our sequence of Hamiltonians appearing in the direct limit a bit more carefully(as compared to (
2.24
)) to ensure that

•

the filtration by
F_{w}
extends to the direct limit

•

certain (“low energy”) log PSS solutions used to define the map
(
4.9
) have sufficiently small topological energy.

This is accomplished by taking a sequence of Liouville domains
\bar{X}_{m}:=\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}_{m}}
(indexed by
m\in\mathbb{N}^{>0}
) closer and closer to the divisor
\mathbf{D}
as the slope increases. We will assume that
\bar{X}_{m_{1}}\subseteq\bar{X}_{m_{2}}
whenever
m_{1}<m_{2}
. Denote by

\Sigma_{m}:=\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}_{m}}
to be the corresponding boundary
\partial\bar{X}_{m}
of
\bar{X}_{m}.
We also let
w_{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{>0}
be a sequence of positive integers with
w_{m+1}>w_{m}
and choose for each
w_{m}
a real number
\lambda_{m}\notin\operatorname{Spec}(\bar{X}_{m})
with
w_{m}<\lambda_{m}<w_{m+1}.
Let
R^{m}
be
the Liouville coordinate for each
m
(which as before extends smoothly over
\mathbf{D}
) and choose an adapted Hamiltonian of slope
\lambda_{m}
,
h^{m}(R^{m})
. As before, this extends
smoothly to a Hamiltonian on all of
M
, which we also call
h^{m}
:

(4.2)

h^{m}:=h^{m}(R^{m}):M\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}.

We also fix the regions
V_{m}
and
V_{0,m}
as well as the perturbed, nondegenerate Hamiltonians
H^{m}
(see
4.2
for more details on these perturbations). Provided all of this data is suitably chosen, it is shown in
[
27
, Lemma 2.16]
that there are filtration preserving continuation maps

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{m_{1},m_{2}}:F_{w}CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{m_{1}})\to F_{w}CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{m_{2}})

whenever
m_{2}\geq m_{1}
. We may therefore define:

(4.4)

\displaystyle SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}}):=\varinjlim_{m}HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{m})

By counting pairs of pants satisfying a suitable variant of
Floer’s equation,
we may also equip
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
with a filtration preserving product.
Using a standard cochain-level direct limit construction, it is not hard to lift these limits to a cochain complex
SC^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
such that
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})\cong H^{*}(SC^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
in such a way that the filtrations by
w(\mathbf{v})
gives it the structure of a filtered complex. To do this, we define

(4.5)

\displaystyle SC^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}}):=\bigoplus_{m}CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{m})[q]

where
q
is
a formal variable of degree
-1
such that
q^{2}=0
. For
a+qb\in CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{m})[q]
, the differential on this complex is given by the
formula

(4.6)

\displaystyle\partial(a+qb)=(-1)^{\deg(a)}\partial(a)+(-1)^{\deg(b)}(q\partial(b)+\mathfrak{c}_{m,m+1}(b)-b)

Moreover, the corresponding descending filtration
F^{p}SC^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
is bounded above and exhaustive, which enables us to make use of the standard machinery of spectral sequences:

Lemma 4.1
.

The descending filtration
F^{p}SC^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
gives rise to a convergent multiplicative spectral sequence

(4.7)

\displaystyle E_{r}^{p,q}=>SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})

whose first page can be identified with the direct limit

(4.8)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{q}E_{1}^{p,q}:=\varinjlim_{m}HF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{m})_{w=-p}

where the groups at each stage of the direct limit are the low energy Floer groups from (
2.29
) and the maps are induced by (
4.3
).

We now turn to recalling the definition of the canonical map

(4.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}\to\bigoplus_{p}E^{p,-p}_{1}

constructed in
[
27
]
. Recalling that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}\in B(M,\mathbf{D})}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}
, we will define this map on each generator
\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}
and extend linearly. For any
(\mathbf{v},c)
, choose
m
so that
w(\mathbf{v})\leq w_{m}
and let
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{m})
be an orbit such that
\deg(x_{0})=0
and
w(x_{0})=w(\mathbf{v})
. The moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0},c)
(recall Definition
3.28
) for such orbits are called low energy log PSS moduli spaces. This is because (provided our Hamiltonians are taken as in Lemma
3.9
) it follows from Equation
3.17
that the energies of such solutions are so small that no sphere bubbling arises in the compactification of these moduli spaces. After taking
J_{S}
generic, the fact there is no bubbling enables us to define an element
\widehat{\operatorname{PSS}}_{log}^{m}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})\in HF^{0}(X\subset M;H^{m})_{w(\mathbf{v})}
by counting such low energy solutions:

(4.10)

\displaystyle\widehat{\operatorname{PSS}}_{log}^{m}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})=\sum_{x_{0},w(x_{0})=w(\mathbf{v})}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},x_{0},c)}\mu_{u}

Where
\mu_{u}\in|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{0}}|
is again an element determined by standard gluing/orientation theory. For any
m^{\prime}\geq m
, we have that:

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{m,m^{\prime}}\circ\widehat{\operatorname{PSS}}_{log}^{m}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})=\widehat{\operatorname{PSS}}_{log}^{m^{\prime}}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})

and thus it follows from (
4.8
) that this defines a well-defined element
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})\in\bigoplus_{p}E^{p,-p}_{1}.
This defines the map (
4.9
) on basis elements after which we extend linearly. For the next result, we equip
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}
with the ring structure
\ast_{\operatorname{SR}}
from (
1.2
). The following is a special case of
[
27
, Theorem 1.1]
:

Theorem 4.2
.

The map (
4.9
) is an isomorphism of rings.

4.2.
Degree zero
SH^{*}
relative to a (stabilizing) divisor

As mentioned in the introduction, we will use the approach of stabilizing divisors to achieve regularity
[
14
]
. This approach is somewhat
ad hoc
as it involves auxillary choices compared to the more canonical virtual techniques, but avoids having to prove analytically involved gluing theorems dictating how the different strata of the moduli space fit together. The basic definitions are the following:

Definition 4.3
.

A stabilizing divisor for the pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
is another ample normal crossings divisor
\mathbf{E}=\cup_{j}E_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\cdots,k^{\prime}\}

which meets
\mathbf{D}
transversely, so that
\mathbf{D}\cup\mathbf{E}
is also a normal crossings divisor.

Definition 4.4
.

A stabilization datum
(M,\mathbf{D})
consists of a stabilizing divisor
\mathbf{E}=\cup_{j}E_{j}
together with a
a regularization for the pair
(M,\mathbf{D}\cup\mathbf{E})
such that for generic
J\in\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D}\cup\mathbf{E})
, and any subset
K\subset\{1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}
, there are no non-constant
J
-holomorphic curves
u:\mathbb{C}P^{1}\to E_{K}
which intersect
\cup_{j\notin K}(E_{j}\cap E_{K})
in two or fewer distinct points.

Note that as per our usual convention, we include the case
I=\emptyset
above,
with
D_{\emptyset}=M
. It is easy to construct stabilized regularizations by taking a
\mathbf{E}
to be union of
2n+1
generic divisors in the linear system of
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes q}
for some sufficiently large
q
. The basic point behind the definition is that any non-constant curves contained in
\mathbf{E}
will be stable and thus we will be able to use surface dependent perturbations on these components to regularize them.

We now introduce “
\mathbf{E}
-marked” (=relative to
\mathbf{E}
) versions of our Floer theoretic constructions. Observe that a regularization for
(M,\mathbf{D}\cup\mathbf{E})
gives one a regularization for
(M,\mathbf{D}).
Whenever we have a stabilizing divisor, we can assume, after changing possibly changing the regularization of
(M,\mathbf{D})
, that the chosen regularization is compatible with the one used to define
SH^{*}(X)
(Liouville domains
\bar{X}_{m}
, Hamiltonian functions
h^{m}
etc.). We may also arrange it so that in tubular neighborhood
U_{I,K}
of
D_{I}\cap E_{K}
we have the Liouville one form
\theta
restricts to the fiber of
\pi_{I,K}:U_{I,K}\to D_{I}\cap E_{K}
as:

(4.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{i}-\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi})d\varphi_{i}+\sum_{j\in J}\frac{1}{2}\rho_{j}d\varphi_{j}

We will do all of this going forward. Fix one of these Liouville manifolds
\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
and consider the set of complex structures:

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}},\mathbf{E}):=\mathcal{J}(\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}},V)\cap\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D}\cup\mathbf{E})

By construction, the Reeb flow on
\partial\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
preserves
\mathbf{E}
and it follows from equation (
4.12
) that the Liouville flow also preserves
\mathbf{E}.
It is therefore not difficult to construct to show that
\mathcal{J}(\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}},\mathbf{E})
is non-empty. For a given admissible
h^{\lambda}
(of fixed slope
\lambda
), the flow preserves
\mathbf{E}.
We almost must consider our perturbed functions
H^{\lambda}
. We need to choose them so that they have the following properties (in addition to those needed in Section 2):

Properties of
H^{\lambda}
:

(a)

X_{H^{\lambda}}
preserves
E_{j}
for all
j
.

(b)

All Hamiltonian orbits
x_{0}
which lie in any
E_{j}\cap X
have
\deg(x_{0})\geq 2
.

Property
(a)
will be needed so that Floer trajectories not entirely contained in
\mathbf{E}
intersect each divisor
E_{j}
with positive multiplicity. Our constructions in this paper will only involve orbits of index
\leq 1
(because we’re studying degree zero
SH^{*}(X)
). Property
(b)
ensures that all of the relevant Hamiltonian orbits are disjoint from
\mathbf{E}
, meaning that none of the Floer curves that connect them can be entirely contained in
\mathbf{E}
. To explain why we can choose Hamiltonians with these properties, we need to recall a bit more of the perturbations from
[
27
, Section 4.1]
:

Isolating neighborhoods:
We first specify the isolating neighborhoods
U_{\mathbf{v}}
of the critical sets
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}.
For nonzero values of
\mathbf{v}
, the orbit sets
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
occur at points
U_{I}
where
\rho_{i}=\rho_{i,\mathbf{v}}
for some
\rho_{i,\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in I
and have boundary and
corner strata where
\rho_{i}=\rho^{c}_{\mathbf{v},i}
for some
\rho^{c}_{\mathbf{v},i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\notin I
. We let
c_{0}
be a sufficiently small positive real number and let
D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}
denote the open manifold
D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}:=D_{|\mathbf{v}|}\setminus\cup_{i\notin|\mathbf{v}|}(U_{i,\rho^{c}_{\mathbf{v},i}-c_{0}}\cap D_{|\mathbf{v}|})
(where the sets
U_{i,\rho^{c}_{\mathbf{v},i}-c_{0}}
are defined just below Theorem
2.3
). Let
S^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}

denote the induced
T^{|\mathbf{v}|}
bundle over
D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}
where

\rho_{i}=\rho^{c}_{\mathbf{v},i}
. We set the isolating sets to be the neighborhoods
U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset U_{I}
such that

\pi_{I}(x)\in D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\rho_{i,\mathbf{v}}-c_{0}<\rho_{i}<\rho_{i,\mathbf{v}}+c_{0},\ i\in I
. For
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{0}}
,
choose our isolating neighborhood
U_{\mathbf{0}}
to be the complement of neighborhood where

U_{\mathbf{0}}:=M\setminus\{R^{\vec{\epsilon}}\geq R_{0}+c_{0}\}

After possibly shrinking
c_{0}
, these neighborhoods are pairwise disjoint, i.e.
U_{\mathbf{v}}\cap U_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for
\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
. We
let
U_{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
to be slightly smaller subsets such that
U_{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\subset U_{\mathbf{v}}

which are of the same form (to construct them just take choose a constant

c_{0}^{\prime}
which is slightly smaller than
c_{0}
).

Hamiltonian perturbations:
All of the
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
are Morse-Bott in their interiors
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}\setminus\partial\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
(see Step 2 of the proof of Theorem 5.16 of
[
63
]
)
and thus Morse-Bott type perturbations are required to make
the orbits non-degenerate. These perturbations all take place locally in each isolating set
U_{\mathbf{v}}.
When
\mathbf{v}=0
, we will perturb by a Morse function

h_{\mathbf{0}}
which near
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}=R_{0}
is a
function of
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}
with positive derivative. To carry this out when
\mathbf{v}\neq 0
, it is convenient to recall the “spinning” construction (see e.g.
[
56
, Proof of Prop. B.4]
for a nice reference). This enables us to (locally in each
U_{\mathbf{v}}
) convert our Hamiltonian system to an equivalent one where the orbits in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
are constant (after which we can then use a suitable Morse function to perturb the flow). For
non-constant orbits, observe that on all of the orbit sets
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
, the Reeb flow generates an
S^{1}
-action on

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
which extends canonically to an
S^{1}
action on
U_{\mathbf{v}}
. This action is Hamiltonian, with some Hamiltonian
K_{S^{1}}
, and
we let
g_{t}
denote the flow of the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{K_{S^{1}}}
on
U_{\mathbf{v}}
. On
U_{\mathbf{v}}
, the function

(4.14)

\displaystyle\hat{h}^{\lambda}:=h^{\lambda}_{|U_{\mathbf{v}}}-K

has constant Hamiltonian orbits along
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
. Next, choose a Morse function
\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}^{base}:S^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that near the corners the function
\hat{h}_{I}
point outwards along the boundary and let
\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}
denote the pull-back of
\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}^{base}
to
U_{\mathbf{v}}
. Finally, we choose cutoff functions
\rho_{\mathbf{v}}
supported in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
(for simplicity we take to be
S^{1}
equivariant in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
) such that

•

\rho_{\mathbf{v}}(x)=0,x\in M\setminus U_{\mathbf{v}}

•

\rho_{\mathbf{v}}(x)=1,x\in U_{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}

Set

\hat{H}^{\lambda}:=\delta_{\mathbf{v}}\rho_{\mathbf{v}}\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}+\hat{h}^{\lambda}

Then for
\delta_{\mathbf{v}}
sufficiently small, vall of the orbits of
\hat{H}^{\lambda}
are in bijection with critical points of
\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}
along
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
(see
[
27
, Lemma 4.2]
based on
[
56
, Proof of Prop. B.4]
). One can now translate this perturbation back to our original Hamiltonian system by setting
H^{\lambda}:S^{1}\times U_{\mathbf{v}}\to\mathbb{R}
to be the function

(4.15)

\displaystyle H^{\lambda}:=\delta_{\mathbf{v}}\rho_{\mathbf{v}}h_{\mathbf{v}}+h^{\lambda}

where
h_{\mathbf{v}}:S^{1}\times U_{\mathbf{v}}\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the time dependent function given by
h_{\mathbf{v}}:=\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}(t,g^{-1}_{t}(x))
(the “spinning of
\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}
”). Orbits of
H^{\lambda}
are then non-degenerate perturbations of the initial orbits in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
. Moreover, if
\hat{J}_{t}:=dg_{t}^{-1}\circ J(g_{t}x)\circ dg_{t}
, the assignment

(4.16)

\displaystyle u\to g_{t}\circ u

takes transverse Floer trajectories
u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\to U_{\mathbf{v}}
for the pair
(\hat{H}^{\lambda},\hat{J}_{t})
to transverse Floer trajectories for the pair
(H^{\lambda},J_{t})
(see
[
76
, Lemma 4.2]
for a much more general result).

To globalize this, notice that a similar construction (spelled out in
[
26
, §4.1]
) yields analogous functions
h_{\mathbf{D}}
,
\rho_{\mathbf{D}}
supported near
\mathbf{D}
which can be used to perturb the divisorial orbit sets. For sufficiently small constants
\delta_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\delta_{\mathbf{D}}
define:

(4.17)

\displaystyle H^{\lambda}=\sum_{\mathbf{v}}\delta_{\mathbf{v}}\rho_{\mathbf{v}}h_{\mathbf{v}}+h^{\lambda}+\delta_{\mathbf{D}}\rho_{\mathbf{D}}h_{\mathbf{D}}

The crucial property for us that the function
H^{\lambda}
is non-degenerate and that the orbits which lie in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
are in bijection with critical points of the corresponding
\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}^{base}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

There exist perturbed Hamiltonians
H^{\lambda}
satisfying Properties
(a)
,
(b)
above.

Proof.

If the Hamiltonian flow of the functions
h_{\mathbf{v}}
,
h_{\mathbf{D}}
,
\rho_{\mathbf{v}}
,
\rho_{\mathbf{D}}
appearing in Equation (
4.17
) all preserve the divisors
E_{i}
, then
X_{H^{\lambda}}
preserves
E_{i}
. It is easy to construct the last three functions with the desired property. However, more care is needed in the construction of
h_{\mathbf{v}}
to ensure that Property
(b)
also holds.

The Hamiltonian flow of
h_{\mathbf{v}}
preserves
E_{i}
if the Hamiltonian flow of
\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}
, the pull back of the Morse function
\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}^{base}
, preserves
E_{i}.
As noted above, the orbits of the perturbed Hamiltonian which lie in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
are in bijection with critical points of
\hat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}^{base}
. Moreover, the degree of the corresponding Hamiltonian orbit is simply
\deg(x_{crit})+2\sum_{i}(1-a_{i})
, where
\deg(x_{crit})
denotes the Morse index of the corresponding critical point. Because we are assuming our pair is log nef, it suffices to construct a Morse function on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
which preserves
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
and such that all critical points which lie in
\mathbf{E}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
have index at least two. The existence of such a function follows from the usual Thom isomorphism in Morse theory— namely, it suffices to assume that near
E_{J}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
the function
\hat{h}
has the form
\sum_{j\in J}G(\rho_{j})+\pi_{J}^{*}\tilde{h}
, where
G
is a function with a maximum at
\rho_{j}=0
and
\tilde{h}
is a function on
E_{J}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
.
∎

Definition 4.6
.

Here we record some variants of the moduli spaces defined in (
2.21
).

•

For pairs of capped orbits
\tilde{x}_{0}:=(x_{0},u_{x_{0}})
,
\tilde{x}_{1}:=(x_{1},u_{x_{1}})\in\mathcal{X}(X;H^{\lambda})
, the moduli space of Floer trajectories
\mathcal{M}(\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1})
is the space of solutions to (
2.21
) satisfying

u\#u_{x_{0}}\simeq u_{x_{1}}

considered up to reparameterization.

•

Similarly, we let
\mathcal{M}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}])
denote the moduli space of solutions in a given relative homology class
[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}]
.

Consider a pair of orbits
x_{0},x_{1}\in(X\setminus\mathbf{E})
and note that elements of
\mathcal{M}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}])
have a well-defined total intersection number

(4.18)

\displaystyle[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}]\cdot E_{j}

with each divisor
E_{j}
. Suppose that we are given a Floer trajectory
u:(C,z)\to X
connecting orbits together with a point
z\in C
such
u(z)\in E_{j}
. Then the identification (
3.12
)(or rather its obvious variant for Floer trajectories) allows one to define a positive intersection multiplicity,
\operatorname{ord}_{j}(z)
of
u
with
E_{j}
at the point
z
. The intersection number (
4.18
) is then the sum of all of these local multiplicities and is hence non-negative. We next turn to defining
\mathbf{E}
-marked Floer moduli spaces
u:(C,\underline{z})\to X
, which are roughly speaking solutions to (
2.22
) where we allow our curves to pass through the divisors
E_{i}
at additional marked points
\underline{z}
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
F
be a finite set and
e_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\cdots,k_{F}\}
a collection of positive integers such that
\sum_{j}e_{j}=|F|
. An
\{e_{j}\}
-labelling function is an assignment
b:F\to\{1,\cdots k_{F}\}
which assigns to
j
exactly
e_{j}
elements of
F
.

For the following definition, we fix two capped orbits
x_{0},x_{1}
in
X\setminus\mathbf{E}
as well as a relative homology class
[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}].
We abbreviate (
4.18
) by
e_{j}
and let

(4.19)

\displaystyle r([u_{x_{0},x_{1}}])=\sum_{j}e_{j}.

We further set
F:=\{1,\cdots,r([u_{x_{0},x_{1}}])\}
,
8
8

8

By convention
F=\emptyset
if
r([u_{x_{0},x_{1}}])=0.
and
b
to denote an
\{e_{j}\}
-labelling function
b:F\to\{1,\cdots k^{\prime}\}
.

Definition 4.8
.

Choose a time dependent almost complex structure
J_{t}
such that each
J_{t}
lies in (
4.13
). Set
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}],b)
to be the moduli space of Floer curves
u:(C,\underline{z}_{f})\to X
(as usual considered up to
\mathbb{R}
-translation) with marked points indexed by
f\in F
such that

u(z_{f})\in E_{b(f)}

with
\operatorname{ord}_{b(f)}(z_{f})=1
(e.g. the intersection is transverse). We also let

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}]):=\bigsqcup_{b}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}],b)

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
x_{0},x_{1}
be two orbits with
\deg(x_{0})=0
,
\deg(x_{1})=1
(hence lie in
X\setminus E
). and let
[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}]
be a relative homology class. Then for a generic choice of
J_{t}
,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}])
is compact.

Proof.

There are essentially two things that might potentially lead the moduli spaces to be non-compact:

Cylindrical breaking:
To rule out cylindrical breaking, we consider two cases. If there is no breaking along
\mathbf{E}
, this follows from standard transversality. If there is breaking in
\mathbf{E}
, there must be a component going from an orbit in
\mathbf{E}
(which has to degree at least two) to an orbit of degree at most one in
X
. Because such a curve cannot lie entirely in
\mathbf{E}
(because the outgoing asymptote lies in
X
), we can still obtain transversality by perturbing
J_{t}
.

Marked points colliding:
Similarly, it is not difficult to see that marked points collide generically in codimension two, which means they do not appear(again assuming
J_{t}
generic) as limits of our one dimensional moduli spaces. ∎

Define a map

(4.20)

\displaystyle\partial_{E}:CF^{0}(X\subset M,H^{\lambda})\to CF^{1}(X\subset M,H^{\lambda})

by the formula

(4.21)

\partial_{CF}(z)=\sum_{x_{0}}\sum_{[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}]}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}])}\frac{1}{r([u_{x_{0},x_{1}}])!}\mu_{u}

where
z\in|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{1}}|
and as before
\mu_{u}
is the isomorphism of orientation lines assigned to each
u
by the theory of coherent orientations. Define

(4.22)

\displaystyle HF_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X\subset M,H^{\lambda}):=\operatorname{ker}(\partial_{E})

to be the kernel of this map. Note that, in the present situation, the signed counts of curves in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}],b)
are manifestly independent of
b
, and hence there is some redundancy in (
4.22
). However, the present formulation will be convenient in the coming sections.

We can similarly define continuation maps between the groups
HF_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X\subset M,H^{m})
. We then define the (degree zero)
\mathbf{E}
-marked symplectic cohomology to be the direct limit:

(4.23)

\displaystyle SH^{0}_{\mathbf{E}}(X):=\varinjlim HF_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X\subset M,H^{m})

Lemma 4.10
.

We have that

(4.24)

\displaystyle HF_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X\subset M,H^{\lambda})\cong HF^{0}(X\subset M,H^{\lambda})

(4.25)

\displaystyle SH^{0}_{\mathbf{E}}(X)\cong SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})

Proof.

As noted above, all of the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}],b)
are identified for different
b
and are in fact isomorphic to
\mathcal{M}(x_{0},x_{1},[u_{x_{0},x_{1}}])
.Thus the differentials and continuation maps are identified and the result follows. ∎

Having defined an
\mathbf{E}
-marked version of Floer cohomology, we are now in position to define
\operatorname{PSS}
moduli spaces with
\mathbf{E}
-markings. First, the requisite combinatorial definitions:

Definition 4.11
.

An
r
-marked PSS tree
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r)}
is a rooted tree with a distinguished leg
l_{0}
and a collection of additional legs indexed by
\{2,\cdots,r+1\}
9
9

9

The reason for this choice of indexing will be apparent in Definition
4.13
.
so that for any vertex
\nu\neq\nu_{root}
,

(4.26)

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}(\nu)|+|\mathbb{L}(\nu)|\geq 3.

Here,
\mathbb{E}(\nu)
denotes the set of internal edges bounding
\nu
and
\mathbb{L}(\nu)
denotes the set of legs bounding
\nu
.

Analogously to Definition
3.11
, we can define bubbled
\operatorname{PSS}
curves with
r+1
-marked points which are modelled on some
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r)}.
If
C
is such a marked PSS solution, we let
\bar{C}
be the domain given by compactifying the thimble domain
S
to
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
. When dealing with marked
\operatorname{PSS}
-solutions, we will use perturbations that depend on the modulus of
\bar{C}
(this will be needed to achieve transverality for stable log
\operatorname{PSS}
-moduli spaces in the presence of sphere bubbles). Given an integer
r\geq 1
, let

\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}\to\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r+2}

denote the universal curve over the moduli space of stable genus zero curves with
r+2
marked points. For the next definition, fix some background
J_{S}
which as usual is surface independent, i.e.
J_{z}
agrees with some fixed almost complex structure
J_{0}
, in some neighborhood
V_{S}
of
z=\infty
. We let
U_{S}
be some open, pre-compact subset of
V_{S}\cap\operatorname{supp}(\beta).

Definition 4.12
.

Equip
TM
with the almost complex structure
J_{0}
. A (
J_{S}
-compatible, for some fixed
r\geq 1
) perturbation datum will be a one-form

(4.27)

\displaystyle\upsilon\in C^{\infty}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}\times M_{S},\Omega_{\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}/\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r+2}}^{0,1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}TM)

which is supported in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}\times U_{S}\times M
and away from marked points and nodes.

We will assume that our perturbation data are coherent with respect to boundary strata in the sense of
[
14
, Section 3]
or
[
78
, Chapter 9]
. Coherent perturbation data exist by a gluing argument similar to Lemma 9.5 of
[
78
]
. For any capped orbit
\tilde{x}_{0}
with
x_{0}\in X\setminus\mathbf{E}
, any
\operatorname{PSS}
solution
u\in\mathcal{M}(\tilde{x}_{0})
has (positive) intersection
[u_{x_{0}}]\cdot E_{j}
with each divisor
E_{j}.
We set

(4.28)

\displaystyle r(\tilde{x}_{0})=\sum_{j}[u_{x_{0}}]\cdot E_{j}

Definition 4.13
.

Let
\tilde{x}_{0}:=(x_{0},[u_{x_{0}}])
be a capped orbit with
x_{0}\in X\setminus\mathbf{E}
and
r(\tilde{x}_{0})\geq 1.
Let
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r(\tilde{x}_{0}))}
be a marked PSS tree. A perturbed bubbled
\operatorname{PSS}
-solution asymptotic to
\tilde{x}_{0}
and modelled on
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r(\tilde{x}_{0}))}
is a domain
C
modelled on
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r(\tilde{x}_{0}))}
together with:

•

A holomorphic map
\bar{C}
onto a fiber
\tau:\bar{C}\hookrightarrow\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}
which:

–

is either 1) an isomorphism or 2) contracts the root vertex and is an isomorphism away from the root vertex.

–

sends the distinguished marked point corresponding to
l_{0}
to
z_{0}
and the marked point corresponding to the output orbit to
z_{1}.

•

A map
\tilde{u}:C\to M_{S}:=S\times M
satisfying

(4.29)

\displaystyle(d\tilde{u})^{0,1}-(\tau,\tilde{u})^{*}\upsilon=0

such that the projection
\pi_{S}\circ\tilde{u}
is a bi-holomorphism at the root vertex and constant for the remaining vertices with
\pi_{S}\circ\tilde{u}(z_{l_{0}})=\{\infty\}
. Here
(d\tilde{u})^{0,1}
is understood with respect to the almost-complex structure (
3.11
).

We denote the remaining marked points on
C
by
z_{q},q\in\{2,\cdots r+1\}.
We now turn to giving the definitions of log versions of the perturbed moduli spaces. The definitions are very similar to those in §
3.3
and we describe the necessary modifications:

Contact functions and log trees:

Let
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
denote the collection of subsets of
\{1,\cdots,k\}\cup\{1,\cdots,k^{\prime}\}.
Given an
r
-marked PSS tree
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r)}
:

•

a stablized depth function is an assignment

I^{\nu}:\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r)})\to\hat{\mathcal{P}},\\
I^{e}:\mathbb{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r)})\to\hat{\mathcal{P}}

such that
I^{\nu_{root}}=\emptyset
and for any
e
which is bounded by vertices
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
,
I^{\nu}\cup I^{\nu^{\prime}}=I^{e}.

•

a contact function is a map

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(-):\vec{\mathbb{E}}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r)})\to\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}}

such that for any pair of neighboring vertices
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
(
3.22
) holds and the support
|\mathbf{v}(e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}})|\subset I^{e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}}
(here we mean the depth function of the edge underlying the oriented edge).

Definition 4.14
.

(Compare Definition
3.15
) An r-marked log
\operatorname{PSS}
tree
\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}=(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r)},I^{\bullet},\mathbf{v}(-))
is a triple consisting of

•

an
r
-marked
\operatorname{PSS}
tree
\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{(r)}
so that if we remove all of the legs attached to
\nu_{root}
(including
\nu_{root}
itself), the result is connected.

•

a depth function
I^{\nu},I^{e}.

•

and a contact function
\mathbf{v}(-)
.

Floer data:

We choose our almost complex structures
J_{S}
as follows:

•

for each

,
J_{z}\in\mathcal{J}(\bar{X}^{\vec{\epsilon}},\mathbf{E})
(recall (

)).

•

J_{z}=J_{0}
is surface independent (i.e.
J_{z}

J_{0}
) in some neighborhood

of

for some

For log moduli spaces, we restrict to
\upsilon
which are compatible with the regularization in the sense of
[
20
, Definition 3.7]
. This compatibility is easiest to explain using a differential-geometric version of the logarithmic tangent bundle
TM(-\operatorname{log}(\mathbf{D}))
, which depends on a choice of a regularization (see
[
20
, Eq. (2.11)]
). For our purposes, we only need recall that if
\pi_{I}:U_{I}\to D_{I}
is a regularizing tubular neighborhood, then over
U_{I}^{o}:=\pi_{I}^{-1}(D_{I}\setminus\cup_{j\notin I}D_{j})
, we have that there is a decomposition

(4.31)

\displaystyle TM(-\operatorname{log}(\mathbf{D}))_{|U_{I}^{o}}\cong\pi_{I}^{*}(T_{D_{I}})\oplus\mathbb{C}^{I}

As one would expect,
TM(-\operatorname{log}(\mathbf{D}))
equipped with a canonical map

TM(-\operatorname{log}(\mathbf{D}))\to TM

which is an isomorphism away from
\mathbf{D}.
To describe this map over
U_{I}^{o}
, we identify (using the regularization) points
\hat{x}\in U_{I}^{o}
with tuples
(x,(\mathbf{n}_{i})_{i\in I}
where
x
is a point in
D_{I}
and
(\mathbf{n}_{i})_{i\in I}\in ND_{i,x}
is a tuple of normal vectors. We then set:

(v_{D_{I}},(\mathbf{c}_{i})_{i\in I})_{(x,(\mathbf{n}_{i})_{i\in I})}\to(v_{D_{I}},\sum_{i}c_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i})

where
v_{D_{I}}
denotes a tangent vector in
TD_{I,x}
and the right hand side uses the decomposition (
3.20
) given by the regularizing connections.

Definition 4.15
.

A perturbing one-form
\upsilon
is compatible with the regularization if:

•

\upsilon
lifts to a section

\upsilon_{log}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}\times M_{S},\Omega_{\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}/\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r+2}}^{0,1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}TM(-\operatorname{log}(\mathbf{D}))

With respect to the decomposition, (
4.31
),

\upsilon:=\pi_{I}^{*}(\upsilon_{I})\oplus\pi_{I}^{*}((\upsilon_{F,i})_{i\in I})

where in the horizontal component,
\upsilon_{I}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}\times M_{S},\Omega_{\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}/\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r+2}}^{0,1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}TD_{I})
is a suitable
(0,1)
form on
D_{I}
, and on the vertical component,
(\upsilon_{F,i})_{i\in I}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}\times M_{S},\Omega_{\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{0,r+2}/\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{0,r+2}}^{0,1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}^{I})
is a tuple of one forms on
\mathbb{C}.

Let
u:\mathbb{CP}^{1}\to M
be a component of a bubbled perturbed PSS solution which attaches at
\{\infty\}\in\mathbb{C}P^{1}\setminus\{0\}
. As in (
3.4
), if
u\subset D_{i}
, then
D_{u}(\bar{\partial}-\upsilon)
descends to a modified
\bar{\partial}
-operator:

(4.32)

\displaystyle D_{u}^{ND_{i}}(\bar{\partial}-\upsilon):\Gamma(\Sigma,u^{*}ND_{i})\to\Gamma(\Sigma,\Omega_{\Sigma,\mathfrak{j}}^{0,1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}u^{*}ND_{i})

which can be used to be define meromorphic sections.

Pre-log and log moduli spaces:

Let
b
be a labelling
b:\{2,\cdots,r+1\}\to\{1,\cdots,k^{\prime}\}.
Because we have an induced
\bar{\partial}
-operator on the normal bundle of curves contained in divisor strata (
4.32
), we can define
\mathbf{E}
-marked pre-log maps

(C_{\nu},u_{\nu},[\zeta]_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)

by directly mimicking Definition
3.17
. To this end, let
\mathbf{v}_{E_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}}
denote the vector:

\mathbf{v}_{E_{j}}=:\vec{0}\times\underbracket{(0,\cdots,1,\cdots 0)}_{\text{1 in j-th position and 0 elsewhere}}

In addiiton to the obvious modifications of Definition
3.17
, we additionally require that at
z_{q}
,
q\in\{2,\cdots,r+1\}
,
u(z_{q})\in E_{b(q)}
and

\operatorname{ord}_{u}(z_{q})=\mathbf{v}_{E_{b(q)}}.

Just as in (
3.33
), after choosing an arbitrary orientation
\tilde{e}:=e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
on each edge
e
, there is a well-defined map

(4.33)

\displaystyle ob_{\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}}:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)\to\mathcal{G}(\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}})

Definition 4.16
.

(Compare Definition
3.20
) A
\mathbf{E}
-marked log map (modelled on
\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}
) consists of a pre-log map

(C_{\nu},u_{\nu},[\zeta]_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)

satisfying the following additional conditions:

(i)

There exists functions
\mathbf{v}_{\nu}:\mathbf{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{r(\tilde{x}_{0})})\to\mathbb{R}^{k+k^{\prime}},\lambda_{e}:\mathbf{E}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS}^{r(\tilde{x}_{0})})\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that

(a)

\mathbf{v}_{\nu}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{I^{\nu}}\times\{0\}^{k+k^{\prime}-|I^{\nu}|}

(b)

For every pair of vertices
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
connected by an (oriented) edge
e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
,

\mathbf{v}_{\nu}-\mathbf{v}_{\nu^{\prime}}=\lambda_{e}(e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}})\mathbf{v}(e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}).

(ii)

We have that

(4.34)

\displaystyle ob_{\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}}(u)=[1]\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}})

We let
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)
denote the moduli space of
\mathbf{E}
-marked log maps (modelled on
\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}
) up to isomorphism.

When
\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}
has a single vertex we use the notation,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0},b):=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b).
Finally, we set

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0}):=\bigsqcup_{b}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0},b)

The important point about
\mathbf{E}
-marked moduli spaces is that all of the spherical components must be stable, meaning they have at least three special points. Hence, we can take the additional perturbations
\upsilon
to be non-trivial over these components providing enough additional flexibility to regularize them(as noted above, the Floer data over the component
u_{root}
the
\operatorname{PSS}
component itself) — in the present situation meaning that these moduli spaces are in fact generically empty when there are sphere bubbles. The precise statement is the following:

Lemma 4.17
.

For generic
(J_{S},\upsilon)
, the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)
are empty when
\operatorname{deg}(x_{0})\leq 1
and the graph underlying
\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}
has more than a single vertex.

Proof.

This is proved as in
[
20
, Theorem 1.5]
whose proof is in turn broadly similar to
[
73
, Proposition 3.16]
and
[
61
, Theorem 6.2.6]
. Indeed, the arguments from these latter two references adapt easily to prove that a certain universal moduli space of (parameterized) pre-log maps

\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\operatorname{univ}}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)

is a Banach manifold. The new feature in the present setting is that one must prove that the obstruction map

ob_{\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}}:\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\operatorname{univ}}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)\to\mathcal{G}(\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}})

can be made transverse as well, meaning that for any
u=(C_{\nu},u_{\nu},[\zeta]_{\nu})_{\nu\in\mathbb{V}(\underline{\Gamma}_{PSS})}
,

(4.35)

\displaystyle\operatorname{D}ob_{\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}}:T_{u}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\operatorname{univ}}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)\to T_{1}\mathcal{G}(\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}})

is surjective. This is a consequence of the following argument from
[
20
, Claim 2, Proof of Theorem 1.5]
which we summarize. Choose representatives
\zeta_{\nu}
for the meromorphic sections and coordinate charts near all of the marked points. Next, fix an edge
e
and let
\tilde{e}:=e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
be the orientation chosen on this edge. (In the case that one of the vertices is
\nu_{root}
, we will assume that
\nu_{root}=\nu^{\prime}
to make the argument written below apply uniformly.) For every
i\in I^{e}
, we will show that
[1_{e,i}]\in T_{1}\mathcal{G}(\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}})
is the image of
(
4.35
)
. There are two cases to consider:

i\in I^{\nu}
: (c.f.
[
20
, Claim 2, Case (i) in proof of Theorem 1.5]
)Let
\mathbb{C}P^{1}_{\nu}
denote the domain of
u_{\nu}
and let
\beta:\mathbb{C}P^{1}_{\nu}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function supported in a neighborhood of
z_{e_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}}
and which is constant in a smaller neighborhood of this marked point. We choose
\beta
so that

\frac{d}{dt}(e^{t\beta(z_{\tilde{e}})}\eta_{e,i})_{|t=0}=1

where
\eta_{e,i}
is defined in (
3.31
). Let
f_{t}
be the path in
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\operatorname{univ}}^{\operatorname{plog}}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)
given by deforming
\zeta_{i,\nu}
to
e^{t\beta}\zeta_{i,\nu}
(and suitably deforming the
\bar{\partial}
operator on
ND_{i}
). Then

\frac{d}{dt}[ob_{\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}}}\circ f_{t}]_{|t=0}=[1_{e,i}]

i\notin I^{\nu}
: (c.f.
[
20
, Claim 2, Case (ii) in proof of Theorem 1.5]
) In this case,
\eta_{i,z_{\tilde{e}}}
is defined by the expansion (
3.3
). Take a cut-off function
\beta
as above, and by a suitable cut-off constuction, one can deform
u_{\nu}
(with
\upsilon
non-trivial) so that the expansion becomes:

(4.36)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{C}}\circ(u_{\nu})|_{\Delta}=e^{t\beta}\eta_{i,z_{\tilde{e}}}z^{\operatorname{ord}_{i}}+O(|z|^{\operatorname{ord}_{i}+1})

from which it follows that
[1_{e,i}]
is in the image of the differential in this case as well.

∎

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\tilde{x}_{0}
be a capped orbit such that
[u_{x_{0}}]\cdot\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{v}
. Assume
\lambda_{m}>\mathbf{v}
,
(J_{S},\upsilon)
is sufficiently generic so that Lemma
4.17
holds and
||\upsilon||
is sufficiently small:

•

If
\operatorname{deg}(x_{0})=0,
the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0})
are compact.

•

If
\operatorname{deg}(x_{0})=1
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0})
admits a compactification whose boundary strata
\partial\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0})
are described as follows:

(4.37)

\displaystyle\partial\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0}):=\bigsqcup_{(y,[u_{x_{0},y}])}\bigsqcup_{(\mathfrak{p},b_{0},b_{1})}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{y},b_{0})\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(x_{0},y,[u_{x_{0},y}],b_{1})

where:

–

y
is an orbit with
\operatorname{deg}(y)=0
,
[u_{x_{0},y}]
is a relative homology class and
\tilde{y}:=(y,[u_{y}])
is a capping disc such that
(-[u_{y}])\#[u_{x_{0},y}]=-[u_{x_{0}}].

–

\mathfrak{p}
is a partition of
\{2,\cdots,r(\tilde{x}_{0})+1\}
into two sets
S_{0},S_{1}
which are identified by the induced ordering with
S_{0}\cong\{2,\cdots,r(\tilde{y}_{0})+1\}
and
S_{1}\cong\{1,\cdots,r([u_{x_{0},y}])\}.

–

b_{0}:S_{0}\to\{1,\cdots,k^{\prime}\}
,
b_{1}:S_{1}\to\{1,\cdots,k^{\prime}\}
are labellings of the parititoned sets.

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of Theorem
3.26
and we only reprise the main steps. First, if
||\upsilon||
sufficiently small, a similar energy argument prevents breaking along Hamiltonian orbits in
\mathbf{D}
. Then, as before, log Gromov-compactness (the adaptation to
\upsilon
-perturbed moduli spaces is carried out in
[
20
, Section 3.3]
) implies that sphere bubbles which attach at points in
S
other than
z=\{\infty\}
must be constant. Unlike in the unstablized case, these constant configurations can arise(when marked points collide). However, where they do collide, the PSS solution must intersect (components of)
\mathbf{E}
with multiplicity
>1
which again happens in codimension two (and hence in our context does not occur generically). Finally, in view of Definition
4.4
, the remaining sphere bubble configurations (with bubbles attaching at
z=\{\infty\}
) will all be stable and hence potential limits belong to some
\mathbf{E}
-marked moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\Gamma^{\operatorname{stab}},\tilde{x}_{0},b)
. These limits can be excluded using Lemma
4.17
. ∎

Definition 4.19
.

Suppose
\mathbf{v}
,
\tilde{x}_{0}
,
(J_{S},\upsilon)
are as in Lemma
4.18
. Let
c
be a component of
D_{|\mathbf{v}|}
. Then as before, we define
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0},c)
to be the
\mathbf{E}
-marked log PSS maps such that

u(z_{0})\in D_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}.

4.3.
The splitting map

Suppose temporarily that
\operatorname{char}({\mathbf{k}})=0.
For any
\mathbf{v}
, choose
m
with
\lambda_{m}>\mathbf{v}
as well as a generic
(J_{S},\upsilon)
(so that Lemma
4.18
holds) and define
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{m}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})\in CF_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X\subset M,H^{m})

by the rule

(4.38)

\displaystyle\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{m}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})=\sum_{\tilde{x}_{0}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0},c)}\frac{1}{r(\tilde{x}_{0})!}\mu_{u}

where
\mu_{u}\in|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{0}}|
is defined by (
4.10
) and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0},c)
is defined in Definition
4.19
.

Lemma 4.20
.

If
\lambda_{m}>\mathbf{v}
and
(J_{S},\upsilon)
chosen as in Lemma
4.18
,
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{m}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})
defines a cohomology class.

Proof.

This is essentially a consequence of Lemmas
4.17
,
4.18
together with the observation that in the strata arising in (
4.37
), there are
\frac{r(\tilde{x}_{0})!}{(r(\tilde{y})!)(r(u_{x_{0},y})!)}
partitions
\mathfrak{p}
which distribute the marked points between the two components. Thus the boundary of the sum of rational chains of the form
\frac{1}{r(\tilde{x}_{0})!}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0})
is exactly the composition
\partial\circ\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{m}.
∎

The cohomology class
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{m}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})\in HF_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X\subset M,H^{m})
does not vary in one parameter families of (generic)
(J_{S},\upsilon).
Moreover as before, we have that for any
m^{\prime}>m
,

(4.39)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{m,m^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{m}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})=\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{m^{\prime}}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})

We therefore obtain a well-defined map:

\operatorname{PSS}_{log}:\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}\to SH^{0}_{\mathbf{E}}(X)

which we can compose with (
4.25
) to obtain a map

(4.40)

\displaystyle\operatorname{PSS}_{log}:\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}\in B(M,\mathbf{D})}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}\to SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})

Theorem 4.21
.

For any log nef pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
, the map (
4.40
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For any
I\subset\{1,\cdots,k\}
, let
\mathbf{v}_{I}=(v_{I,i})
denote the vector defined by the rule:

(4.41)

\displaystyle v_{I,i}=\begin{cases}1\text{ if }i\in I\\
0\text{ if }i\notin I\end{cases}

For any
I
such that
\mathbf{v}_{I}\in B(M,\mathbf{D})
and any component
D_{I,c}
of
D_{I}
, consider the corresponding element
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v}_{I,i},c)})
which we can view lying in the
q^{0}
part of
SC^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
(recall (
4.5
)). In the
E_{1}
page
H^{*}(\oplus_{p}F^{p}SC^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})/F^{p+1}SC^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}}))
, this becomes the element
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v}_{I,i},c)})
and it follows that these elements survive to the
E_{\infty}
page. As the elements
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v}_{I,i},c)})
generate the degree zero part of the spectral sequence multiplicatively, it follows that the spectral sequence degenerates in degree 0 i.e. that we have
E_{1}^{p,-p}\cong E_{\infty}^{p,-p}.
Chasing through the definitions, it is not difficult to see that we have a commutative square:

(4.42)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{p}F^{p}SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})/F^{p+1}SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{p}E_{1}^{p,-p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{p}E_{\infty}^{p,-p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{gr}_{F}\operatorname{PSS}_{log}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where in the upper horizontal arrow we have identified
\operatorname{gr}_{F}(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})
with
\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}
because the filtration is canonically split. The map
\operatorname{gr}_{F}\operatorname{PSS}_{log}
is an isomorphism because all of the other maps in the diagram are isomorphisms. It follows that (
4.40
) is an isomorphism as well. ∎

Corollary 4.22
.

For an arbitrary field
{\mathbf{k}}
(of any characteristic) there is a canonical isomorphism of rings

(4.43)

\displaystyle gr_{F_{w}}SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})\cong\mathcal{SR}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\Delta(\mathbf{D})_{(0)})

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{v}_{I}
be the vector from (
4.41
). Choose some slope
\lambda_{m}
so that
\lambda_{m}>w(\mathbf{v}_{I})
for all
\mathbf{v}_{I}\in B(M,\mathbf{D})
. By Theorem
4.21
, the Floer differential vanishes on
CF^{0}(X;H^{m})
in characterstic zero. By comparing ranks, it follows that the Floer differential vanishes over the field
{\mathbf{k}}.
Thus, again over
{\mathbf{k}}
, the classes
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v}_{I},c)})
on the
E_{1}
survive to the
E_{\infty}
page. Again because the elements
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v}_{I},c)})
generate the degree zero part of the spectral sequence multiplicatively, we have that
E_{1}^{p,-p}\cong E_{\infty}^{p,-p}.
We conclude that there is an isomorphism of the form (
4.43
). ∎

Remark 4.23
.

•

We do not address the question of whether our constructions are independent of
\mathbf{E}.

•

In view of Corollary
4.22
, one suspects that the map (
4.40
) can be constructed over fields of arbitrary characteristic(or in fact over
\mathbb{Z}
). This seems to involve being able to regularize the moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0})
directly by perturbing almost complex structures because typically virtual methods requiring working with fields of characteristic zero (even our fairly elementary approach involves dividing by
r(\tilde{x}_{0})!
). Closely related questions are discussed in
[
20
, Section 4.5.]
.

Theorem implies the following finiteness result for
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
, which generalizes
[
27
]
:

Lemma 4.24
.

For any log Calabi-Yau pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
:

(1)

SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
is a finitely generated
{\mathbf{k}}
-algebra.

(2)

SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
is a finitely generated module over
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
(and hence is finitely generated as a graded
{\mathbf{k}}
-algebra).

Proof.

Claim (1):
Let
F_{w}SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
(depending as before on the line bundle
\mathcal{L}
over
M
) be the filtration on
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
. We have by Theorem
4.25
, that the associated graded algebra
\operatorname{gr}_{F}SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
is generated by the elements
\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v}_{I},c)})
where
\mathbf{v}_{I}
are the vectors from (
4.41
) and
D_{I,c}
are the irreducible components of
D_{I}
. Because this filtration induces the discrete topology on
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
, we have by
[
8
, Chapter III,§2]
that lifts of these elements generate
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
as well.

Claim (2):
(Compare with the proof of
[
27
, Theorem 5.30]
.) Theorem 1.1 of
[
27
]
identifies the full
E_{1}
page of the spectral sequence induced from the
F_{w}
filtration with a certain ring
H^{*}_{log}(M,\mathbf{D})
.
H^{*}_{log}(M,\mathbf{D})
is module finite over
\operatorname{gr}_{F}SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
and so finite generation of
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
again follows from
[
8
, Chapter III, §2]
.
∎

4.3.1.
\Lambda
-twisted coefficients

This section is quite similar to
[
66
, Section 8]
. Fix a ground field
{\mathbf{k}}
, let
\hat{H}_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
be
H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
divided by torsion

(4.44)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z}):=H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})/H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})_{\operatorname{tor}},

and let
\Lambda={\mathbf{k}}[\hat{H}_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})]
be the group ring on
\hat{H}_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z}).
Our goal is to prove a version of Theorem
1.2
for an enhanced version of symplectic cohomology,
SH^{*}(X,\underline{\Lambda})
, which is linear over
\Lambda
and where one keeps track of the homology class of Floer curves after attaching suitable capping discs (similar constructions are sometimes referred to as “
\Lambda
-twisted coefficients” in the Floer theory literature).
SH^{*}(X,\underline{\Lambda})
is thus an invariant of the pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
rather than
X
itself, but one can recover the usual symplectic cohomology ring
SH^{*}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
discussed above by base changing to the augmentation ideal. As in
[
27
]
, we equip (the co-chain complex computing)
SH^{*}(X,\underline{\Lambda})
with an integer valued version of the action filtration,
F_{w}SH^{*}(X,\underline{\Lambda}).

It should be noted that this twisted story is not needed for proving Theorem
1.1
and is thus not relevant to the main “thread” of this paper (in particular, note that we don’t know of a categorical version of
\Lambda
-twisting). Nevertheless, it is potentially interesting for two reasons. First, taking
\operatorname{Spec}(SH^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda}))
over
\operatorname{Spec}(\Lambda)
gives a family of varieties with potential modular interpretations
[
38
]
(one could also possibly contemplate things like connections and periods for this family). Second, the twisted coefficients are the natural context for making comparisons with algebro-geometric constructions (
[
69
]
). As it involves very little extra work, we lay some groundwork for these potential future applications here.

The
\Lambda
-twisted Floer complex (first of some fixed admissible
H^{\lambda}
) is defined as follows:

(4.45)

\displaystyle CF_{\Lambda}^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda}):=\bigoplus_{\tilde{x}}{\mathbf{k}}\cdot\langle\tilde{x}\rangle

For any orbit, the set of capping discs is a torsor under
\hat{H}_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
, giving
CF_{\Lambda}^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})
the structure of an
\Lambda
-module. In fact, observe that for any
\mathbf{v}\in B(M,\mathbf{D}),
the orbits
x\subset U_{\mathbf{v}}
lie entirely in the fiber of the projection
U_{|\mathbf{v}|}\to D_{|\mathbf{v}|}
and hence have a canonical fiberwise capping disc
F_{x}\subset M
. Using this disc, we may view the Novikov enhanced Floer cochains as being free
\Lambda
-modules

CF_{\Lambda}^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda}):=\bigoplus_{x}\Lambda\cdot\langle[x,F_{x}]\rangle

We have

(4.46)

\displaystyle CF_{\Lambda}^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})\otimes_{\Lambda}{\mathbf{k}}\cong CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})

where the
\Lambda
module structure on
{\mathbf{k}}
is induced from the augmentation homomorphism
\epsilon_{\operatorname{aug}}:\Lambda\to{\mathbf{k}}.

The differential again counts index one Floer trajectories in
\mathcal{M}(\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1})
(recall (
4.6
)):

(4.47)

\partial_{CF}(\langle\tilde{x}_{1}\rangle)=\sum_{\tilde{x}_{0}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}(\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1})}\mu_{u}\langle\tilde{x}_{0}\rangle

where
\mu_{u}=\pm 1
is again the sign associated to
u\in\mathcal{M}(\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1})
