. There are also relative versions of these constructions in degree zero, giving rise to cochain groups
CF_{\Lambda,\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda}).
Using continuation maps, we can define
SH^{*}(X,\underline{\Lambda})
and in degree zero the relative version
SH_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda})\cong SH^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda}).
Just as before, we can define a class (in some fixed
CF_{\Lambda,\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X\subset M;H^{\lambda})
) by the formula

(4.48)

\displaystyle\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{m}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)})=\sum_{\tilde{x}_{0}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{v},\tilde{x}_{0})}\frac{1}{r(\tilde{x}_{0})!}\mu_{u}<\tilde{x}_{0}>

which gives rise to a map

(4.49)

\displaystyle\operatorname{PSS}_{log}:\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}}\Lambda\cdot\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}\to SH_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda})\cong SH^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda})

We are finally in a position to prove the twisted version of Theorem
1.2
mentioned in the introduction:

Theorem 4.25
.

For any log nef pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
, the map (
4.49
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The proof is the same as Theorem
4.21
, with appropriate modifications that we spell out. The filtration
F_{w}
obviously lifts to the
\Lambda
-twisted Floer complexes giving rise to a spectral sequence. Theorem
4.2
adapts without change to give rise to
a ring isomorphism:

\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}:(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}}\Lambda\cdot\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)},\ast_{\operatorname{SR}})\cong E_{1}^{p,-p}

which as before implies the collapse of the spectral sequence. The analogue of (
4.42
) is then given by

(4.50)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}}\Lambda\cdot\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{p}F^{p}SH^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda})/F^{p+1}SH^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{p}E_{1}^{p,-p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{p}E_{\infty}^{p,-p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{gr}_{F}\operatorname{PSS}_{log}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PSS}_{log}^{low}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

as before, the map
\operatorname{gr}_{F}\operatorname{PSS}_{log}
is an isomorphism because all of the other maps in the diagram are isomorphisms thereby implying that (
4.49
) is an isomorphism as well.
∎

By (
4.46
) and flatness, we have

(4.51)

\displaystyle SH^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda})\otimes_{\Lambda}{\mathbf{k}}\cong SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})

In view of (
4.51
), Lemma
4.24
implies that for any log Calabi-Yau pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
,
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
is also finitely generated. Parallel to Corollary
4.22
and Lemma
4.24
, we have:

Corollary 4.26
.

For an arbitrary field
{\mathbf{k}}
(of any characteristic) there is a canonical isomorphism of rings

(4.52)

\displaystyle gr_{F_{w}}SH^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda})\cong\mathcal{SR}_{\Lambda}(\Delta(\mathbf{D})_{(0)})

Proof.

The proof is identical to Corollary
4.22
and is omitted. ∎

Lemma 4.27
.

For any log Calabi-Yau pair
(M,\mathbf{D})
:

(1)

SH^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda})
is a finitely generated
\Lambda
-algebra.

(2)

SH^{*}(X,\underline{\Lambda})
is a finitely generated module over
SH^{0}(X,\underline{\Lambda})
(and hence is finitely generated as a graded
\Lambda
-algebra).

Proof.

The proof is identical to Lemma
4.24

and is omitted. ∎

4.4.
\mathcal{W}(X)
is semi-affine

Note:
For the remainder of this article, all pairs
(M,\mathbf{D})
will be assumed to be Calabi-Yau pairs. The notation in this section will be a bit heavier than in previous sections, so we begin by listing the main notations which will be used throughout the section— the reader shouldd refer back to this list as needed.

Fix some sufficiently small
\epsilon_{1}
and recall the notations
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}},\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}
from (
2.9
). Fix a specific rounding
\Sigma_{\vec{\epsilon}}
(:=
\partial\bar{X}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
) of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
.

Further notations:

•

For any
\epsilon
such that
\epsilon^{2}\leq\operatorname{inf}_{i}\frac{2\pi\epsilon_{0}^{2}}{\kappa_{i}},
we set
U_{i,\epsilon}
to be the
region where
\frac{\rho_{i}}{\kappa_{i}/2\pi}\leq\epsilon^{2}.
For
I\subset\{1,\cdots,k\},
define
U_{I,\epsilon}
to be

(4.53)

\displaystyle U_{I,\epsilon}:=\cap_{i\in I}U_{i,\epsilon}.

•

Let
(\epsilon^{\operatorname{pert}}_{i})_{i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}}
be a
k
-tuple of real numbers with norm
||\epsilon^{\operatorname{pert}}_{i}||<<\epsilon_{1}
. Define the “
(\epsilon^{\operatorname{pert}}_{i})
-skew manifold with corners”

(4.54)

\displaystyle\widehat{X}_{(\epsilon^{\operatorname{pert}}_{i})}:=M\setminus(\cup_{i}U_{i,\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon^{\operatorname{pert}}_{i}})

•

Let
\delta^{\operatorname{max}}
be a small positive real number and let
\vec{\delta}_{I}=(\vec{\delta}_{I,i})_{i\in I}
be an
I
-tuple (for some
I\subset\{1,\cdots,k\}
) of real numbers such that
|\vec{\delta}_{I,i}|\leq\delta^{\operatorname{max}}.
Let
S_{I,\epsilon_{1},\vec{\delta}_{I}}
denote the open submanifolds

(4.55)

\displaystyle S_{I,\epsilon_{1},\vec{\delta}_{I}}:=(\cap_{i\in I}\{x\in X:2\pi\frac{\rho_{i}}{\kappa_{i}}=(\epsilon_{1}+\vec{\delta}_{I})^{2}\})\setminus(\cup_{j\notin I}\{2\pi\frac{\rho_{i}}{\kappa_{i}}\geq\epsilon_{1}^{2}\})

When
\vec{\delta}_{I,i}=\vec{0},
we will use the simpler notation
S_{I,\epsilon_{1}}
for
S_{I,\epsilon_{1},\vec{0}}.

•

For any non-empty subset
I
, set
US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}
be the union of all
S_{I,\epsilon_{1},\vec{\delta}_{I}}
over all
\vec{\delta}_{I},

(4.56)

\displaystyle US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}:=\cup_{\vec{\delta}_{I}}S_{I,\epsilon_{1},\vec{\delta}_{I}}

•

Finally, we have a tubular neighborhood of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
given by

(4.57)

\displaystyle U\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}:=\cup_{I}US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}

(Here
I
ranges over all non-empty subsets.) In our main argument, we will need to consider Hamiltonian functions which are not (small perturbations of) functions of a single Liouville coordinate. We will therefore work with a slight variation of the functions from Definition
2.4
.

Definition 4.28
.

A function
g_{L}
will be called homogeneous if:

(1)

on each subset
U_{I,\epsilon_{1}+\delta^{\operatorname{max}}}\setminus(\cup_{j\notin I}U_{j,\epsilon_{1}+\delta^{\operatorname{max}}}),

g_{L}=g_{L}(\rho_{i})
is a function of the variables
\rho_{i}
with
i\in I
.

(2)

there exists
K_{\vec{\epsilon}}
with
\operatorname{min}_{\mathbf{D}}R^{\vec{\epsilon}}>K_{\vec{\epsilon}}>1
so that when
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}\geq K^{\vec{\epsilon}}
,
g_{L}\geq 0
and

(4.58)

\displaystyle\theta(X_{g_{L}})=g_{L}+\lambda

for some
\lambda>0.

The main example of homogeneous functions (other than Liouville admissible functions) that we will need is the following. Let
(\epsilon^{\operatorname{pert}}_{i})
be a small vector and let
\tilde{X}_{0},\tilde{X}_{1}
be two different
C^{0}
-close roundings of
\widehat{X}_{(\epsilon^{\operatorname{pert}}_{i})}
. Let
g_{0,L}
be Liouville admissible with respect to
\tilde{X}_{0}
and
g_{1,L}
Liouville admissible with respect to
\tilde{X}_{1}
. Then

(4.59)

\displaystyle g_{0,L}+g_{1,L}

is also homogeneous (even though it is not a function of any Liouville coordinate). The ability to take sums of functions with respect to two different Liouville coordinates will be very useful when considering the module action of
SH^{*}(X)
on
WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
.

As usual, given two Lagrangian branes
L_{0},L_{1}
, we will consider homogeneous functions such that each Hamiltonian
g_{L}
have no chords at infinity. Let
G_{L}
be a small perturbation of
g_{L}
which is supported near the chords and let
J_{t}
be an almost complex structure which is of contact type near the level set where
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}=K^{\vec{\epsilon}}
. Then for
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}>K^{\vec{\epsilon}},
we have that:

(4.60)

\displaystyle dR^{\vec{\epsilon}}(X_{G_{L}})=0

To check (
4.60
), note that
G_{L}=g_{L}
is a function of the radial coordinates
\rho_{i}
so
X_{G_{L}}
is a linear combination of the angular vector fields. Then using (
4.58
), (
4.60
), the integrated maximum principle argument carries over to show that Floer strips for the pair
(G_{L},J_{t})
have to lie below in the region
X\setminus\{R^{\vec{\epsilon}}\geq K^{\vec{\epsilon}}\}
(for similar arguments see
[
27
, §2.3]
). Thus, there are well-defined Floer cochain complexes
CF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};G_{L})
and cohomology groups
HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};G_{L})
.

Suppose
L_{0},L_{1}
are cylindrical and separated outside of
\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}
. We next recall a key proposition from
[
64
]
which will enable us to Hamiltonian isotope any pair of
L_{0},L_{1}
into a normal form where Hamiltonian chords (for homogeneous functions satisfying a few extra conditions) between them can be easily computed. To state this proposition requires the following preliminary definition.

Definition 4.29
.

Let
y
be a point of
U_{I}
and let
F_{y}
denote the fiber of the projection
\pi_{I}:U_{I}\to D_{I}
which passes through
y.
A Lagrangian submanifold
L\subset X
is fiber radial (with respect to
\pi_{I}
) near
y
if there is a neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{y}
of
y
such that in any unitary trivialization of the fiber
F_{y}\cong U\subset\mathbb{C}^{|I|}
,

(4.61)

\displaystyle L\cap\mathcal{N}_{y}\cap F_{y}=\cap_{i\in I}\{\phi_{i}=\alpha_{i}\}\cap\mathcal{N}_{y}.

for some angles
\alpha_{i}.

Proposition 4.30
.

[
64
, Lemma 5.4]
There is Hamiltonian diffeomorphism
\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}:X\to X
which is supported in
U\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
such that for each
I\subset\{1,\cdots,k\}
:

•

\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{0}),\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{1})
are transverse to
S_{I,\epsilon_{1}}
and the Lagrangian immersions
i_{0,I}:=(\pi_{I})_{|\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{0})\cap S_{I,\epsilon_{1}}}
and
i_{1,I}:=(\pi_{I})_{|\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{1})\cap S_{I,\epsilon_{1}}}
are transverse to each other and also the intersection points between
i_{0,I}\cap i_{1,I}
are isolated if
|I|<n
.

•

If
y\in\operatorname{Im}(i_{0,I})\cap\operatorname{Im}(i_{1,I})
then
\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{0})
(respectively
\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{1})
) is fiber radial (with respect to
\pi_{I}
) near each point of
\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{0})\cap\pi_{I}^{-1}(y)\cap S_{I,\epsilon_{1}}
(respectively
\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{1})\cap\pi_{I}^{-1}(y)\cap S_{I,\epsilon_{1}}
).

We will use the simpler notation
\tilde{L}_{0}:=\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{0})
and
\tilde{L}_{1}:=\phi_{\operatorname{ham}}(L_{1}).
Note that the Lagrangians
\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1}
are still exact Lagrangians with primitives
f_{\tilde{L}_{i}}
such that
df_{\tilde{L}_{i}}=\theta_{|\tilde{L}_{i}}.
After making a further small perturbation, we can and will also assume that
\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1}
meet transversely in
\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}
as well. For any
I
, we also set

\mathcal{X}_{I}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1}):=\operatorname{Im}(i_{0,I})\cap\operatorname{Im}(i_{1,I})

Lemma 4.31
.

Set
i_{0,I,\vec{\delta}_{I}}:=(\pi_{I})_{|\tilde{L}_{0}\cap S_{I,\epsilon_{1},\vec{\delta}_{I}}}
and
i_{1,I,\vec{\delta}_{I}}:=(\pi_{I})_{|\tilde{L}_{1})\cap S_{I,\epsilon_{1},\vec{\delta}_{I}}}.
For
\delta^{\operatorname{max}}
sufficiently small, we have that:

(4.62)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{I}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})=\operatorname{Im}(i_{0,I,\vec{\delta}_{I}})\cap\operatorname{Im}(i_{1,I,\vec{\delta}_{I}})

Proof.

If
|I|=n
, the assertion is trivial. Otherwise, because the Lagrangians are fiber radial, we have an inclusion
\mathcal{X}_{I}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1}))\subset\operatorname{Im}(i_{0,I,\vec{\delta}_{I}})\cap\operatorname{Im}(i_{1,I,\vec{\delta}_{I}})
provided
\delta^{\operatorname{max}}
is sufficiently small. The opposite inclusion follows from the fact that for
\vec{\delta}_{I}
near zero, the intersections are transverse and isolated and as
\vec{\delta}_{I}\to 0
, converge (after possibly passing to a subsequence) to points in
\mathcal{X}_{I}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})
. ∎

For the remainder of this section, we choose
\delta^{\operatorname{max}}
used in the definition of (
4.55
)-(
4.57
) small enough so that Lemma
4.31
holds. We further assume

(1)

There are no chords of
g_{L}
at infinity.

(2)

all of the non-constant chords of
g_{L}
lie in
U\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
and that all of the chords of the Hamiltonians
g_{L}
are all non-degenerate.

(3)

On
US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}\setminus(\cup_{I^{\prime},I\subset I^{\prime}}US_{I^{\prime},\epsilon_{1}}),

g_{L}=g_{L}(\rho_{i})
is a convex function with
\frac{\partial g_{L}(\rho_{i})}{\partial\rho_{i}}\leq 0
.

Having done this, we can give concrete descriptions of all of the chords in each
US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}
. In view of Lemma

4.31
, any chord in
US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}
lies in a fiber
F_{y}:=\pi_{I}^{-1}(y)
for
y\in\mathcal{X}_{I}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1}).
For any such
y
, set
US_{I,\epsilon_{1},y}=:US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}\cap F_{y}
and
S_{I,\epsilon_{1},y}=:S_{I,\epsilon_{1}}\cap F_{y}.
We have

US_{I,\epsilon_{1},y}\cong[\epsilon_{1}-\delta^{\operatorname{max}},\epsilon_{1}+\delta^{\operatorname{max}}]^{|I|}\times S_{I,\epsilon_{1},y}

Similarly, let
\tilde{L}_{i,\epsilon_{1},y}:=\tilde{L}_{i}\cap S_{I,\epsilon_{1},y}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
(this is a finite collection of points).

(4.63)

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{0}\cap US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}\cap F_{y}=[\epsilon_{1}-\delta^{\operatorname{max}},\epsilon_{1}+\delta^{\operatorname{max}}]^{|I|}\times\tilde{L}_{0,\epsilon_{1},y}

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{1}\cap US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}\cap F_{y}=[\epsilon_{1}-\delta^{\operatorname{max}},\epsilon_{1}+\delta^{\operatorname{max}}]^{|I|}\times\tilde{L}_{1,\epsilon_{1},y}

Hamiltonian chords in
US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}
are determined by:

•

a point
y\in\mathcal{X}_{I}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1}).

•

points
\alpha_{0}\in\tilde{L}_{0,\epsilon_{1},y}
,
\alpha_{1}\in\tilde{L}_{1,\epsilon_{1},y}.

•

a winding vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
with support
|\mathbf{v}|\subseteq I.

Concretely, after choosing a
U(1)^{|I|}
equivariant identification

(4.64)

\displaystyle S_{I,\epsilon_{1},y}\cong T^{|I|},

we can identify
\tilde{L}_{0,\epsilon_{1},y}=\cup_{\ell}(\alpha_{i}^{\ell})
where
(\alpha_{i}^{\ell})
is an
I
-tuple of angles lying in
[0,2\pi)
and
\ell
ranges over
\{1,\cdots,|\tilde{L}_{1,\epsilon_{1},y}|\}
(and similarly
\tilde{L}_{1,\epsilon_{1},y}=\cup_{\ell^{\prime}}(\alpha_{i}^{\ell^{\prime}})
). Fix two points
\alpha_{0}=(\alpha_{0,i})
and
\alpha_{1}=(\alpha_{1,i})
and set

(4.65)

v_{s,i}=\begin{cases}0,\quad\text{if }\alpha_{1,i}<\alpha_{0,i}\\
1,\quad\text{if }\alpha_{1,i}>\alpha_{0,i}.\end{cases}

After passing to the universal cover of
US_{I,\epsilon_{1},y}
, we have a “short chord” which is given by
(\epsilon_{1}+\vec{\delta}_{I})\times\vec{P}\subset[\epsilon_{1}-\delta^{\operatorname{max}},\epsilon_{1}+\delta^{\operatorname{max}}]^{|I|}\times\mathbb{R}^{|I|}
where
\vec{\delta}_{I}
is an appropriate vector in
[0,\delta^{\operatorname{max}}]^{I}
and
\vec{P}
is a straight line connecting
(\alpha_{0,i})
with
(\alpha_{1,i}-2\pi v_{s,i}).
More general chords are similarly determined by the straight-line connecting
(\alpha_{0,i})
with
(\alpha_{0,i}-2\pi(v_{s,i}+v_{i}))
for some winding vector
\mathbf{v}=(v_{i})
with support
|\mathbf{v}|\subseteq I
. Convexity and nondegeneracy imply that for any
\mathbf{v}
, there is at most one chord determined by
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\in\tilde{L}_{1,\epsilon_{1},y}
of winding vector
\mathbf{v}
which we denote by
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
. We also have finitely many chords
x_{y}
corresponding to intersection points
y
in
\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}.

For later use, we record an observation about the actions of these chords. Because the Lagrangians are fiber radial, the primitive
f_{\tilde{L}_{0}}
is constant along each component of
\tilde{L}_{0}\cap US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}\cap F_{y}
(and similarly for
f_{\tilde{L}_{1}}
). After identifying these components with
\alpha_{0}\in\tilde{L}_{0,\epsilon_{1},y}
(respectively
\alpha_{1}\in\tilde{L}_{1,\epsilon_{1},y}
), we denote the resulting value by
f_{\tilde{L}_{0}}(\alpha_{0})
(respectively
f_{\tilde{L}_{1}}(\alpha_{1})
). Exactly as in Lemma
2.7
, we have that:

Lemma 4.32
.

Suppose that
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})\in US_{I,\epsilon_{1}}
and
(\epsilon_{i}^{\operatorname{pert}})
is a small vector. By taking:

•

The
\rho_{i}
coordinate of
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
sufficiently close to
\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi}(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{i}^{\operatorname{pert}})^{2}
for every
i\in I
,

•

the Hamiltonian term
g_{L}
to be sufficiently small along
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
,

the action of the chord
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
can be taken arbitrarily close to

(4.66)

\displaystyle A_{g_{L}^{m}}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))\approx f_{\tilde{L}_{1}}(\alpha_{1})-f_{\tilde{L}_{0}}(\alpha_{0})+\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}(1-\frac{(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{i}^{\operatorname{pert}})^{2}}{2})(\frac{\alpha_{1,i}}{2\pi}-(v_{i}+v_{s,i})-\frac{\alpha_{0,i}}{2\pi})

where we have chosen identifications
\alpha_{0}=(\alpha_{0,i})
,
\alpha_{1}=(\alpha_{1,i})
as in (
4.64
). (Note that the right-hand side of (
4.66
) is independent of this identification.)

Proof.

Omitted. ∎

4.4.1.
Local product computation

Let
g_{0,L}
,
h_{F}
be Liouville admissible Hamiltonians (of some slopes
\lambda_{L}
and
\lambda_{F}>0
) with respect to two possibly different roundings
X^{L}_{m},X^{F}_{m}
of some
\widehat{X}_{(\epsilon^{\operatorname{pert}}_{i})}
(recall (
4.54
)). Then, as noted at the beginning of this section,

g_{L}:=g_{0,L}+h_{F}

is also homogeneous. We assume that
g_{0,L}
and
g_{L}
satisfy the conditions right after Proposition
4.30
so that all of the non-constant chords are of the form
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
.

Lemma 4.33
.

After further deforming
\tilde{L}_{0}
, choosing
(\epsilon_{i}^{\operatorname{pert}})
generically, and taking our Hamiltonians as in Lemma
4.32
, we can ensure that all of the non-constant chords of
g_{0,L}
(respectively
g_{L}
) have distinct actions and periods.

Proof.

For any
y\in\mathcal{X}_{I}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})
,
\alpha_{0}\in\tilde{L}_{0,\epsilon_{1},y}
, we can deform
\tilde{L}_{0}
using a Hamiltonian perturbation to slightly modify
\alpha_{0}
(over the same
y
) and then apply
[
64
, Lemma 5.11]
(as in Step 2 of the proof of
[
64
, Lemma 5.4]
) to make
\tilde{L}_{0}
fiber radial near this new point. Doing this as needed for each point, we can modify all of the positions of
\tilde{L}_{0}
over each
y
. The result then follows by examining the formula for the approximate action in (
4.66
). ∎

In what follows, we will always assume that actions/periods are separated as in Lemma
4.33
. Before proceeding further, we need to recall that solutions
u
of (
2.43
) have an associated geometric energy

(4.67)

E_{\operatorname{geo}}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}||du-X_{K}||^{2}

as well as a topological energy

(4.68)

E_{\operatorname{top}}(u)=\int_{\Sigma}u^{*}\omega-d(u^{*}K).

We have a relationship between these two energies:

(4.69)

E_{\operatorname{geo}}(u)=E_{\operatorname{top}}(u)-\int_{\Sigma}\mathfrak{R}_{K},

where
\mathfrak{R}_{K}
is the curvature of the perturbation 1-form
K
. In local coordinates
(s,t)
on
\Sigma
, we have

(4.70)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{K}=(\partial_{s}K(\partial_{t})-\partial_{t}K(\partial_{s})+\{K(\partial_{s}),K(\partial_{t})\})ds\wedge dt

For our specific application, we let
\mathring{Z}
be a strip
Z:=\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
with one interior puncture. Equip this with a positive cylindrical end at the interior puncture and two strip-like ends (one positive and one negative) at the boundary punctures. Consider two closed one-forms
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
which agree with
dt
along the negative strip-like end. We assume

•

\beta_{1}
agrees with
dt
along the cylindrical end and vanishes along the positive strip-like end.

•

\beta_{2}
vanishes along the cylindrical end and agrees with
dt
along the positive strip-like end.

Set

K_{\operatorname{inv}}:=h_{F}\cdot\beta_{1}+g_{0,L}\cdot\beta_{2}.

Lemma 4.34
.

The curvature of
K_{\operatorname{inv}}
,
\mathfrak{R}_{K_{\operatorname{inv}}},
vanishes.

Proof.

The third term in (
4.70
) vanishes because the functions
h_{F}
and
g_{0,L}
Poisson commute. The combination of the first two terms vanish because
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
are both closed on
\mathring{Z}.
∎

Next, let
H_{F}
be a small time-dependent perturbation of
h_{F}
of the form discussed in §
4.1
. For simplicity, in what follows we take our perturbed Hamiltonian
H_{F}
to have exactly one degree zero orbit
x_{\mathbf{v},c}
in each connected component
U_{\mathbf{v},c}
of each isolating set
U_{\mathbf{v}}
. Take a small perturbation one-form
K_{\operatorname{pert}}
supported on the cylindrical end so that for
s>>0
,
K_{\operatorname{inv}}+K_{\operatorname{pert}}=H_{F}dt.

Definition 4.35
.

Set
K=K_{\operatorname{inv}}+K_{\operatorname{pert}}
. Fix a surface dependent almost complex structure
J_{\mathring{Z}}
(as usual translation invariant along the ends), and
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H_{F})
,
x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{0,L})
,
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L})
. Let

\mathcal{M}_{\mathring{Z}}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})

denote the moduli space of solutions to (
2.43
) such that along the strip-like ends

(4.71)

\displaystyle\operatorname{lim}_{s\to\infty}u(\varepsilon_{2}(s,t))=x_{2}

(4.72)

\displaystyle\operatorname{lim}_{s\to-\infty}u(\varepsilon_{0}(s,t))=x_{0}

and along the cylindrical end

(4.73)

\displaystyle\operatorname{lim}_{s\to\infty}u(\varepsilon_{1}(s,t))=x_{1}

Lemma
4.34
implies that for maps
u\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathring{Z}}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}),

E_{top}(u)+\hbar\geq E_{geo}(u),

where
\hbar>0
is a constant that can be taken arbitrarily small. Concretely this means that for any such solution with inputs
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}(X;H_{F})
,
x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{0,L})
,
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L})
,

(4.74)

\displaystyle A_{g_{0,L}}(x_{2})+A_{H_{F}}(x_{1})\leq A_{g_{L}}(x_{0})+\hbar

By counting solutions to the moduli spaces from Definition
4.35
in the usual way (now using a generic choice of surface dependent almost complex structure
J_{\mathring{Z}}
), we obtain a map:

(4.75)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{1,1}:CF^{*}(X;H_{F})\otimes CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{0,L})\to CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L})

which is compatible with filtrations in view of (
4.74
). Recall that for any
\mathbf{v}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
denotes the manifold of orbits of
h_{F}
with winding number
\mathbf{v}
. Fix some
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
and consider the special situation where
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}\setminus\partial\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
intersects
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
at its starting point. Denote
A_{g_{L}}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))
by
a
. Fix some small
\hbar
, let
CF_{\geq a-\hbar}^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L})
denote the chords with action at least
a-\hbar
, let
CF_{\geq a+\hbar}^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L})
denote the chords with action at least
a+\hbar
and consider the quotient complex

CF_{(a-\hbar,a+\hbar)}^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}):=CF_{\geq a-\hbar}^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L})/CF_{\geq a+\hbar}^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}).

For any element
\mathfrak{c}\in CF_{\geq a-\hbar}^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L})
, we denote its image in
CF_{(a-\hbar,a+\hbar)}^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L})
by
[\mathfrak{c}]_{(a-\hbar,a+\hbar)}.
The key local calculation is the following:

Lemma 4.36
.

Fix some
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})\subset U_{\mathbf{v},c}
and assume that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}\setminus\partial\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
intersects
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
at its starting point. Let
\hbar>0
be a small number and suppose that the one-form
K_{\operatorname{pert}}
is sufficiently small. Then the lowest energy term,
[\mathfrak{m}_{1,1}]_{(a-\hbar,a+\hbar)}
takes
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{\mathbf{v},c}}|\otimes|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|
isomorphically onto
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|
:

(4.76)

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{m}_{1,1}]_{(a-\hbar,a+\hbar)}:|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{\mathbf{v},c}}|\otimes|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|\cong|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|\subset CF_{(a-\hbar,a+\hbar)}^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}).

The proof follows after the preparatory Lemma
4.37
. Recall that the Hamiltonian flow on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
generates an
S^{1}
-action which extends to the entire regularizing neighborhood
U_{|\mathbf{v}|}
(and in particular each component
U_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}
). The basic idea of our proof is to “unwind” (in the sense of (
4.14
)) the flows of
H_{F}
and
g_{L}
using the Hamiltonian action on
U_{|\mathbf{v}|}
so that the contribution (
4.76
) becomes multiplication by a certain element (
4.86
). To this end, set

(4.77)

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{F}=h_{F}-K_{S^{1}}

(4.78)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{F}=H_{F}-K_{S^{1}}

where
K_{S^{1}}
is the momentum map for the
S^{1}
-action in
U_{|\mathbf{v}|}
. The spinning construction from (
4.16
) identifies
x_{\mathbf{v},c}
(and associated orientation lines) with a constant orbit
\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}
of
\hat{H}_{F}
in
U_{\mathbf{v},c}
. Because
\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}
is a constant orbit, we can multiply
\hat{H}_{F}
by a small positive real number
\delta^{p}
while keeping
\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}
fixed. Set

X_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}=U_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}\cap X.

Then for
\delta^{p}
sufficiently small,
\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}
is the only degree zero orbit of
\delta^{p}\hat{H}_{F}
in
X_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}
. It will be useful for our purposes to modify this Hamiltonian outside of
U_{\mathbf{v}}
. First, we consider a variant of
H_{F}
,
H_{\mathbf{v}}
, defined by (compare (
4.17
))

(4.79)

\displaystyle H_{\mathbf{v}}:=\delta_{\mathbf{v}}\rho_{\mathbf{v}}h_{\mathbf{v}}+h_{F}

This Hamiltonian is only perturbed in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
and hence there is some
\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}
close to
\epsilon_{1}
such that for
\rho_{i}>\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi}(\epsilon_{1}^{\prime})^{2}
, the Hamiltonian
H_{\mathbf{v}}
is independent of
\rho_{i}
. Now let
\hat{H}_{\mathbf{v}}
be the spinning of
H_{\mathbf{v}}.
By construction, we have that for
\rho_{i}>\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi}(\epsilon_{1}^{\prime})^{2}
,

\delta^{p}\hat{H}_{\mathbf{v}}=\delta^{p}\pi v_{i}\rho_{i}+G(\rho_{j})

where
G(\rho_{j})
is independent of
\rho_{i}.
We choose some
\epsilon_{1}^{*}
which is slightly larger than
\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}
and consider functions
b_{i}(\rho_{i})
such that

(4.80)

b_{i}(\rho_{i})=\begin{cases}0&\rho_{i}\leq\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi}(\epsilon_{1}^{\prime})^{2}\\
c^{*}-\pi\rho_{i}&\rho_{i}\geq\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi}(\epsilon_{1}^{*})^{2}\end{cases}

for some small positive constant
c^{*}.
Finally, set

(4.81)

\displaystyle H_{\delta^{p}}=\sum_{i\in I}\delta^{p}v_{i}b_{i}(\rho_{i})+\delta^{p}\hat{H}_{\mathbf{v}}

This modification creates some additional (constant) orbits in
X_{|\mathbf{v}|}\setminus U_{\mathbf{v}}
, but we can ensure that for any such orbit
x
,

(4.82)

\displaystyle H_{\delta^{p}}(x)>H_{\delta^{p}}(\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c})

Now let
S
be the thimble domain from §
3.2
and let
\beta
be the subclosed one-form over
S
from that section. Choose a surface dependent almost complex structure
J_{S}
with each
J_{S,z}\in\mathcal{J}(M,\mathbf{D})
(as always, surface independent near
z=\infty
) and consider
\operatorname{PSS}
thimbles for the Hamiltonian
H_{\delta^{p}}
— that is to say maps
u:S\to X_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}
satisfying:

(4.83)

(du-X_{H_{\delta^{p}}}\otimes\beta)^{0,1}=0

(where
({0,1})
is taken with respect to
J_{S}
) such that

(4.84)

\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}u(\varepsilon(s,t))

\displaystyle=\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}

Denote this moduli space by
\mathcal{M}(H_{\delta^{p}},\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c})
.

Lemma 4.37
.

Assume that for every
z\in S
,
J_{S,z}
is sufficiently close to some standard
J_{0}
(recall Definition
3.13
) and that
J_{S}
is generic. Then for
\delta^{p}
sufficiently small, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(H_{\delta^{p}},\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c})
is compact.

Proof.

We need to demonstrate that, under these assumptions, thimbles do not escape
X_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}
. The key point is that for
\delta^{p}
sufficiently small, such
\operatorname{PSS}
solutions have very small energy. The fact that these solutions have low energy together with the fact that
J_{S}
is close to a standard almost- complex structure allows us to employ the monotonicity argument of
[
27
, Lemma 4.10]
to conclude that the PSS solutions cannot escape
U_{|\mathbf{v}|,\epsilon^{\prime}}
(recall (
4.53
)) for some small
\epsilon^{\prime}
. Note that this lemma is proven by projecting (pieces of)
\operatorname{PSS}
solutions to suitable holomorphic curves in divisor strata. In our case, our deformed Hamiltonian
H_{\delta^{p}}
is independent of
\rho_{i}
when
\rho_{i}>\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi}(\epsilon_{1}^{\ast})^{2}
, allowing these arguments to go through.

Breaking along
\mathbf{D}
can also be excluded for energy reasons (see the proof of
[
27
, Lemma 4.5]
for a similar argument). Namely, in view of
[
27
, Lemma 2.9]
, we have that for any
x\in\mathbf{D}
,

H_{\delta^{p}}(x)\geq\delta^{p}h_{F}(x)\approx\delta^{p}\lambda_{F}(\frac{1}{1-1/2\epsilon_{1}^{2}}-1)

In particular, for any
x\in\mathbf{D}
,
H_{\delta^{p}}(x)>H_{\delta^{p}}(\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}).
Suppose we had a PSS solution
u_{1}
asymptotic to such an
x
. Then the topological energy of such a solution is

(4.85)

\displaystyle E_{top}(u_{1})=\int_{u_{1}}u_{1}^{*}(\omega)+H_{\delta^{p}}(x)

If
\int_{u_{1}}u_{1}^{*}(\omega)<0
, this is negative when
\delta^{p}
small (hence impossible) and when
\int_{u_{1}}u_{1}^{*}(\omega)\geq 0
, this is larger than the topological energy of solutions in
\mathcal{M}(H_{\delta^{p}},\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}).
Therefore such solutions cannot break along
\mathbf{D}
and hence remain in
X_{|\mathbf{v}|}
. Similarly, there is no breaking along other orbits in
X_{|\mathbf{v}|}\setminus U_{\mathbf{v}}
in view of (
4.82
). Finally, breaking inside of
U_{\mathbf{v}}
does not occur for generic
J_{S}
and so Gromov compactness implies that
\mathcal{M}(H_{\delta^{p}},\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c})
is compact as claimed. ∎

It follows that, after taking
J_{S}
generic (but still close to a split almost-complex structure), the count of index zero thimbles gives rise to a well-defined map:

(4.86)

\displaystyle e:{\mathbf{k}}\to|\mathfrak{o}_{\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}}|

Remark 4.38
.

In the case of a single smooth divisor
\mathbf{D}
and
\mathbf{v}\neq 0
, the Hamiltonian
H_{\delta^{p}}
is essentially a
\bigvee
-shaped Hamiltonian of small slope and the construction of the element (
4.86
) matches the construction of the unit in Rabinowitz Floer (co)homology.

Proof.

(
Of Lemma
4.36
:) We now use the map (
4.86
) to analyze the module structure (
4.75
). First, we apply Gromov compactness to argue that provided
\hbar
,
K_{\operatorname{pert}}
are sufficiently small, solutions contributing to (
4.76
) do not escape the isolating neighborhood
U_{\mathbf{v},c}
. To see this, consider a sequence of
H_{F,n}
tending to
h_{F}
and a corresponding
K_{\operatorname{pert},n}\to 0
. Let
u_{n}
be Floer solutions with respect to the forms
K_{\operatorname{inv}}+K_{\operatorname{pert},n}
with inputs in
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{\mathbf{v},c}}|
and
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|
and which have energy less than the action gap of
g_{L}
. Then (after passing to a subsequence)
\{u_{n}\}

C^{\infty}_{loc}
-converges to solutions of zero topological energy, which are necessarily constant by Lemma
4.34
. Suppose that on each
u_{n}
there is some point
z_{n}
which intersects the boundary of
U_{\mathbf{v},c}
. The points
z_{n}
must escape to infinity along the cylindrical or strip-like ends. Thus, after passing to a subsequence, we can write
z_{n}=(s_{n},t_{n})
where
(s,t)
are coordinates along the end. Then by rescaling by
s_{n}
and passing to a subsequence, we can produce a Floer cylinder or strip which crosses the boundary of
U_{\mathbf{v},c}
. As this solution must also have zero energy, this is a contradiction. Next, let
\hat{H}_{F}
be as in (
4.77
) and set

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{L}=g_{L}-K_{S^{1}}.

Using another “spinning argument”(see e.g.
[
42
, Proposition 3.8]
), we obtain that (
4.76
) is equivalent to an operation

(4.87)

\displaystyle[\hat{m}_{1,1}]_{a^{\prime}-h,a^{\prime}+h}:|\mathfrak{o}_{\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}}|\otimes|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|\to CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};\hat{g}_{L})_{(a^{\prime}-h,a^{\prime}+h)}

Where
\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}
is the orbit in
U_{\mathbf{v},c}
corresponding to
x_{\mathbf{v},c}
(now a constant orbit) and
a^{\prime}=A_{\hat{g}_{L}}(\hat{x}_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))
.
Let
\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}
be the orbit of
\hat{H}_{F}
in
U_{\mathbf{v},c}
corresponding to
x_{\mathbf{v},c}
(now a constant orbit). As above, if
\delta^{p}
is sufficiently small this is the only index zero orbit of
\delta^{p}\hat{H}_{F}
in
X_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}.
We modify the Hamiltonian
\delta^{p}\hat{H}_{F}
to
H_{\delta^{p}}
and set
g_{\delta^{p},L}=g_{0,L}+H_{\delta^{p}}
. Then (
4.87
) becomes identified with a map

(4.88)

\displaystyle[\hat{m}_{1,1}]_{a^{\prime\prime}-h,a^{\prime\prime}+h}:|\mathfrak{o}_{\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}}|\otimes|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|\to CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{\delta^{p},L})_{(a^{\prime\prime}-h,a^{\prime\prime}+h)}

Assume our complex structures are sufficiently close to some standard
J_{0}
and let
e:{\mathbf{k}}\to|\mathfrak{o}_{\hat{x}_{\mathbf{v},c}}|
denote the map from (
4.86
). The composition
[\hat{m}_{1,1}]_{(a^{\prime\prime}-h,a^{\prime\prime}+h)}(e\otimes\operatorname{id})
produces a map

m_{e}:|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|\to CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{\delta^{p},L})_{(a^{\prime\prime}-h,a^{\prime\prime}+h)}.

The composition is defined by counting broken configurations of thimbles
S
and maps from
\mathring{Z}
which match up at the punctures and can thus be identified with a continuation map by performing the standard gluing construction along the cylindrical ends. Finally, we deform the resulting family of Hamiltonians over the strip to a constant family of Hamiltonians (and our complex structures as needed), concluding the proof. ∎

Remark 4.39
.

We sketch an alternative approach to Lemma
4.36
using Morse-Bott models for
HF^{*}(X;H_{F})
and the map
\mathfrak{m}_{1,1}
. Consider (an outward pointing) Morse function
h_{D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}}
on
D_{\mathbf{v}}^{c_{0}}
with a unique index zero critical point
\bar{x}_{crit}
at the point in the base where
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
projects.
\pi_{I}^{*}(h_{D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}})_{|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}}
then has a torus
T^{|\mathbf{v}|}_{\bar{x}_{crit}}
worth of critical point over
\bar{x}_{crit}
. We can perturb
h_{F}
using this function to a time-independent function
H_{MB}
which has a Morse-Bott set of orbits over
\bar{x}_{crit}
which we also denote by
T^{|\mathbf{v}|}_{\bar{x}_{crit}}.

Now take
g_{0,L}=g_{L}-H_{MB}.
Then there is a map
u:\mathring{Z}\to X
of zero geometric/topological energy with inputs the fundamental cycle on
[T^{|\mathbf{v}|}_{\bar{x}_{crit}}]
,
x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
, and output
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
that should contribute to a Morse-Bott model for
\mathfrak{m}_{1,1}.
By perturbing this solution to the non-degenerate setting, one could then potentially obtain an alternative justification for (
4.76
). However, making this approach precise would require developing more Morse-Bott theory than we wish to undertake here.

4.4.2.
Proof of Theorem

Definition 4.40
.

A homogeneous collection is a sequence of functions
g^{m}_{L}
(as before indexed by
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}
) so that there exists
K_{\vec{\epsilon}}
with
\operatorname{min}_{\mathbf{D}}R^{\vec{\epsilon}}>K_{\vec{\epsilon}}>1
such that:

(1)

On each subset
U_{I,\epsilon_{1}+\delta^{\operatorname{max}}}\setminus(\cup_{j\notin I}U_{j,\epsilon_{1}+\delta^{\operatorname{max}}}),

g^{m}_{L}=g^{m}_{L}(\rho_{i})
is a function of the variables
\rho_{i}
with
i\in I
.

(2)

When
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}\geq K^{\vec{\epsilon}}
,
g^{m^{\prime}}_{L}\geq g^{m}_{L}
whenever
m^{\prime}\geq m
and

(4.89)

\displaystyle\theta(X_{g^{m}_{L}})=g^{m}_{L}+\lambda_{m}^{L}

for some increasing sequence of
\lambda_{m}^{L}
tending to infinity.

(3)

For any
\lambda>0
, there exists a
g^{m}_{L}
such that
g^{m}_{L}>\lambda(R^{\vec{\epsilon}}-1)
when
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}\geq K^{\vec{\epsilon}}.

(4)

Each
g^{m}_{L}
is a non-positive function of small norm on
\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}
.

For any
m^{\prime}\geq m,
there are continuation maps:

\mathfrak{c}_{m,m^{\prime}}:HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};G^{m}_{L})\to HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};G^{m^{\prime}}_{L})

This is again a consequence of the integrated maximum principle. Let
\rho(s)
be a non-negative, monotone non-increasing cutoff function such that

(4.90)

\rho(s)=\begin{cases}0&s\gg 0\\
1&s\ll 0\end{cases}

Set

(4.91)

\displaystyle G_{s,t}=(1-\rho(s))G^{m}_{L}+\rho(s)G^{m^{\prime}}_{L}

For
R^{\vec{\epsilon}}
large we have that this is a monotone homotopy and we now have the following

(4.92)

\displaystyle\theta(X_{G_{s,t}})

\displaystyle=G_{s,t}+\lambda_{s},

(4.93)

\displaystyle dR^{\vec{\epsilon}}(X_{G_{s,t}})

\displaystyle=0

Choose
J_{s,t}
to be of contact type, the integrated maximum principle again applies to show that these continuation maps are well-defined. It is also not difficult to see that the wrapped Floer cohomology groups (
2.42
) can be computed as :

(4.94)

\displaystyle WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\cong\varinjlim_{m}HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};G_{L}^{m})

To show this, choose a sequence of
h^{\lambda}
which are admissible in the sense of Definition
2.4
and which are linear for
R\geq K^{\vec{\epsilon}}
. We can use the same argument as above to produce continuation maps between the directed systems
\{HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H_{L}^{\lambda})\}
(
H_{L}^{\lambda}
are small perturbations of the
h^{\lambda}
) and the directed system
\{HF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};G_{L}^{m})\}.
Standard gluing and homotopy arguments show that these maps induce isomorphisms on the direct limits.

Now assume that we have deformed
L_{0},L_{1}
to be fiber radial and that, for each
m,
our Hamiltonians
g^{m}_{L}
are taken to satisfy the conditions listed after Proposition
4.30
so that the non-constant chords are of the form
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
. Observe that the chords between
\tilde{L}_{0}
and
\tilde{L}_{1}
then exhibit a certain “periodic” structure — a single short chord
x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
gives rise to an entire collection of chords,
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
. The basic idea of our proof is that this periodic structure of these chords is reflected in the
SH^{0}(X)
module structure. If
x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
lies in
U_{|\mathbf{v}|,c}
, then to lowest order, the action of
\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}
in symplectic cohomology should take (a generator corresponding to)
x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
to (a generator corresponding to)
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
. As in the previous section, this can be formalized using filtrations and spectral sequences. For any
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}
choose an identification as in (
4.64
) so that
\alpha_{0}=(\alpha_{0,i})
,
\alpha_{1}=(\alpha_{1,i})
and define:

(4.95)

\displaystyle w_{L}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))=\frac{-f_{\tilde{L}_{1}}(\alpha_{1})+f_{\tilde{L}_{0}}(\alpha_{0})}{1-\epsilon_{1}^{2}/2}+\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}(\frac{\alpha_{0,i}}{2\pi}+(v_{i}+v_{s,i})-\frac{\alpha_{1,i}}{2\pi})

Given a non-positive integer
p
, we let
w_{L}^{p}
denote the
-p
-th largest value among the numbers
w_{L}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})),
listed in order. Sending
\epsilon_{i}^{\operatorname{pert}}\to 0
, the approximation from Lemma
4.32
becomes:

(4.96)

\displaystyle A_{g_{L}}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))\approx-(1-\epsilon_{1}^{2}/2)w_{L}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))

We can therefore choose our Hamiltonian
g_{L}^{m}
so that
w_{L}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))
defines a descending filtration,
F^{p}CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})
, (
p
ranging over non-positive integers) by setting

(4.97)

\displaystyle F^{p}CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m}):=\bigoplus_{x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}),w_{L}\leq w_{L}^{p}}|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|

Next, set

(4.98)

\displaystyle CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})^{p}:=\frac{F^{p}CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})}{F^{p+1}CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})}

and take
HF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})^{p}:=H^{*}(CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})^{p}).
As in (
4.5
), defined the (filtered) wrapped co-chains by the formula:

(4.99)

\displaystyle WC^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};\{g_{L}^{m}\}):=\bigoplus_{\ell}CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})[q]

with differential given by the same formula as (
4.6
). We have

H^{*}(WC^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};\{g_{L}^{m}\}))\cong WF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1}).

Suppose that whenever
m^{\prime}>m
,
g_{L}^{m^{\prime}}\geq g_{L}^{m}
(
\forall x\in X
not just at
\infty
), ensuring that (
4.91
) is a monotone homotopy. Then the induced continuation maps preserve the filtration by
w_{L}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))
and there is an extension of this filtration,
F^{p}WC^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};\{g_{L}^{m}\}),
to wrapped co-chains. We therefore have a spectral sequence:

(4.100)

\displaystyle E^{pq}_{r}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})=>WF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})

Finally, suppose that

(4.101)

\operatorname{lim}_{m\to\infty}g_{L}^{m}(x)=\begin{cases}0&x\in\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}\\
\infty&x\notin\widehat{X}_{\epsilon_{1}}\end{cases}

Then, if one has two sequences of functions
g_{L}^{m}
and
\tilde{g}_{L}^{m}
, (
4.101
) implies that for any
g_{L}^{m}
, one can always find a
\tilde{g}_{L}^{m^{\prime}}
which dominates it (and vice-versa). This allows one to construct a filtered comparison map on the wrapped co-chains:

WC^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};\{g_{L}^{m}\})\cong WC^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};\{\tilde{g}_{L}^{m}\})

which means that the spectral sequence is independent of this choice. By construction, the first page of the spectral sequence is given by

(4.102)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{q}E^{pq}_{1}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})\cong\varinjlim_{m}HF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})^{p}

The argument of Lemma
4.33
shows that by further perturbing
\tilde{L}_{0}
by a small isotopy and possibly shifting
\epsilon_{1}
, we can assume that
w_{L}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))
and
w_{L}(x_{y^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}(\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\alpha^{\prime}_{1}))
are distinct except when

y=y^{\prime},\alpha_{0}=\alpha^{\prime}_{0},\alpha_{1}=\alpha^{\prime}_{1},w(\mathbf{v})=w(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})

In particular, we can assume that the orientation lines
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|
define subspaces of the
E_{1}
page. We now turn to constructing a filtered action
SH^{0}(X,{\mathbf{k}})
on
WF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})
which will induce an action of
\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,\mathbf{D})
on the pages of (
4.100
). To do this, we will want to work want to define
SH^{*}(X)
using homogeneous collections— in particular our Hamiltonians orbits get closer to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
rather than getting progressively closer to
\mathbf{D}
as was the case in in §
4.1
. The obvious analogue of (
4.100
) is a spectral sequence

(4.103)

\displaystyle E_{r,\operatorname{hom}}^{pq}=>SH^{*}(X)

which is superficially different from the spectral sequence in Lemma
4.1
. However, there is a simple “rescaling trick” which enables us to easily relate this setup to the one from §
4.1
. In particular, we have:

Lemma 4.41
.

E^{p,-p}_{r,\operatorname{hom}}\cong\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,\mathbf{D})

Proof.

We let
\psi_{t}(x)
denote the time
\operatorname{ln}(t)
flow of the Liouville field for any pair
(x,t)
,
x\in X,t\in\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
, for which the flow is well-defined. Let
\bar{X}_{m}
and
h^{m}
denote the sequence of Liouville domains and Hamiltonians from §
4.1
. We also perturb
h^{m}
to a Hamiltonian
H^{m}
, which is perturbed exactly as before in each of the isolating sets
U_{\mathbf{v}}
but no longer perturbed near
\mathbf{D}
(as we do not need to consider curves which pass through
\mathbf{D}
). In a slight abuse of notation, we use the same notation for these Hamiltonians. Next set
t_{m}=\frac{1-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{1-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{m}^{2}}
and let
X^{\#}_{m}
be the rescaling of
\bar{X}_{m}
,

X^{\#}_{m}:=\psi_{t_{m}}(\bar{X}_{m})

so that its boundary is
C^{0}
close to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\epsilon_{1}}
(the rounding gets sharper as
m
gets larger). We let

(4.104)

\displaystyle h_{\#}^{m}:=e^{t_{m}}h^{m}\circ\psi_{-t_{m}}

(4.105)

\displaystyle H^{m}_{\#}=e^{t_{m}}H^{m}\circ\psi_{-t_{m}}

extended to all of
X
by linearity along the collar region. Pulling back Hamiltonian orbits and Floer solutions gives rise to a canonical bijection of Floer complexes:

(4.106)

\displaystyle CF^{*}(X\subset M;H^{m})\cong CF^{*}(X\subset M;H_{\#}^{m})

where the first complex defined with respect to some almost complex structure
J_{t}
and the second is defined with respect to any almost complex structure
\tilde{J}_{t}
on
M
which agrees with
\psi_{t_{m},*}(J_{t})
on
\psi_{t_{m}}(X\setminus V_{0,m}).
Note that the complex on the right-hand side does not depend on the choice of extension
\tilde{J}_{t}
by the integrated maximum principle. The isomorphism from (
4.106
) allows us to invoke the consequences of §
4.3
to show that the spectral sequence degenerates in degree zero. ∎

The basic theory of spectral sequences shows that
\mathfrak{m}_{1,1}
from (
4.75
) gives rise to a module structure:

\mathfrak{m}_{1,1}:E^{pq}_{r,\operatorname{hom}}\otimes\bigoplus_{p,q}E^{pq}_{r}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})\to\bigoplus_{p,q}E^{pq}_{r}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})

where
E^{pq}_{r,\operatorname{hom}}
denotes the pages of the symplectic cohomology spectral sequence. from (
4.103
). Restricting this to the action of the degree zero part,
E^{p,-p}_{r,\operatorname{hom}}
, and using the identification
E^{p,-p}_{r,\operatorname{hom}}\cong\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,\mathbf{D})
, yields an action:

(4.107)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{1,1}:\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,\mathbf{D})\otimes\bigoplus_{p,q}E^{pq}_{r}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})\to\bigoplus_{p,q}E^{pq}_{r}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})

We have finally collected all of the ingredients needed to prove the finiteness of wrapped Floer cohomology groups as modules over
SH^{0}(X)
:

Theorem 4.42
.

For any affine log Calabi-Yau variety
X
and any pair of Lagrangian branes
L_{0},L_{1}
, the wrapped Floer groups
WF^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
are finitely generated modules over
SH^{0}(X)
.

Proof.

Deform
L_{0},L_{1}
them to be fiber radial Lagrangians
\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1}
and consider the resulting spectral sequence

E_{r}^{pq}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1})=>WF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1}).

The key claim is that the
E_{1}
page is finitely generated as
\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,\mathbf{D})
modules — more precisely, we have that
\bigoplus_{p,q}E_{1}^{pq}
is generated as a
\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,\mathbf{D})
module by the orientation lines
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|
associated to short chords (including orientation lines associated to interior intersection points). To see this, note that by the description of the first page, every element which is homogeneous with respect to the
p
-grading lies in some
HF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})^{p}
. The chain complex
CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})^{p}
is freely generated as a
{\mathbf{k}}
-vector space by the elements
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|
where
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
is a chord with
w_{L}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))=w_{L}^{p}
. We can assume that
h^{m}_{F},

g_{0,L}^{m}
are both Liouville admissible with respect to possibly different roundings of some
\widehat{X}_{(\epsilon_{i}^{\operatorname{pert}})_{m}}
. Lemma
4.33
shows that by choosing
\epsilon_{i}^{\operatorname{pert}}
generically, we can assure that the chords with
w_{L}(x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}))=w_{L}^{p}
all arise in distinct action windows.

Now fix a
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
. We can deform
h^{m}_{F}
to some
\tilde{h}^{m}_{F}
(and
g_{L}^{m}
to some
\tilde{g}^{m}_{L}
) so that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
intersects
x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
at its starting point, while ensuring that the continuation maps

CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})^{p}\to CF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};\tilde{g}_{L}^{m})^{p}

preserve the distinct action windows. Note that
\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}\in E_{r,\operatorname{hom}}^{p,-p}
is represented by a generator of the orientation line
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{\mathbf{v},c}}|.
(As before
c
denotes the component
U_{\mathbf{v},c}
of
U_{\mathbf{v}}
where
x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
lies.) In view of Lemma
4.36
, it follows that on the level of the spectral sequence we have

\mathfrak{m}_{1,1}(\theta_{(\mathbf{v},c)}\otimes z_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})})=z_{x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}+\ldots\in HF^{*}(\tilde{L}_{0},\tilde{L}_{1};g_{L}^{m})^{p},

where
z_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}
is a generator of
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|
,
z_{x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}
is a generator of
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|
(determined by the choice of
z_{x_{y,\vec{0}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}
), and
\ldots
denotes terms with higher action
A_{g_{L}^{m}}
. Using induction on the action level, we see that the elements in
|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{y,\mathbf{v}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})}|
are themselves elements of the
E_{1}
page and in the module generated by the short chords. As these generate all classes additively, we obtain the desired result.

To finish the argument, note that since
\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,\mathbf{D})
is finitely generated (and in particular Noetherian), it follows that the remaining pages are also finitely generated as
\mathcal{SR}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,\mathbf{D})
modules, concluding the proof. ∎

(Proof of Theorem
1.1
):
Part
(a)
is Part
1
of Lemma
4.24
. Part
(b)
is Part
2
of the same Lemma. Finally, Part
(c)
is Theorem
4.42
.

5.
Applications

5.1.
Homological properties of
\mathcal{W}(X)

This section is purely expository and can be skipped by experts. Its purpose is to introduce some fundamental concepts from homological algebra and then state some recent results showing how these concepts apply in the context of Fukaya categories. As before
{\mathbf{k}}
will denote a coefficient field and all our categories will be small and linear over
{\mathbf{k}}
.

We will carry out our homological algebra in the framework of dg-categories and assume the reader is familiar with the definitions of dg categories and their functors as well as derived categories of modules and bimodules over dg categories from
[
49
,
18
]
. Of course, it is well-known that the Fukaya category is naturally an
A_{\infty}
category rather a dg-category. However, for any (small)
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{D}
, the Yoneda embedding provides a quasi-isomorphism onto a dg-subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{D})

[
78
, Chapter 1]
and so the two settings are equivalent for our purposes. Given an
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{D}
, we let
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{D})\subset\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{D})
denote the category of perfect modules, which is a pre-triangulated idempotent closed dg-category.

Recall the Hochschild (co)homology groups of dg-categories:

(5.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C}):=H^{*}(R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}))

(5.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C}):=H^{*}(\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{op}}^{L}\mathcal{C})

If
\mathcal{C}:=\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{D})
for some
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{D}
, we will use sometimes use the notations
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{D})
and
HH_{*}(\mathcal{D})
. It is immediate from the definition that
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
is a (unital) ring. Slightly less obvious is the fact that it is graded-commutative. For any object
L\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{D})
, there is a ring map

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{L}:\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{D})\to H^{*}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\bullet}(L,L)).

Thus, we have that the Hom groups of the cohomological category (taking all cohomology groups not just degree zero) all have the structure of a (graded) module over
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{D}).

For many purposes, it is convenient to use the more explicit chain level models for Hochschild invariants. These are the so-called “bar-complexes”
(\operatorname{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}),\partial)
(or
(\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}),\partial)
) that compute
\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})
(or
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
). Ignoring differentials, the complex
\operatorname{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is a sum of components of the form

(5.4)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{X_{0},\cdots X_{i}\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}\mathcal{C}(X_{i},X_{0})\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}\mathcal{C}(X_{i-1},X_{i})

where
i
is a non-negative integer. Again ignoring differentials, the Hochschild cochains
\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
are a product over components of the form:

(5.5)

\displaystyle\prod_{X_{0},\cdots X_{i}\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}\mathcal{C}(X_{i-1},X_{i}),\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{i}))

Using these models, one constructs a “cap product” action (see
[
1
]
for a convenient reference)

\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\operatorname{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})

given by taking a tensor of the form
\phi\otimes a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}
, where
\phi
has arity
k\leq i
, to the following element:

(5.6)

\displaystyle\phi\otimes a_{0}\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}\to(-1)^{\deg(\phi)(\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\deg(a_{j})+1))}a_{0}\phi(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{k})\otimes a_{k+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}

It is well-known that the cap product induces a module structure

(5.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})

The bar complexes are also very convenient for discussing functoriality of Hochschild invariants. In particular, it is easy to see that Hochschild homology is covariant. Given a dg-functor
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C},
there is an induced map

(5.8)

\displaystyle F_{*}:\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{A})\to\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})

induced by the naive inclusion
\operatorname{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\operatorname{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})
.
Similarly, one sees easily that Hochschild cohomology is contravariant for (fully-faithful) inclusions of dg-subcategories, namely given such an inclusion
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C},
there is an induced map

(5.9)

\displaystyle F^{*}:\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{A}).

The map
F^{*}
is given by taking a cochain
\phi\in\operatorname{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
, and applying it to tuples where the objects
X_{j}
all lie in the subcategory
\mathcal{A}.
This pull-back turns out to be a ring map
[
48
]
meaning (again in the setting of fully faithful inclusions) that it turns
\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{A})
into a module over
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C}).
We have the following straightforward observation:

Lemma 5.1
.

The pull-back makes
F_{*}
into an
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
module homomorphism.

Proof.

This is an explicit computation using (
5.7
) together with the explicit bar models for
F_{*}
and
F^{*}
.
∎

We next recall some basic concepts from non-commutative (algebraic) geometry. These are properties of dg-categories which are modelled on similar properties in classical algebraic geometry.

Definition 5.2
.

A dg category
\mathcal{C}
is smooth if
\mathcal{C}
is perfect as a
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
bimodule; i.e. it lies in the subcategory split generated by tensor products of left and right Yoneda (= representable) modules.

The smoothness of a finitely generated
{\mathbf{k}}
-algebra
R
is equivalent to the smoothness of its category of perfect complexes,
\operatorname{Perf}(R)
.

Given any perfect
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
bimodule,
\mathcal{B}
, there is a dual bimodule
\mathcal{B}^{!}
such that if
\mathcal{N}
is any other bimodule

(5.10)

\displaystyle R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{B}^{!},\mathcal{N})\cong\mathcal{B}\otimes^{L}_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{N}

The main case which will be relevant for us is when
\mathcal{C}
is a category with a single object (e.g. a dg-algebra) when

\mathcal{C}^{!}\cong R\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{op})

where the right hand side is viewed as a
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
-bimodule. The following structures were initially introduced by Ginzburg
[
32
]
in the setting of algebras and then further generalized to dg (and
A_{\infty}
) categories
[
50
,
30
]
.

Definition 5.3
.

We say that a smooth dg category is weakly Calabi-Yau of dimension
n
(hereafter referred to as “n-CY”) if it is equipped with the data of a bi-module quasi-isomorphism

(5.11)

\displaystyle\eta:\mathcal{C}^{!}\cong\mathcal{C}[-n]

Remark 5.4
.

The adjective “weakly” in Definition
5.3
is standard in the literature, where the term “Calabi-Yau category of dimension n” is reserved for an isomorphism as in (
5.11
) which lifts in a suitable sense to cyclic homology. This extra layer of complexity does not have any obvious implications for the present circle of ideas and so we stick to the simpler, weaker notion.

These formulations make it clear that
\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is a module over
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
. If
\mathcal{C}
is a smooth
n-CY
category, combining (
5.10
) and (
5.11
) gives rise to a (“Van den Bergh duality”) isomorphism:

(5.12)

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C})\cong\operatorname{HH}_{n-*}(\mathcal{C})

which is a map of
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
modules.

We now turn to discussing our main example, the derived wrapped Fukaya category. It is profitable to organize the collection of Lagrangian branes into an
A_{\infty}
category, the wrapped Fukaya category,
\mathcal{W}(X)
. The objects of this category are Lagrangian branes
L
and, as the notation indicates, its cohomological category is the Donaldson category
H^{*}(\mathcal{W}(X))
. The derived wrapped Fukaya category is then given by taking
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
. The details of the construction of
\mathcal{W}(X)
will not be important for us, as we will only make use of some of its formal properties. The first concerns the relationship between
SH^{*}(X)
and Hochschild invariants of
\mathcal{W}(X)
. Namely, recall that there is a natural comparison map

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}:SH^{*}(X)\to\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{W}(X))

which is a lift of (
2.45
) in the sense that

(5.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{(0)}=\mathcal{B}_{L}\circ\mathcal{CO}

where
\mathcal{B}_{L}
is the morphism from (
5.3
). It is a fundamental result of
[
11
,
25
]
that
\mathcal{W}(X)
is generated by Lagrangian co-cores of any Weinstein handlebody presentation. Combining this with the results of
[
30
,
31
]
(see also
[
11
, §11]
) yields:

Theorem 5.5
.

\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
is a smooth n-CY category with a compact generator. Moreover, the map (
5.13
) is an isomorphism.

Remark 5.6
.

On a technical note, we should point out that what Ganatra proves is that
\mathcal{W}(X)
is smooth n-CY as an
A_{\infty}
category. So in our statement of the result, we are using the well-known fact that
A_{\infty}
smooth n-CY structures are preserved by Morita equivalences—for a detailed proof in the setting of dg-categories see
[
50
, Proposition 3.10(e)]
(the
A_{\infty}
case does not seem to appear explicitly in the literature but is very similar).

For later use it will be useful to reformulate Theorem
4.42
in more categorical terms. The following definition is somewhat non-standard (though related ideas appeared in
[
40
]
):

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a dg-category such that
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
is a finitely generated
{\mathbf{k}}
-algebra. We say that a category is semi-affine over
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
if for any two objects
E_{1},E_{2}
,
H^{*}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(E_{1},E_{2}))
is a finitely generated module over
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C}).

Theorem
4.42
can therefore be rephrased as:

Corollary 5.8
.

For any affine log Calabi-Yau variety
X
, the dg-category
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
is semi-affine.

Remark 5.9
.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is actually a dg-category linear over
R=\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
(which we take to be finitely generated over
{\mathbf{k}}
). It is useful to compare this with the more common notion of properness for dg-categories. Recall that a category is called proper if for any two objects
E_{1},E_{2}
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(E_{1},E_{2})
is perfect as an
R
-module. Thus, the two definitions of properness coincide if
R
is a smooth
{\mathbf{k}}
-algebra. On the other hand, our definition of properness is very natural from the point of view of algebraic geometry: given a smooth quasi-projective variety
Y
over
{\mathbf{k}}
,
Y
is semi-affiine iff
\operatorname{Perf}(Y)
is semi-affine (Lemma
A.1
).

5.2.
Automatic generation

In this section, we give the proof of Proposition
5.18
. Before explaining our argument, we must recall the definitions of admissible subcategories and semi-orthogonal decompositions which play a central role in our proof. In the discussion which follows, we continue with the convention that all categories are linear over a field
{\mathbf{k}}.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
C
be a triangulated category and let
i:A\to C
be a full triangulated subcategory. We say that
A
is right (left)-admissible if the inclusion
i
has a right (left) adjoint.

For any full triangulated subcategory
A
, we can define the left and right orthogonal subcategories of
A
, which are denoted by
A^{\perp}
and
{}^{\perp}A
and are also triangulated categories. The property of a category being right-admissible is then equivalent to requiring that for each object
E
of
C
, there is an exact triangle

E_{A}\to E\to E_{A^{\perp}}

where
E_{A}
is in
A
and
E_{A^{\perp}}
is in
A^{\perp}
(the obvious analogue holds for left admissible categories as well). From this alternative characterization, it follows that if the category
A
is right-admissible, than
A^{\perp}
is left admissible. If there is a semi-orthogonal decomposition, we denote this by
C=\langle A^{\perp},A\rangle.
Finally, we have that for any right (respectively left) admissible subcategory
A
, the Verdier quotient
C/A
is equivalent to
A^{\perp}
(respectively
{}^{\perp}A
).

Turning to the dg-versions, we say that a full pretriangulated dg-subcategory
\mathcal{A}
of a pre-triangulated dg category
\mathcal{C}
is right (left) admissible if

H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is a right-admissible subcategory of
H^{0}(\mathcal{C}).
Given such a subcategory, we can also define
\mathcal{A}^{\perp}
to be the full subcategory of objects whose image in the homotopy category lies in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})^{\perp}\subset H^{0}(\mathcal{C}).
We have a quasi-equivalence

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\perp}\cong\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}

where
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}
denotes the Drinfeld quotient dg-category (there is an analogous equivalence
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}^{\perp}
). Hochschild homology is an additive invariant
[
84
, Theorem 11.7]
, in the sense that

(5.16)

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{A})\oplus\operatorname{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{A}^{\perp})

as
\operatorname{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{C})
modules. The final ingredient we will need is the following result:

Lemma 5.11
.

[
50
, Proposition 3.10(d)]
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (pretriangulated) smooth n-CY dg-category and suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a Drinfeld quotient of
\mathcal{C}
. Then
\mathcal{A}
is a (pretriangulated) smooth n-CY dg-category.

Remark 5.12
.

Lemma
5.11
can be viewed as a non-commutative analogue of the fact that an open subscheme of smooth Calabi-Yau variety is smooth and Calabi-Yau.

Having reviewed these definitions and results, we are now in a position to prove the following lemma which rules out semi-orthogonal decompositions in smooth
n
-CY dg-categories:

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pretriangulated smooth n-CY dg-category with a compact generator Let
i:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
be a (non-trivial) right admissible subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
. Suppose further that either:

•

(i)
\operatorname{Spec}(\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C}))
is connected.

•

(ii) The map
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is an isomorphism.

Then
i:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
is a quasi-equivalence.

Proof.

Case (i)
: We have a semi-orthogonal decomposition of
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{C}=\langle\mathcal{A}^{\perp},\mathcal{A}\rangle.
As
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\perp}
are both Drinfeld quotients of
\mathcal{C}
, they are both smooth n-CY dg categories by Lemma
5.11
. By (
5.16
), we have that

\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{C})\cong\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{A})\oplus\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{A}^{\perp})

as
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
modules. Because
\mathcal{C}
is
n
-CY,
\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{C})
is a rank one free module over
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
. The assumption that
\operatorname{Spec}(\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C}))
is connected implies that there is no non-trivial decomposition of a rank one free module into module summands and so
\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{A}^{\perp})=0.
Because
\mathcal{A}^{\perp}
is
n
-CY, this implies
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{\perp})=0
and that the category is trivial.

Case (ii)
: We proceed as in
Case (i)
and note that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the map
\pi_{1}
denotes projection onto the first factor of (
5.16
). It follows that
\pi_{1}
is an isomorphism and we have
\operatorname{HH}_{n}(\mathcal{A}^{\perp})=0.
Again, because
\mathcal{A}^{\perp}
is
n-CY
, this implies that the category is trivial.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

The above argument is similar to
[
28
, Theorem 36]
which proves a similar result in the case of proper Calabi-Yau categories.

In complete generality, it can be hard to apply Lemma
5.13
because the definition of admissibility is a little abstract. The key observation in our proof is that, in the setting of semi-affine categories, there is a readily checkable criterion for a subcategory
\mathcal{A}
to be a right admissible subcategory. This relevant definition is the following:

Definition 5.15
.

Let
S^{\bullet}
be a dg-algebra over
{\mathbf{k}}
and let
R\subseteq\operatorname{HH}^{0}(S^{\bullet})
be a finitely generated
{\mathbf{k}}
-algebra. We say that
S
is
friendly
(relative to
R
) if

•

a dg-module
N^{\bullet}
is perfect over
S^{\bullet}
iff
H^{*}(N^{\bullet})
is finitely generated over
R
.

There are two main sources of friendly algebras:

•

Classical algebras
S
which

–

have finite homological dimension

–

are module finite over a finitely generated center
R
(
[
72
, Proposition 7.25]
).

•

Derived categories of coherent sheaves provide a natural source of friendly algebras. More precisely, if
R
is a finitely generated
{\mathbf{k}}
-algebra, let
Y\to\operatorname{Spec}(R)
be a projective
R
-scheme which is smooth over
{\mathbf{k}}
, and let
E
be a compact generator of
\operatorname{Perf}(Y)
. Then
S^{\bullet}:=\operatorname{Hom}^{\bullet}(E,E)
is friendly relative to
R
(see Proposition
A.2
).

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pretriangulated, idempotent closed, semi-affine dg-category. Let
E
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
whose endomorphism dg-algebra
S^{\bullet}=\operatorname{Hom}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}(E,E)
which is friendly over
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
. Then
\langle E\rangle
is a right admissible subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

This is basically just pushing the standard argument for admissibility to its maximal extent and so we only give a sketch. The object
E
allows us to define a functor

(5.17)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(E,-):\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Mod}(S^{\bullet})

which lands in the subcategory of modules which are cohomologically finite over
R
by properness of
\mathcal{C}
. Then by our assumption, image of this functor in fact lies in
\operatorname{Perf}(S^{\bullet})
. So our functor induces a functor

(5.18)

\displaystyle i^{*}:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Perf}(S^{\bullet}).

which is right-adjoint to the inclusion

(5.19)

\displaystyle i:\operatorname{Perf}(S^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{C}.

Thus
<E>
is a right-admissible subcategory. ∎

Combining Lemmas
5.16
,
5.13
together with Proposition
A.2
yields the following result:

Corollary 5.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a smooth dg-category (stable, with compact generator) over
{\mathbf{k}}
which is both n-CY and semi-affine. Suppose further that

•

E
an object such that

\operatorname{Perf}(\operatorname{Hom}^{\bullet}(E,E))\cong\operatorname{Perf}(Y)

where
Y
is a projective
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
-scheme which is smooth over
{\mathbf{k}}
.

•

Either

(1)

\operatorname{Spec}(\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C}))
is connected or

(2)

the induced map
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\operatorname{Perf}(Y))=H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})
is an isomorphism.

Then
<E>=H^{0}(\mathcal{C}).

Proof.

By Lemma
5.16
and Proposition
A.2
,
<E>
is an admissible subcategory of
H^{0}(\mathcal{C}).
The fact that
\mathcal{C}
is n-CY together with either of the hypotheses in the second bullet allow us to invoke Lemma
5.13
to conclude that
<E>
is the full category. ∎

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
(M,\mathbf{D})
be a Calabi-Yau pair and let
E
be an object of
\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
such that

\operatorname{Perf}(\operatorname{Hom}^{\bullet}(E,E))\cong\operatorname{Perf}(Y)

where
Y
is a smooth quasi-projective scheme over
{\mathbf{k}}
. Then
<E>=H^{0}(\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))).

Proof.

Note that
Y
inherits the structure of a
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X)))\cong SH^{0}(X)
scheme along the morphism
\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X)))\to\operatorname{HH}^{0}(\operatorname{Perf}(Y))=H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}).
The argument of Lemma
A.1
allows one to deduce that
Y
is projective over
SH^{0}(X)
. Moreover,
SH^{0}(X)
is connected because it degenerates to a Stanley Reisner ring, which is connected. We can therefore apply Corollary
5.17
with
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Perf}(\mathcal{W}(X))
. ∎

Proposition
5.18
yields the following Corollary:

Corollary 5.19
.

Suppose
SH^{0}(X)
is smooth and
X
admits a homological section, then

\operatorname{Perf}(SH^{0}(X))\cong\mathcal{W}(X).

5.2.1.
Local models of Lagrangian fibrations

We next give an extended example to illustrate Corollary
5.19
. Consider specifically the affine variety defined by the equation

(5.20)

\displaystyle X=\{(z,u,v)\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n-1}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}|\;uv=c+z_{1}+\cdots+z_{n-1}\}

for some
c>0.

We will use Corollary
5.19
to prove homological mirror symmetry for this affine log Calabi-Yau. To prepare for this, we give a general geometric criterion (Proposition
5.23
), for a contractible conical Lagrangian
L_{0}
to define a homological section. For notational simplicity(i.e. to avoid cluttering our notation with connected components of strata), we state our criterion only for those Calabi-Yau pairs
(M,\mathbf{D})
where all of the strata
D_{I}
are connected. The basic idea of this criterion is that:

•

All Hamiltonian chords start and end at the same point. (We will say that “all of the Hamiltonian chords are Hamiltonian orbits.”)

•

the Lagrangian
L_{0}
should meet each of the (psuedo-) Morse Bott manifolds of Hamiltonian orbits cleanly in contractible submanifolds.

To spell this out more precisely, fix an
m
(meaning a
\Sigma_{m}
,
h^{m}
etc.) and let
h^{m}_{\#}
denote the rescaled Hamiltonians from (
4.104
). We will keep the same notation for manifolds of orbits
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
, with winding vector
\mathbf{v}
, as well as the analogous submanifolds
D_{\mathbf{v}}^{c_{0}}
,
S_{\mathbf{v}}^{c_{0}}
and isolating sets
U_{\mathbf{v}}
from the “Isolating neighborhoods” mini-section in §
4.2
. (We will freely use the constructions and notations from this and the “Hamiltonian perturbations” mini-section in §
4.2
, so the reader may wish to quickly review these.)

Suppose that all of the Hamiltonian chords
\mathcal{X}(L_{0};h^{m}_{\#})
are Hamiltonian orbits. Then the set of chords of
L_{0}
inside
U_{\mathbf{v}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}^{L_{0}}\subset\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{v}}
, is identified with a subset of the Hamiltonian orbits inside
U_{\mathbf{v}}
and all of the chords lie inside one of these
U_{\mathbf{v}}.
In what follows, we let
L_{0,\mathbf{v}}:=L_{0}\cap U_{\mathbf{v}}.

Perturb
h^{m}_{\#}
to functions
H^{m}_{F}
,
H^{m}_{L}
so that all Hamiltonian orbits and chords of
L_{0}
in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
are non-degenerate. Consider the Floer complex

(5.21)

\displaystyle CF_{\operatorname{loc}}^{*}(U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset M;h^{m}_{\#}):=\bigoplus_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(U_{\mathbf{v}};H^{m}_{F})}|\mathfrak{o}_{x_{0}}|

Assume that
||H^{m}_{F}-h^{m}_{\#}||_{C^{2}}
is sufficiently small. Then letting
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
be two Hamiltonian orbits in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
, a Gromov compactness argument shows that any Floer trajectory connecting
x_{0},y_{0}
(in
X
) lies entirely in
U_{\mathbf{v}}.
Assuming the perturbation is small enough, it follows from the usual arguments that the usual Floer differential defines a differential on (
5.21
). We define
HF_{\operatorname{loc}}^{*}(U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset M;h^{m}_{\#})
to be the cohomology of the resulting complex (this is called the “local Floer cohomology”.)

One can analogously construct the local Lagrangian Floer cohomology groups

HF^{*}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset M,L_{0,\mathbf{v}};h^{m}_{\#})

by considering the complex generated by chords of
L_{0}
in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
,

CF_{\operatorname{loc}}^{*}(U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset M,L_{0,\mathbf{v}};h^{m}_{\#}):=\bigoplus_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}(U_{\mathbf{v}},L_{0};H^{m}_{L})}|\mathfrak{o}_{L_{0},L_{0},x_{0}}|

and (again under the assumption that
||H_{L}^{m}-h^{m}_{\#}||_{C^{2}}
is sufficiently small) equipping it with the usual Lagrangian Floer theory differential. After possibly making the perturbation even smaller, we have a map

(5.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{\mathbf{v}}:HF^{*}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset M;h^{m}_{\#})\to HF^{*}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset M,L_{0,\mathbf{v}};h^{m}_{\#})

Before turning to our criteria, we make a preliminary definition.

Definition 5.20
.

Let
L
be a manifold equipped with a Riemannian metric. We say that a function
h:T^{*}L\to\mathbb{R}
, is split if it is of the form

h:=\pi^{*}(h_{L})+|v|^{2}

Definition 5.21
.

Fix an
m
and consider the following assumptions on a contractible Lagrangian
L_{0}
:

Assumptions:

(a)

All of the Hamiltonian chords
\mathcal{X}(L_{0};h^{m}_{\#})
are Hamiltonian orbits.

(b)

For any
\mathbf{v}
,
L_{0,\mathbf{v}}
is contractible and
\pi_{I}(L_{0,\mathbf{v}})=\bar{L}_{0,\mathbf{v}}
is a contractible Lagrangian submanifold with corners which is embedded transversely in
D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}.
Moreover, for any point
x\in\bar{L}_{0,\mathbf{v}}
,

L_{0,\mathbf{v}}\cap\pi_{I}^{-1}(x)

is a Lagrangian section of the fibration

(\rho_{1},\cdots,\rho_{n}):\pi_{I}^{-1}(x)\cap U_{\mathbf{v}}\to\prod_{i\in I}[\rho_{i,\mathbf{v}}-c_{0},\rho_{i,\mathbf{v}}+c_{0}]\subset\mathbb{R}^{I}

(c)

There is an outward pointing Morse function
h_{D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}}:D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}\to\mathbb{R}
which:

•

restricts to a function which is split on some Weinstein tubular neighborhood
U_{\bar{L}_{0,\mathbf{v}}}
, of
\bar{L}_{0,\mathbf{v}}.

•

has a unique critical point
c
with
\deg(c)=0
. (It follows that
c\in\bar{L}_{0}.
)

In each neighborhood
U_{\mathbf{v}}
, the conditions
(a)
-
(c)
provide strong control over the local Lagrangian Floer cohomology as well as the map
\mathcal{CO}_{\mathbf{v}}
.

Lemma 5.22
.

Suppose that
L_{0}
is contractible and satisfies conditions
(a)
-
(c)
. Then for every
\mathbf{v}
,
HF_{\operatorname{loc}}^{*}(U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset M,L_{0};h^{m}_{\#})=0
if
\ast\neq 0
and the local closed-open map

(5.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathbf{k}\cong HF^{0}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset M;h^{m}_{\#})\to HF^{0}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U_{\mathbf{v}}\subset M,L_{0};h^{m}_{\#})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Condition
(a)
ensures that there are no Hamiltonian chords outside of
\cup_{\mathbf{v}}U_{\mathbf{v}}
and we can thererfore prove the lemma for each
\mathbf{v}
separately. So, for the rest of the argument, we fix some
\mathbf{v}
. We will construct a perturbation
H^{m}_{\#}
of
h^{m}_{\#}
which behave nicely inside of
U_{\mathbf{v}}
. Pull back
h_{D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}}:D^{c_{0}}_{I}
to a Morse-Bott function
\pi_{I}^{*}(h_{D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}})
on the torus bundle over
D^{c_{0}}_{I}
where
\rho_{i}=\rho_{i,\mathbf{v}}.
The critical locus of
\pi_{I}^{*}(h_{D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}})
is a Morse-Bott function with tori as critical loci.

Over the critical point
c
, condition
(c)
ensures that there is a unique point
\hat{c}
where
L_{0}\cap\pi_{I}^{-1}(c)
intersects the torus bundle. We can perturb
\pi_{I}^{*}(h_{D^{c_{0}}_{\mathbf{v}}})
to a Morse function
\widehat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}^{base}
in such a way that it has a unique degree zero critical point at
\hat{c}.
We then spin
\widehat{h}_{\mathbf{v}}^{base}
to a time-dependent function
h_{\mathbf{v}}
and plug this into (
4.15
). To extend this to all of
M
, choose the other
h_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}
for
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{v}
and
h_{\mathbf{D}}
arbitrarily and construct the perturbation
H^{m}_{\#}
via (
4.17
). By construction, our Hamiltonians have a unique degree zero orbit
x_{c}
(corresponding to the critical point
\hat{c}
) in
U_{\mathbf{v}}
which is simultaneously a chord of
L_{0}
(we denote this by
x_{c}^{L})
. The chord
x_{c}^{L}
has degree zero again using Condition
(b)
.

Take
H^{m}_{F}=H^{m}_{L}=H^{m}_{\#}
. Next, we consider a specific perturbation one-form over the “chimney domain” (the disc with one boundary puncture and one interior puncture), which will ensure that the topological and geometric energy of solutions which define (
2.44
